\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}\partial_{x}(\Delta_{x}u_{x})\overline{\eta}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\Delta_{x}u_{x}\overline{\eta_{x}}.

(5.22)

The mixed term gives
-2\int B(u_{t})_{\phi}\overline{\eta}
. A final periodic integration by parts in
\phi
gives the alternate expression with
\eta_{\phi}
. This proves the coordinate and weak sign conventions used later.
∎

5.2.2. A quadratic form and its sign

Equation (
5.5
) is the Euler-Lagrange equation of the quadratic functional

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}[u]

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\left(g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}u\,\partial_{\nu}u-\frac{k}{3}u^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\mu_{g}

(5.23)

on any domain
\Omega
where boundary terms can be controlled. The sign of the potential part is again determined by
k
. When the equation is viewed on a time slab and one singles out the
t
-direction, this quadratic functional becomes the energy analyzed later. At this stage it is already clear that positive
k
creates the possibility of negative directions in the spatial quadratic form, while negative
k
strengthens coercivity.

The functional (
5.23
) also reveals why the conformal sector is a natural reduced problem for a paper about “linear stability” in the absence of a tensorial field equation. A scalar wave equation with a geometric mass term is a standard, closed, variational PDE. It admits a spectral theory, an energy theory, and a mode analysis. The scalar-curvature linearization does not supply such a closed theory for general
h
, but it supplies one perfectly for
u
.

5.3.  Divergence-free stress tensor and multiplier currents

For solutions of (
5.5
) one may introduce the usual Klein-Gordon stress tensor

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}[u]

\displaystyle:=\partial_{\mu}u\,\partial_{\nu}u-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\left(g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u\,\partial_{\beta}u-\frac{k}{3}u^{2}\right).

(5.24)

A direct computation using the equation of motion gives

\nabla^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}[u]=0.

(5.25)

Consequently any vector field
X
determines a current

J^{X}_{\mu}[u]:=T_{\mu\nu}[u]X^{\nu}

(5.26)

with divergence

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mu}J^{X}_{\mu}[u]

\displaystyle=T_{\mu\nu}[u]\,\pi_{X}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\pi_{X}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla^{\mu}X^{\nu}+\nabla^{\nu}X^{\mu}).

(5.27)

When
X
is Killing, the deformation tensor
\pi_{X}
vanishes and the current is conserved. This general identity underlies the time-translation energy used in the axisymmetric sector and the horizon-adapted currents discussed later for separated modes.

It is worth emphasizing that (
5.25
) is not an extra assumption imported from a matter model. It is simply the conserved stress tensor of the reduced scalar equation. In this sense the conformal problem is self-contained: once one passes from
h
to
u
, all of the familiar analytical machinery of wave equations becomes available.

The
k=0
specialization

From this point onward we impose
k=0
. The conformal master equation therefore becomes

\Box_{g}u=0.

(5.28)

In the axisymmetric sector this has two immediate consequences. First, the conserved energy controls only first derivatives and not the
L^{2}
norm of
u
itself. Second, the constant function belongs to the kernel of the spatial operator, so zero frequency must be analyzed explicitly rather than treated as a harmless endpoint.

The first consequence explains why one cannot simply imitate the coercive arguments appropriate to positive-mass Klein-Gordon equations. The second consequence explains why the bounded-slab theorem must be formulated modulo a finite-dimensional generalized zero-mode space. In other words, the
k=0
theory is neither coercive nor unstable: it is a genuinely borderline wave problem whose main structure is concentrated at zero frequency.

The rest of the paper now proceeds in the order dictated by this threshold phenomenon. Section
6
first proves an abstract stationary bounded-slab theorem modulo the full zero-frequency threshold space, with no axial symmetry assumed. The Carter sections that follow then verify those abstract conditions on strictly stationary slabs and, in the axisymmetric sector, sharpen the conclusion by explicit self-adjoint spectral analysis. Only after that bounded-slab theory is complete do we turn to ergoregions and horizons, where positivity fails and the appropriate continuation is microlocal/scattering rather than a direct sign argument.

6.  Abstract stationary bounded-slab theorem modulo the full threshold space

The basic bounded-slab mechanism is stationary and spectral rather than axisymmetric. Exact Carter separation will be important later for the concrete Carter family and for the axisymmetric spectral refinement, but the threshold theorem itself can be proved once and for all at the level of the stationary evolution group.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a real or complex Hilbert space of Cauchy data, and let

U_{t}=\mathcal{G}U

(6.1)

denote the first-order stationary evolution associated with a bounded-slab hyperbolic problem. We write
U(t)=e^{t\mathcal{G}}U_{0}
for the solution with initial data
U_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
.

Definition 6.1

(Full zero-frequency threshold space)
.

The
full zero-frequency threshold space
of the stationary evolution is

\mathcal{T}_{0}:=\bigcup_{N\geq 1}\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{G}^{N}.

(6.2)

Equivalently,
\mathcal{T}_{0}
is the generalized eigenspace of the generator at spectral value
0
. When
0
is isolated in the spectrum of
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathcal{T}_{0}
is the range of the corresponding Riesz projection.

The hypotheses used in the abstract theorem are stated before the main theorems in Assumption
B.1
. The proof below uses only those listed items.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Let

U_{\mathrm{thr}}(t):=\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}U(t),\qquad U_{\mathrm{bd}}(t):=(I-\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}})U(t).

(6.3)

Because
\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
commutes with the group, both components solve the evolution and

U(t)=U_{\mathrm{thr}}(t)+U_{\mathrm{bd}}(t)

(6.4)

is the unique decomposition of the solution into its threshold and threshold-complement parts.

Since
\mathcal{T}_{0}
is finite-dimensional and every vector in
\mathcal{T}_{0}
is annihilated by a sufficiently high power of
\mathcal{G}
, the restriction
\mathcal{G}|_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}
is nilpotent. Hence for some
N
,

e^{t\mathcal{G}}|_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{t^{j}}{j!}\,\mathcal{G}^{j}|_{\mathcal{T}_{0}},

(6.5)

so
U_{\mathrm{thr}}(t)
is polynomial in
t
.

For the bounded component,
U_{\mathrm{bd}}(t)\in\operatorname{Ker}\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
for all
t
. Conservation of
\mathscr{E}
and Assumption
B.1
(iii) therefore give

\displaystyle c\|U_{\mathrm{bd}}(t)\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathscr{E}[U_{\mathrm{bd}}(t)]=\mathscr{E}[U_{\mathrm{bd}}(0)]\leq C\|U_{\mathrm{bd}}(0)\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2},

(6.6)

which is the desired uniform bound.

Finally, suppose
U(t)=e^{\lambda t}V
with
\operatorname{Re}\lambda>0
and
V\in\operatorname{Ker}\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
. Then

c\,e^{2\operatorname{Re}\lambda t}\|V\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\leq\mathscr{E}[e^{\lambda t}V]=\mathscr{E}[V]

(6.7)

for all
t
, which forces
V=0
. This proves the theorem.
∎

Remark 6.2

(Interpretation)
.

Assumption
B.1
(ii) is the abstract way of saying that the full zero-frequency threshold space has been isolated before one states boundedness. On bounded stationary problems the commuting projection is often the Riesz projection at
\omega=0
. Theorem
1.2
then says that once the conserved energy is coercive on the threshold complement, the entire bounded-slab theorem follows immediately, with no use of axial symmetry.

7.  Exact separation of variables

7.1.  Mode ansatz and separation mechanism

The Carter structure of the metric suggests the standard Fourier ansatz in the Killing variables
t
and
\phi
:

\displaystyle u(t,\phi,r,x)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\Omega t+\mathrm{i}m\phi}R(r)S(x)

\displaystyle\Omega\in\mathbb{C},\quad m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(7.1)

Substituting (
7.1
) into (
5.15
) and dividing by
RS
yields an expression that splits into a sum of a purely radial term and a purely angular term. This is the exact analogue of Carter separation for the scalar wave equation on Kerr-type backgrounds.

The relevant algebra is straightforward but illuminating, so we write it out. The time and azimuthal derivatives contribute

\displaystyle\rho^{2}g^{tt}\,\partial_{t}^{2}u

\displaystyle=-\Omega^{2}\rho^{2}g^{tt}u,

\displaystyle 2\rho^{2}g^{t\phi}\,\partial_{t}\partial_{\phi}u

\displaystyle=2\Omega m\,\rho^{2}g^{t\phi}u,

\displaystyle\rho^{2}g^{\phi\phi}\,\partial_{\phi}^{2}u

\displaystyle=-m^{2}\rho^{2}g^{\phi\phi}u.

(7.2)

Using the explicit inverse coefficients, these combine to

\left(\frac{((r^{2}+a^{2})\Omega-am)^{2}}{\Delta_{r}}-\frac{(a(1-x^{2})\Omega-m)^{2}}{\Delta_{x}}\right)u.

(7.3)

The potential term contributes

\frac{k}{3}\rho^{2}u=\left(\frac{k}{3}r^{2}+\frac{k}{3}a^{2}x^{2}\right)u.

(7.4)

Hence the radial and angular variables separate exactly. No spectral assumption is used in this computation:
\Omega
may be complex,
m
is fixed by periodicity in
\phi
, and the separation constant is introduced only after the equality of a function of
r
and a function of
x
is obtained. This point is useful later, because the bounded-slab energy theorem does not rely on separation, whereas the mode and resolvent discussions use separation only as an additional diagnostic for the same operator.

Theorem 7.1

(Separated conformal equations)
.

The conformal equation (
5.5
) admits separated solutions of the form (
7.1
). For such a mode there exists a separation constant
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
such that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\left(\Delta_{r}\frac{\mathrm{d}R}{\mathrm{d}r}\right)+\left[\frac{((r^{2}+a^{2})\Omega-am)^{2}}{\Delta_{r}}+\frac{k}{3}r^{2}-\lambda\right]R

\displaystyle=0,

(7.5)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\left(\Delta_{x}\frac{\mathrm{d}S}{\mathrm{d}x}\right)+\left[-\frac{(a(1-x^{2})\Omega-m)^{2}}{\Delta_{x}}+\frac{k}{3}a^{2}x^{2}+\lambda\right]S

\displaystyle=0.

(7.6)

Conversely, any pair
(R,S)
solving (
7.5
)-(
7.6
) determines a separated solution of (
5.5
).

Proof.

Insert (
7.1
) into (
5.15
). After division by
RS
one obtains

\displaystyle 0={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\left(\Delta_{r}\frac{\mathrm{d}R}{\mathrm{d}r}\right)+\frac{((r^{2}+a^{2})\Omega-am)^{2}}{\Delta_{r}}+\frac{k}{3}r^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{S}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\left(\Delta_{x}\frac{\mathrm{d}S}{\mathrm{d}x}\right)-\frac{(a(1-x^{2})\Omega-m)^{2}}{\Delta_{x}}+\frac{k}{3}a^{2}x^{2}.

(7.7)

The first line depends only on
r
, the second only on
x
, so both must equal a constant with opposite signs. Writing that constant as
\lambda
gives the stated equations. The converse is immediate by reversing the calculation.
∎

7.1.1. Radial and angular operators

It is convenient to package (
7.5
)-(
7.6
) into differential operators. For fixed frequency parameters
(\Omega,m)
define

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(r)}_{\Omega,m}R

\displaystyle:=-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\left(\Delta_{r}\frac{\mathrm{d}R}{\mathrm{d}r}\right)-\left[\frac{((r^{2}+a^{2})\Omega-am)^{2}}{\Delta_{r}}+\frac{k}{3}r^{2}\right]R,

(7.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(x)}_{\Omega,m}S

\displaystyle:=-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\left(\Delta_{x}\frac{\mathrm{d}S}{\mathrm{d}x}\right)+\left[\frac{(a(1-x^{2})\Omega-m)^{2}}{\Delta_{x}}-\frac{k}{3}a^{2}x^{2}\right]S.

(7.9)

Then the separated equations read

\mathcal{L}^{(r)}_{\Omega,m}R=-\lambda R,\qquad\mathcal{L}^{(x)}_{\Omega,m}S=\lambda S.

(7.10)

For real
\Omega
the angular operator is formally symmetric on any interval where
\Delta_{x}>0
, and in the axisymmetric case
m=0
it reduces to a weighted Sturm-Liouville operator with a real potential. The radial operator is likewise formally symmetric on bounded intervals with suitable endpoint conditions, although on unbounded or horizon-penetrating domains one must pay attention to flux terms.

In the
k=0
regime these operators simplify substantially: the angular operator loses its bounded potential correction, and the radial operator loses the
r^{2}
term altogether. The remaining geometry is therefore carried entirely by
\Delta_{r}
and
\Delta_{x}
, which on the Theorem 1 family reduce to quadratic polynomials. This simplification is one of the main reasons why the
k=0
problem admits a particularly clean bounded-slab spectral theory.

7.2.  Wronskian identity

Separated second-order equations admit a conserved Wronskian. For the radial equation define

W[R_{1},R_{2}](r):=\Delta_{r}\bigl(R_{1}R_{2}^{\prime}-R_{1}^{\prime}R_{2}\bigr).

(7.11)

If
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
solve (
7.5
) with the same parameters
(\Omega,m,\lambda)
, then differentiating and using the equation shows

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}W[R_{1},R_{2}]=0.

(7.12)

In particular, for a complex solution
R
one has the current

\mathfrak{W}[R]:=\Delta_{r}\left(\overline{R}R^{\prime}-\overline{R}^{\prime}R\right)

(7.13)

constant in
r
. This is the radial flux associated with separated modes.

A completely analogous identity holds in the angular variable:

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\Bigl(\Delta_{x}(\overline{S}S^{\prime}-\overline{S}^{\prime}S)\Bigr)=0

(7.14)

for any two angular solutions with the same
(\Omega,m,\lambda)
. On bounded angular intervals regularity at endpoints usually forces this constant to vanish, yielding self-adjoint spectral conditions. In the radial variable, by contrast, nonzero Wronskian corresponds to nontrivial flux through horizons or through asymptotic ends.

The Kerr limit

If
k=0
,
\Lambda=0
, and
C_{1}=\cdots=C_{5}=0
, then

\Delta_{r}=r^{2}-2Mr+a^{2},\qquad\Delta_{x}=1-x^{2},

(7.15)

and (
7.5
)-(
7.6
) reduce to the familiar separated scalar equations on Kerr after the conventional reshuffling of the separation constant. This check is reassuring for two reasons. First, it confirms that the reduced theory constructed here is genuinely the scalar-wave theory of a Carter-type background in the appropriate limit. Second, it shows that the Theorem 1 family inherits the algebraic separability structure of the Einstein subfamily even though it is generically non-Einstein.

Separation constant as a spectral parameter

For real
\Omega
and under endpoint conditions making the angular operator self-adjoint, the separation constant
\lambda
is naturally viewed as an angular eigenvalue depending on
(\Omega,m)
and on the background parameters. One may therefore write

\lambda=\lambda_{\ell m}(\Omega),\qquad\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

(7.16)

in analogy with spheroidal harmonics. The corresponding eigenfunctions form an orthogonal basis of the angular
L^{2}
space under the appropriate weight, at least on bounded intervals with regular endpoints. This turns the separated mode analysis into a partially diagonalized representation of the full conformal dynamics.

Later sections exploit this perspective in two ways. First, on bounded slabs it allows one to recast the spatial operator as a direct sum of one-dimensional radial problems indexed by angular eigenmodes. Second, in perturbative comparisons near the Einstein limit it identifies the Theorem 1 angular operator as a lower-order deformation of the usual spheroidal operator. Although the current paper does not pursue full analytic perturbation theory for all complex frequencies, the self-adjoint real-frequency regime already supplies a robust spectral framework.

8.  Angular operator, spectral theory, and endpoint regularity

8.1.  Self-adjoint formulation for real frequencies

Fix a real frequency
\Omega\in\mathbb{R}
and an azimuthal number
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. On any closed interval
I_{x}\subset(-1,1)
or, more generally, on a compact angular segment contained in a regular exterior patch, the angular equation (
7.6
) can be written as the Sturm-Liouville problem

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\left(\Delta_{x}\frac{\mathrm{d}S}{\mathrm{d}x}\right)+V_{\Omega,m}(x)S

\displaystyle=\lambda S

\displaystyle V_{\Omega,m}(x)

\displaystyle:=\frac{(a(1-x^{2})\Omega-m)^{2}}{\Delta_{x}}-\frac{k}{3}a^{2}x^{2}.

(8.1)

Because
\Delta_{x}>0
on a regular patch and
V_{\Omega,m}
is real-valued for real
\Omega
, the operator is formally symmetric on
L^{2}(I_{x},\mathrm{d}x)
. With standard separated boundary conditions (Dirichlet, Neumann, Robin, or regularity conditions inherited from endpoint geometry), one obtains a self-adjoint realization with compact resolvent.

Proposition 8.1

(Discrete angular spectrum)
.

Let
I_{x}=[x_{-},x_{+}]
be a compact interval on which
\Delta_{x}>0
and let
\Omega\in\mathbb{R}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Any self-adjoint realization of (
8.1
) with separated boundary conditions has purely discrete real spectrum

\lambda_{0}(\Omega,m)\leq\lambda_{1}(\Omega,m)\leq\cdots\nearrow+\infty,

(8.2)

with each eigenvalue repeated according to multiplicity. The corresponding eigenfunctions form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(I_{x})
.

Proof.

Form the quadratic form

q_{\Omega,m}[S]:=\int_{I_{x}}\left(\Delta_{x}|S^{\prime}|^{2}+V_{\Omega,m}|S|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x

(8.3)

on the boundary domain determined by the chosen separated conditions. On a compact interval with
\Delta_{x}>0
, this is a closed semibounded form with compact embedding into
L^{2}(I_{x})
. If one endpoint is geometric rather than literal, Proposition
8.2
supplies the required Wronskian vanishing for the regular branch. The compact-resolvent argument of Appendix
D
, applied now in one space dimension, therefore yields a self-adjoint realization with purely discrete real spectrum and an orthonormal eigenbasis.
∎

The proposition is elementary but useful. It means that once the time and azimuthal frequencies are fixed, the angular variable does not create any continuous spectrum on bounded intervals. All nontrivial analytic issues are then pushed to the radial equation and to the dependence of the angular eigenvalues on
\Omega
.

Rayleigh quotient and lower bounds

For normalized eigenfunctions the separation constant can be characterized variationally:

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}(\Omega,m)

\displaystyle=\inf_{S\neq 0}\frac{\displaystyle\int_{I_{x}}\left(\Delta_{x}|S^{\prime}|^{2}+V_{\Omega,m}|S|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x}{\displaystyle\int_{I_{x}}|S|^{2}\mathrm{d}x}.

(8.4)

This formula immediately yields qualitative bounds. Since the potential
V_{\Omega,m}
is bounded below on a compact interval, one obtains

\lambda_{0}(\Omega,m)\geq\inf_{I_{x}}V_{\Omega,m}.

(8.5)

Likewise, because
\Delta_{x}
is positive, one has the coercive estimate

\int_{I_{x}}\Delta_{x}|S^{\prime}|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\leq\left(\lambda_{j}(\Omega,m)-\inf_{I_{x}}V_{\Omega,m}\right)\|S_{j}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

(8.6)

for each eigenfunction
S_{j}
. Such bounds are helpful when establishing continuity of the spectrum under parameter variation.

In the
k=0
axisymmetric case
m=0
, the potential simplifies to

V_{\Omega,0}(x)=\frac{a^{2}(1-x^{2})^{2}\Omega^{2}}{\Delta_{x}}\geq 0.

(8.7)

Thus for fixed real
\Omega
the angular operator is manifestly nonnegative on any compact interval where
\Delta_{x}>0
. This positivity is one of the basic inputs in the later bounded-slab analysis, where the only obstruction to coercivity comes from the constant spatial mode rather than from the angular sector.

8.2.  Endpoint regularity

When the interval endpoints correspond merely to coordinate boundaries inside a local patch, one is free to impose standard boundary conditions. In globally regular axisymmetric geometries, however, the endpoints may represent axis points, and then regularity imposes a more geometric condition. The precise condition depends on the normalization of the angular variable and on whether
\Delta_{x}
vanishes simply at the endpoints. Rather than commit to a single global model, we record the abstract regularity principle that is needed for analysis.

The endpoint regularity hypothesis is stated in
H7
of Assumption
B.2
. Under that hypothesis the angular Wronskian vanishes at geometric endpoints for regular solutions, and the Sturm-Liouville operator becomes self-adjoint on the corresponding domain. This is what happens in the standard Kerr case, where regularity of spheroidal harmonics at the poles replaces the need to prescribe explicit boundary values there.

Proposition 8.2

(Verification under the simple endpoint model)
.

Suppose
x_{\star}
is an angular endpoint with

\Delta_{x}(x)=\kappa_{\star}(x-x_{\star})+O\bigl((x-x_{\star})^{2}\bigr),\qquad\kappa_{\star}\neq 0,

(8.8)

and suppose the admissible separated modes are required to lie on the Frobenius branch selected by smoothness in an endpoint chart. Then the angular equation (
7.6
) has a regular singular point at
x_{\star}
, the regular branch is explicit, and the boundary Wronskian

\Delta_{x}(\overline{S}_{1}S_{2}^{\prime}-\overline{S}_{1}^{\prime}S_{2})

(8.9)

vanishes at
x_{\star}
for any two regular-branch solutions. In particular the corresponding Sturm-Liouville realization is self-adjoint. Thus the endpoint convention in
H7
of Assumption
B.2
is completely internal under this concrete endpoint model.

Proof.

Appendix
E
proves the relevant Frobenius expansion and the Wronskian vanishing statement.
∎

Dependence on parameters

Because the coefficients of (
8.1
) depend smoothly on
(a,\Lambda,k,C_{1},\dots,C_{5},\Omega,m)
away from degeneracies of
\Delta_{x}
, classical perturbation theory implies that simple eigenvalues vary smoothly with the parameters, while repeated eigenvalues vary continuously and admit local analytic branches after suitable diagonalization. We do not require the full Kato theory in detail, but the following consequence is useful.

Proposition 8.3

(Continuous dependence of angular eigenvalues)
.

Fix a compact parameter set on which
\Delta_{x}
stays uniformly positive on a fixed compact interval
I_{x}
. Then the angular eigenvalues and the spectral projectors depend continuously on the parameters in the operator norm topology.

Proof.

On such a compact set the differential operators (
8.1
) form a norm-continuous family from
H^{2}(I_{x})
to
L^{2}(I_{x})
with common domain determined by the chosen endpoint conditions. The associated self-adjoint resolvents therefore vary continuously, and the continuity of isolated spectral data follows from standard perturbation theory.
∎

This continuity is sufficient for all perturbative arguments later in the paper. In particular it shows that angular eigenvalues in the Theorem 1 family are small deformations of the familiar spheroidal eigenvalues near the Einstein limit.

Orthogonality and mode decomposition

Let
\{S_{j}^{\Omega,m}\}_{j\geq 0}
be an orthonormal basis of angular eigenfunctions. Any sufficiently regular solution of the conformal equation can then be decomposed formally as

u(t,\phi,r,x)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{j\geq 0}u_{jm}(t,r)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}m\phi}S_{j}^{\Omega,m}(x),

(8.10)

with the understanding that the precise meaning of the expansion depends on whether one works with fixed real frequency or with a time-dependent functional calculus for the angular operator. On bounded slabs this decomposition turns the spatial problem into a countable family of radial equations coupled only through the choice of frequency parameter. Even when one does not exploit the expansion explicitly, it is conceptually useful because it isolates the radial mechanism responsible for the low-frequency threshold structure.

An elementary
k=0
positivity statement

In the
k=0
axisymmetric case the angular potential is nonnegative, so for every real
\Omega
and every test function
S
one has

\displaystyle\int_{I_{x}}\left(\Delta_{x}|S^{\prime}|^{2}+\frac{a^{2}(1-x^{2})^{2}\Omega^{2}}{\Delta_{x}}|S|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 0.

(8.11)

This simple inequality records the main structural fact needed later: no negative contribution is created by the angular operator in the
k=0
axisymmetric theory.

9.  Radial equation, flux identities, and boundary data

9.1.  The radial operator on finite and infinite intervals

For a separated mode, the radial equation is

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\left(\Delta_{r}\frac{\mathrm{d}R}{\mathrm{d}r}\right)+\left[\frac{((r^{2}+a^{2})\Omega-am)^{2}}{\Delta_{r}}+\frac{k}{3}r^{2}-\lambda\right]R

\displaystyle=0.

(9.1)

The character of this equation depends strongly on the geometry of the radial interval. On a bounded slab with
\Delta_{r}>0
the leading coefficient is smooth and strictly positive, so the equation is an ordinary regular Sturm-Liouville equation after moving the frequency-dependent potential to one side. Near a simple root of
\Delta_{r}
, however, one encounters a regular singular point corresponding to a horizon-like boundary. At large
r
in the
k=0
regime, the asymptotic behavior is governed entirely by the quadratic polynomial
\Delta_{r}
, so one must distinguish Kerr-like, weakly confining, and finite-end geometries according to the sign of its leading coefficient.

These distinctions matter because all energy and boundedness statements are ultimately statements about allowable radial boundary conditions. Reflecting boundary conditions lead to self-adjoint spectral theory on bounded slabs. Ingoing or outgoing conditions at simple roots of
\Delta_{r}
correspond to nontrivial flux through horizons. Decay or radiative conditions at infinity depend on whether the end is asymptotically flat-like, weakly confining, or cut off by another regular boundary. One of the reasons for expanding the original note into a much longer paper is to keep these cases conceptually separate.

Wronskian flux and conserved radial current

For any complex solution
R
of (
9.1
) the Wronskian current (
7.13
) is constant in
r
:

\mathfrak{W}[R]=\Delta_{r}(\overline{R}R^{\prime}-\overline{R}^{\prime}R)=\text{const}.

(9.2)

Multiplying the radial equation by
\overline{R}
, subtracting the complex conjugate equation, and integrating over an interval
[r_{1},r_{2}]
yields

\mathfrak{W}[R](r_{2})-\mathfrak{W}[R](r_{1})=0.

(9.3)

Thus any boundary condition at one endpoint induces a corresponding flux at the other.

When
\Omega\in\mathbb{R}
, the quantity
\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}}\mathfrak{W}[R]
is the radial energy flux associated with the separated mode. Reflecting boundary conditions make it vanish. Ingoing boundary conditions at a horizon select a specific sign. This is the one-dimensional shadow of the conserved stress-energy current for the full scalar field.

Boundary conditions on bounded slabs

Let
I_{r}=[r_{1},r_{2}]
be a compact interval contained in a regular exterior patch. Three classical boundary conditions are relevant for the analysis below.

(i)

Dirichlet:

R(r_{1})=R(r_{2})=0
.

(ii)

Neumann:

R^{\prime}(r_{1})=R^{\prime}(r_{2})=0
.

(iii)

Robin/reflecting:

\alpha_{i}R(r_{i})+\beta_{i}\Delta_{r}(r_{i})R^{\prime}(r_{i})=0
with real coefficients
(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})
, not both zero.

Each of these conditions annihilates the boundary contribution in the integration by parts of the self-adjoint radial operator. In the time-dependent problem they correspond to vanishing radial energy flux. The bounded-slab stability theorem proved later is independent of which one of these reflecting classes is used, provided the constant test function remains admissible or can be approximated by admissible functions.

9.2.  Near-horizon asymptotics

Suppose
r_{h}
is a simple zero of
\Delta_{r}
, so

\Delta_{r}(r)=\kappa_{h}(r-r_{h})+O((r-r_{h})^{2}),\qquad\kappa_{h}\neq 0.

(9.4)

Then (
9.1
) has a regular singular point at
r=r_{h}
. A Frobenius analysis gives local solutions of the form

R(r)\sim(r-r_{h})^{\pm\mathrm{i}\sigma_{h}},\qquad\sigma_{h}:=\frac{(r_{h}^{2}+a^{2})\Omega-am}{\kappa_{h}}.

(9.5)

The sign choice corresponds to ingoing or outgoing behavior in a horizon-adapted coordinate system. For real
\Omega
these solutions oscillate logarithmically in
r-r_{h}
, and the Wronskian evaluates to a constant proportional to
\pm 2\sigma_{h}
.

The detailed geometric interpretation of these solutions depends on whether the zero of
\Delta_{r}
represents an event horizon, cosmological horizon, or another coordinate singularity. The present paper does not require a full global causal analysis of every parameter regime. What matters analytically is that a simple radial zero carries a natural one-parameter family of regular singular behaviors and that physical boundary conditions at such a point are flux conditions rather than reflective ones.

Large-
r
asymptotics in the
k=0
regime

In the zero-curvature regime the quartic contribution to
\Delta_{r}
disappears. The radial asymptotics are therefore controlled by the quadratic polynomial

\Delta_{r}(r)=\alpha_{2}r^{2}+\alpha_{1}r+\alpha_{0}.

(9.6)

If
\alpha_{2}>0
, then large
r
resembles a Kerr-like or weakly confining end depending on the lower-order coefficients; if
\alpha_{2}=0
, then the geometry is controlled by the linear and constant terms and one is effectively on a finite or asymptotically cylindrical end; if
\alpha_{2}<0
, then
\Delta_{r}
must eventually change sign and the regular exterior patch terminates at a finite outer root. Thus even inside the
k=0
family there are several radial geometries, and any global theorem must take them into account.

For the bounded-slab analysis of the present paper, however, these distinctions are secondary. The main theorem uses only that
\Delta_{r}
is smooth and positive on the chosen interval. Still, the
k=0
asymptotic classification is worth keeping in mind because it explains why the bounded-domain theorem is both robust and genuinely geometric: it does not depend on choosing a preferred global end structure.

A coercive identity on bounded intervals

For real
\Omega
and any solution
R
of (
9.1
), multiplication by
\overline{R}
and integration over a bounded interval yield

\displaystyle\int_{r_{1}}^{r_{2}}\left(\Delta_{r}|R^{\prime}|^{2}-\left[\frac{((r^{2}+a^{2})\Omega-am)^{2}}{\Delta_{r}}+\frac{k}{3}r^{2}-\lambda\right]|R|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\qquad=\left[\Delta_{r}\overline{R}R^{\prime}\right]_{r_{1}}^{r_{2}}.

(9.7)

Under any reflecting boundary condition the right-hand side vanishes, and the identity becomes a variational characterization of the admissible radial modes. Later, when combined with the angular spectral decomposition, this identity leads to the weighted Rayleigh quotient governing the bounded-slab spectral problem.

10.  Verification of the abstract bounded-slab theorem on strictly stationary Carter slabs

10.1.  The full
k=0
equation on
S^{1}_{\phi}\times\Omega

We now drop the axisymmetry restriction and retain the full
\phi
-dependence of the conformal wave equation. On a periodic slab

\Sigma=S^{1}_{\phi}\times\Omega,\qquad\Omega=[r_{-},r_{+}]\times[x_{-},x_{+}],

(10.1)

define

\displaystyle B(r,x)

\displaystyle:=\rho^{2}g^{t\phi}=-\frac{a(r^{2}+a^{2})}{\Delta_{r}}+\frac{a(1-x^{2})}{\Delta_{x}}

\displaystyle\Phi(r,x)

\displaystyle:=\rho^{2}g^{\phi\phi}=-\frac{a^{2}}{\Delta_{r}}+\frac{1}{\Delta_{x}}.

(10.2)

Then (
5.28
) becomes

\displaystyle-Au_{tt}+2B\,u_{t\phi}+\Phi\,u_{\phi\phi}+\partial_{r}(\Delta_{r}\partial_{r}u)+\partial_{x}(\Delta_{x}\partial_{x}u)

\displaystyle=0.

(10.3)

Because

\displaystyle g_{tt}

\displaystyle=\frac{a^{2}\Delta_{x}-\Delta_{r}}{\rho^{2}}

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{r}-a^{2}\Delta_{x}}{\Delta_{r}\Delta_{x}}=\frac{-\rho^{2}g_{tt}}{\Delta_{r}\Delta_{x}}

(10.4)

the positivity of
\Phi
is equivalent to the condition
g_{tt}<0
, namely that the stationary Killing field
\partial_{t}
stays timelike on the slab.

Definition 10.1

(Strictly stationary slab)
.

A
strictly stationary slab
is a product
\Sigma=S^{1}_{\phi}\times\Omega
, with
\Omega=[r_{-},r_{+}]\times[x_{-},x_{+}]
bounded and connected, such that the metric coefficients extend smoothly to a neighborhood of
\overline{\Omega}
and

\rho^{2}>0,\qquad\Delta_{r}>0,\qquad\Delta_{x}>0,\qquad A>0,\qquad\Phi>0

(10.5)

on
\overline{\Omega}
.

The inequalities
A>0
and
\Phi>0
are the precise bounded-domain conditions needed for a positive conserved energy. The first says that the
t=\mathrm{const}
slices are spacelike; the second says that
\partial_{t}
is timelike, so the slab contains no ergoregion. The condition
\Delta_{x}>0
is also a genuine interior-slab condition. If
x=\pm 1
is a true rotational axis and
\Delta_{x}
vanishes there, that endpoint is not part of Theorem
1.3
unless one replaces the present slab by a separately constructed regular-axis form realization. The separated regular-singular discussion later in the paper is therefore not an additional hypothesis of the full bounded-slab theorem.

The displayed sign conditions also mark exactly where the proof would fail. If
A
lost positivity, the kinetic part of the energy would no longer control
u_{t}
. If
\Phi
changed sign, the azimuthal derivative term could not be bounded below by
\|u_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
. Thus the theorem is not using an implicit small-rotation or separation assumption; it is using a concrete positive-energy region where each derivative appearing in the form is controlled.

Closed reflecting form domains and positive energy

All boundary conditions used in the proved theorem are imposed through a closed quadratic form. Concretely, throughout the full slab argument we use a closed subspace of the energy Sobolev space, dense in
L^{2}(\Sigma)
,

V_{\mathrm{full}}\subset H^{1}(\Sigma)

(10.6)

which consists of
\phi
-periodic functions, is invariant under complex conjugation, contains the constant function
1
, and is equipped with the closed spatial form

\displaystyle q[u,\eta]

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\left(\Phi u_{\phi}\overline{\eta_{\phi}}+\Delta_{r}u_{r}\overline{\eta_{r}}+\Delta_{x}u_{x}\overline{\eta_{x}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\phi\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x.

(10.7)

Thus “reflecting” is not an informal extra boundary condition: it means that the divergence terms in the strong equation are represented by this form. Natural Neumann realizations, regular endpoint realizations, and Robin realizations admitting the constant mode fit this framework. A pure homogeneous Dirichlet realization is a different coercive problem because it removes the threshold constant and is not the realization used in Theorem
1.3
. The complete list of theorem-level exterior conditions is given in Section
19
; the bounded-slab form-domain hypotheses are Assumption
B.2
.

10.2.  Core-free form interpretation of reflection

The preceding paragraph is the convention under which all subsequent integrations by parts are to be read. We record it as a lemma because it prevents a common ambiguity in bounded-domain wave arguments.

Lemma 10.2

(Form reflection is the primary boundary condition)
.

Let
V_{\mathrm{full}}\subset H^{1}(\Sigma)
be the closed form domain in Assumption
B.2
. Define

\displaystyle\langle M_{A}f,\eta\rangle

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Sigma}Af\overline{\eta}

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{full}}u,\eta\rangle

\displaystyle:=q[u,\eta]

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{full}}v,\eta\rangle

\displaystyle:=2\int_{\Sigma}Bv\overline{\eta_{\phi}}.

(10.8)

Then the weak equation

\displaystyle M_{A}u_{tt}+\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{full}}u_{t}+\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{full}}u

\displaystyle=0\quad\hbox{in }V_{\mathrm{full}}^{\prime}

(10.9)

is the definition of the reflected bounded-slab problem. If, in addition,
u
is smooth up to the boundary and the domain is the natural Neumann realization, then (
10.9
) is equivalent to the strong equation (
10.3
) together with the conormal boundary condition

\Delta_{r}u_{r}n_{r}+\Delta_{x}u_{x}n_{x}=0\quad\hbox{on }\partial\Omega,

(10.10)

where
(n_{r},n_{x})
is the Euclidean outward normal in the
(r,x)
-rectangle. For a general closed subspace
V_{\mathrm{full}}
, no pointwise trace condition is assumed beyond membership in that form domain.

Proof.

For smooth functions satisfying the displayed conormal condition, multiply (
10.3
) by
-\overline{\eta}
, integrate over
\Sigma
, use periodicity in
\phi
, and integrate the
r
- and
x
-divergence terms. The boundary contribution is

-\int_{S^{1}_{\phi}\times\partial\Omega}(\Delta_{r}u_{r}n_{r}+\Delta_{x}u_{x}n_{x})\overline{\eta}\,\mathrm{d}S,

(10.11)

which is zero. The remaining terms are exactly (
10.9
). Conversely, if the weak identity holds for all compactly supported tests in the interior and
u
is smooth, the strong PDE follows by the fundamental lemma of the calculus of variations. Testing against arbitrary smooth boundary-admissible
\eta
then leaves precisely the conormal boundary functional above, hence the natural boundary condition. For a closed form domain the first implication is not used as an assumption: the closed form itself defines the realization, and (
10.9
) is meaningful without assigning pointwise traces.
∎

Lemma 10.3

(Boundedness and skewness of the gyroscopic operator)
.

The form

\mathfrak{c}[v,\eta]:=2\int_{\Sigma}Bv\overline{\eta_{\phi}}

(10.12)

extends continuously from
L^{2}(\Sigma)\times V_{\mathrm{full}}
to
\mathbb{C}
, and its restriction to
V_{\mathrm{full}}\times V_{\mathrm{full}}
satisfies

\mathfrak{c}[v,w]=-\overline{\mathfrak{c}[w,v]},\qquad\Re\mathfrak{c}[v,v]=0.

(10.13)

Consequently
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{full}}:L^{2}(\Sigma)\to V_{\mathrm{full}}^{\prime}
is a bounded skew gyroscopic perturbation of the conservative wave equation.

Proof.

Since
B\in L^{\infty}(\Sigma)
,

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{c}[v,\eta]|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|B\|_{L^{\infty}}\|v\|_{L^{2}}\|\eta_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\|v\|_{L^{2}}\|\eta\|_{V_{\mathrm{full}}}.

(10.14)

If
v,w\in V_{\mathrm{full}}
, periodic integration by parts in
\phi
, together with
\partial_{\phi}B=0
, gives

2\int_{\Sigma}Bv\overline{w_{\phi}}=-2\int_{\Sigma}Bv_{\phi}\overline{w}.

(10.15)

Taking the complex conjugate of the same identity with
v
and
w
interchanged gives the stated skew-Hermitian relation. The second identity follows by setting
w=v
.
∎

Theorem 10.4

(Conserved full energy for smooth form-admissible solutions)
.

Let
u
be a sufficiently regular complex-valued solution of (
10.3
) on
[0,T]\times\Sigma
, with
\Sigma
a strictly stationary slab and with each time slice lying in the form-realized reflecting domain
V_{\mathrm{full}}
. Then

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{full}}[u](t)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\left(A|u_{t}|^{2}+\Phi|u_{\phi}|^{2}+\Delta_{r}|u_{r}|^{2}+\Delta_{x}|u_{x}|^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\phi\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x

(10.16)

is independent of
t
.

Proof.

Multiply (
10.3
) by
\overline{u_{t}}
and take real parts. Since the coefficients are independent of
t
and
\phi
,

\displaystyle\Re(-Au_{tt}\overline{u_{t}})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}(A|u_{t}|^{2}),

\displaystyle\Re(2Bu_{t\phi}\overline{u_{t}})

\displaystyle=B\,\partial_{\phi}(|u_{t}|^{2}),

\displaystyle\Re(\Phi u_{\phi\phi}\overline{u_{t}})

\displaystyle=\partial_{\phi}\Re(\Phi u_{\phi}\overline{u_{t}})-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}(\Phi|u_{\phi}|^{2}),

\displaystyle\Re(\partial_{r}(\Delta_{r}u_{r})\overline{u_{t}})

\displaystyle=\partial_{r}\Re(\Delta_{r}u_{r}\overline{u_{t}})-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}(\Delta_{r}|u_{r}|^{2}),

\displaystyle\Re(\partial_{x}(\Delta_{x}u_{x})\overline{u_{t}})

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\Re(\Delta_{x}u_{x}\overline{u_{t}})-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}(\Delta_{x}|u_{x}|^{2}).

(10.17)

Integrating over
\Sigma
, the total
\phi
-derivatives vanish by periodicity and the
(r,x)
-boundary flux vanishes by the reflecting hypothesis. The remaining terms give

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}E_{\mathrm{full}}[u](t)=0.

(10.18)

∎

10.3.  Weak form, gyroscopic skewness, and quantitative energy constants

We now write the full bounded-slab equation in the exact weak form used in the proof. Let
V_{\mathrm{full}}\subset H^{1}(\Sigma)
be the closed reflecting form domain; for the standard natural reflecting realization one has
V_{\mathrm{full}}=H^{1}(\Sigma)
. The domain is assumed to contain constants and to encode periodicity in
\phi
. Define, for
u,\eta\in V_{\mathrm{full}}
,

\displaystyle q[u,\eta]

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Sigma}\left(\Phi u_{\phi}\overline{\eta_{\phi}}+\Delta_{r}u_{r}\overline{\eta_{r}}+\Delta_{x}u_{x}\overline{\eta_{x}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\phi\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x,

(10.19)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}[v,\eta]

\displaystyle:=-2\int_{\Sigma}B\,v_{\phi}\overline{\eta}\,\mathrm{d}\phi\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=2\int_{\Sigma}B\,v\overline{\eta_{\phi}}\,\mathrm{d}\phi\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x.

(10.20)

For
v\in V_{\mathrm{full}}
the two formulas in (
10.20
) agree by periodic integration by parts in
\phi
, using
\partial_{\phi}B=0
. The second formula is the one used to extend
\mathfrak{c}
boundedly to
L^{2}(\Sigma)\times V_{\mathrm{full}}
, since
B\in L^{\infty}
. The weak equation associated with (
10.3
) is

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}Au_{tt}\overline{\eta}\,\mathrm{d}\phi\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x+q[u,\eta]+\mathfrak{c}[u_{t},\eta]=0,\quad\eta\in V_{\mathrm{full}}.

(10.21)

Indeed, multiplying (
10.3
) by
-1
, integrating against
\overline{\eta}
, and using the reflecting boundary condition gives (
10.21
).

The gyroscopic form is skew-Hermitian. For
v,w\in V_{\mathrm{full}}
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}[v,w]

\displaystyle=-2\int_{\Sigma}Bv_{\phi}\overline{w}=2\int_{\Sigma}Bv\overline{w_{\phi}},

\displaystyle-\overline{\mathfrak{c}[w,v]}

\displaystyle=-\overline{-2\int_{\Sigma}Bw_{\phi}\overline{v}}=2\int_{\Sigma}Bv\overline{w_{\phi}}.

(10.22)

Thus

\mathfrak{c}[v,w]=-\overline{\mathfrak{c}[w,v]},\qquad\Re\mathfrak{c}[v,v]=0.

(10.23)

Taking
\eta=u_{t}
in (
10.21
), using (
10.23
), and differentiating
q[u,u]
gives the weak energy identity

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{\Sigma}A|u_{t}|^{2}+q[u,u]\right)=0.

(10.24)

This is the same identity as Theorem
10.4
, but now no pointwise integration-by-parts regularity is hidden.

Theorem 10.5

(Weak energy equality)
.

Let
u
satisfy the weak equation (
10.21
) on an interval
I
in the following energy sense:

\displaystyle u\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(I;V_{\mathrm{full}})

\displaystyle u_{t}\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(I;L^{2}(\Sigma))

\displaystyle u_{tt}\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(I;V_{\mathrm{full}}^{\prime})

(10.25)

and the identity holds in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(I;V_{\mathrm{full}}^{\prime})
. Then the function

E_{\mathrm{full}}[u,u_{t}](t)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}A|u_{t}(t)|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}q[u(t),u(t)]

(10.26)

has a representative which is constant on
I
. In particular, for the strongly continuous energy solutions constructed in Theorem
10.9
, the displayed energy is independent of
t
for every
t\in I
.

Proof.

Let
J\Subset I
and let
\chi_{\varepsilon}
be an even time mollifier supported so close to zero that convolution is defined on
J
. Convolving the weak equation in time gives, in
V_{\mathrm{full}}^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}A(\chi_{\varepsilon}*u_{tt})\overline{\eta}+q[\chi_{\varepsilon}*u,\eta]+\mathfrak{c}[\chi_{\varepsilon}*u_{t},\eta]=0,\quad\eta\in V_{\mathrm{full}}.

(10.27)

Because the coefficients are independent of
t
and
u\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(I;V_{\mathrm{full}})
, the mollified function
u^{\varepsilon}:=\chi_{\varepsilon}*u
is smooth as a
V_{\mathrm{full}}
-valued function. Moreover

(u^{\varepsilon})_{t}=\chi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}*u\in V_{\mathrm{full}},

(10.28)

and this derivative agrees distributionally with
\chi_{\varepsilon}*u_{t}
. This point is important: the test function belongs to the form domain even though the unmollified velocity is only in
L^{2}
. Taking
\eta=(u^{\varepsilon})_{t}
and taking real parts gives

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}A|(u^{\varepsilon})_{t}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}q[u^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon}]\right)

\displaystyle=0

(10.29)

on
J
, because
\Re\,\mathfrak{c}[(u^{\varepsilon})_{t},(u^{\varepsilon})_{t}]=0
. Since
u\in L^{\infty}(J;V_{\mathrm{full}})
and
u_{t}\in L^{\infty}(J;L^{2})
, standard properties of time convolution give

\displaystyle u^{\varepsilon}\to u\quad\hbox{in }L^{2}(J;V_{\mathrm{full}})

\displaystyle(u^{\varepsilon})_{t}\to u_{t}\quad\hbox{in }L^{2}(J;L^{2}(\Sigma)).

(10.30)

Consequently the mollified energies converge to
E_{\mathrm{full}}[u,u_{t}]
in
L^{1}(J)
. Each mollified energy is constant on the slightly smaller interval on which the convolution is defined; hence the distributional derivative of the limiting energy is zero on
J
. Thus
E_{\mathrm{full}}[u,u_{t}]
has a constant representative on
J
. Since
J\Subset I
was arbitrary, the representative is constant on all of
I
. If
u
and
u_{t}
are strongly continuous in the energy topology, this representative agrees with the pointwise quadratic expression for every time.
∎

Lemma 10.6

(Pointwise recovery of the conserved weak energy)
.

In the setting of Theorem
10.5
, assume in addition that

u\in C(I;V_{\mathrm{full}}),\qquad u_{t}\in C(I;L^{2}(\Sigma)).

(10.31)

Then the constant representative of the energy agrees at every time with the pointwise expression

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}A|u_{t}(t)|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}q[u(t),u(t)].

(10.32)

Consequently no exceptional time set is hidden in the conservation law used in Theorem
1.3
.

Proof.

The map
t\mapsto\int_{\Sigma}A|u_{t}(t)|^{2}
is continuous because
A\in L^{\infty}
and
u_{t}
is strongly continuous in
L^{2}
. The map
t\mapsto q[u(t),u(t)]
is continuous because
q
is a continuous quadratic form on the Hilbert space
V_{\mathrm{full}}
. Hence the pointwise energy is a continuous representative of the same
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
function whose distributional derivative is zero in Theorem
10.5
. A continuous function agreeing almost everywhere with a constant is equal to that constant everywhere.
∎

For later coercivity estimates set

\displaystyle a_{-}

\displaystyle:=\inf_{\overline{\Omega}}A,\qquad a_{+}:=\sup_{\overline{\Omega}}A,

\displaystyle\phi_{-}

\displaystyle:=\inf_{\overline{\Omega}}\Phi,\qquad\phi_{+}:=\sup_{\overline{\Omega}}\Phi,

\displaystyle d_{r,-}

\displaystyle:=\inf_{[r_{-},r_{+}]}\Delta_{r},\qquad d_{r,+}:=\sup_{[r_{-},r_{+}]}\Delta_{r},

\displaystyle d_{x,-}

\displaystyle:=\inf_{[x_{-},x_{+}]}\Delta_{x},\qquad d_{x,+}:=\sup_{[x_{-},x_{+}]}\Delta_{x}.

(10.33)

On a strictly stationary slab all lower constants are positive and all upper constants are finite. Hence

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}a_{-}\|u_{t}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}m_{\nabla}\|\nabla_{\phi,r,x}u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq E_{\mathrm{full}}[u,u_{t}]\leq\frac{1}{2}a_{+}\|u_{t}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}M_{\nabla}\|\nabla_{\phi,r,x}u\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

(10.34)

where

m_{\nabla}:=\min\{\phi_{-},d_{r,-},d_{x,-}\},\qquad M_{\nabla}:=\max\{\phi_{+},d_{r,+},d_{x,+}\}.

(10.35)

Thus the only missing part of the
H^{1}\times L^{2}
norm is the
L^{2}
-size of
u
, exactly as claimed.

If
C_{P}
is the weighted Poincaré constant in Proposition
10.11
, then on the threshold complement

\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C_{P}q[u,u].

(10.36)

Consequently

\displaystyle c_{\rm low}

\displaystyle:=\min\left\{\frac{a_{-}}{2},\frac{1}{2(C_{P}+m_{\nabla}^{-1})}\right\},

\displaystyle C_{\rm high}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\max\{a_{+},M_{\nabla}\}.

(10.37)

satisfy

\displaystyle c_{\rm low}\bigl(\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma)}^{2}+\|u_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq E_{\mathrm{full}}[u,u_{t}]\leq C_{\rm high}\bigl(\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma)}^{2}+\|u_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\bigr)

(10.38)

whenever
\Pi_{0}u=\Pi_{0}u_{t}=0
. Therefore one may take

C_{\rm stab}=\left(\frac{C_{\rm high}}{c_{\rm low}}\right)^{1/2}

(10.39)

in Theorem
1.3
, up to the harmless equivalence constants between the standard and coefficient-weighted norms.

Proposition 10.7

(Finite-dimensional Galerkin identity)
.

Let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{N}\in V_{\mathrm{full}}
and set
u_{N}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}d_{j}(t)e_{j}
. The Galerkin equation

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}A(u_{N})_{tt}\overline{e}_{j}+q[u_{N},e_{j}]+\mathfrak{c}[(u_{N})_{t},e_{j}]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\leq j\leq N

(10.40)

has the exact conserved energy

E_{N}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}A|(u_{N})_{t}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}q[u_{N},u_{N}].

(10.41)

Moreover the
A
-weighted mean satisfies

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\int_{\Sigma}Au_{N}=0

(10.42)

whenever the constant function belongs to the Galerkin space.

Proof.

Multiply (
10.40
) by
\overline{d_{j}^{\prime}(t)}
, sum over
j
, and take real parts. The mass term gives

\Re\int_{\Sigma}A(u_{N})_{tt}\overline{(u_{N})_{t}}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Sigma}A|(u_{N})_{t}|^{2}.

(10.43)

The symmetric form gives

\Re q[u_{N},(u_{N})_{t}]=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}q[u_{N},u_{N}].

(10.44)

The gyroscopic term has zero real part by (
10.23
). Hence
E_{N}^{\prime}(t)=0
. If
1
is an admissible test function, then taking
e_{j}=1
in the weak equation, or equivalently testing (
10.21
) with
1
, gives
q[u_{N},1]=0
and
\mathfrak{c}[(u_{N})_{t},1]=0
. Therefore

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\int_{\Sigma}Au_{N}=0.

(10.45)

This proves both identities.
∎

Lemma 10.8

(Galerkin limit, energy recovery, and strong continuity)
.

Let
u_{N}
be Galerkin solutions satisfying (
10.55
) on a compact interval
J=[-T,T]
, with data converging strongly in
V_{\mathrm{full}}\times L^{2}(\Sigma)
. Assume that a subsequence converges weak-star as

\displaystyle u_{N}\rightharpoonup^{*}u\quad\hbox{in }L^{\infty}(J;V_{\mathrm{full}})

\displaystyle(u_{N})_{t}\rightharpoonup^{*}u_{t}\quad\hbox{in }L^{\infty}(J;L^{2})

(10.46)

and that
(u_{N})_{tt}\rightharpoonup^{*}u_{tt}
in
L^{\infty}(J;V_{\mathrm{full}}^{\prime})
. Then the limit solves the weak equation. If the weak solution is unique, the whole Galerkin sequence has the same limit. Furthermore the affine averages converge explicitly,

\Pi_{0}u_{N}(t)\to\Pi_{0}u(0)+t\Pi_{0}u_{t}(0),\qquad\Pi_{0}(u_{N})_{t}(t)\to\Pi_{0}u_{t}(0),

(10.47)

and the convergence is strong in the augmented energy topology at every fixed time for which the energy identity holds for the limit. Consequently the limit has a representative in

C(J;V_{\mathrm{full}})\cap C^{1}(J;L^{2}(\Sigma))

(10.48)

when written in first-order form
(u,u_{t})\in C(J;V_{\mathrm{full}}\times L^{2})
.

Proof.

Passing to the limit in the finite-dimensional identities is legitimate because each term is paired with a fixed
\eta\in V_{\mathrm{full}}
, and finite-dimensional projections of
\eta
converge strongly in
V_{\mathrm{full}}
. Thus the limit solves (
10.21
). The affine identities follow by testing the Galerkin equations with the constant basis vector and passing to the limit in the scalar relation

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\int_{\Sigma}Au_{N}=0.

(10.49)

For strong recovery, decompose each time slice into its
A
-average and its mean-zero part. The averages converge by the explicit affine formula. On the mean-zero part, Proposition
10.11
makes the energy seminorm equivalent to the full
V_{\mathrm{full}}\times L^{2}
-norm. Lower semicontinuity gives

E_{\mathrm{full}}[u,u_{t}](t)\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}E_{N}(t).

(10.50)

Since
E_{N}(t)=E_{N}(0)
and the initial data converge strongly, the right side equals the initial limiting energy. Whenever the weak energy identity holds for
u
, equality holds in the preceding lower-semicontinuity inequality. In a Hilbert space, weak convergence plus convergence of the norm implies strong convergence. Applying this to the kinetic and spatial form components and then adding the affine average part gives strong convergence in the augmented energy topology. The same argument applied on a short interval to time-translated solutions gives norm continuity in time. Uniqueness removes the need to pass to subsequences.
∎

The proposition shows explicitly where every sign is used. Positivity of
A,\Phi,\Delta_{r},\Delta_{x}
supplies the energy; independence of
B
from
\phi
supplies skewness; periodicity and reflection remove boundary flux; and the admission of constants identifies the affine threshold.

Because
A,\Phi,\Delta_{r},\Delta_{x}
are positive and bounded above and below on a strictly stationary slab,
E_{\mathrm{full}}
controls the standard
L^{2}
-norm of
u_{t}
together with the standard
L^{2}
-norm of the spatial gradient on
\Sigma
. The only quantity not controlled directly is the
L^{2}
-size of
u
itself, and that failure is the
k=0
zero-mode phenomenon.

The next theorem internalizes the corresponding well-posedness input.

Theorem 10.9

(Direct well-posedness of the full slab evolution)
.

Let
\Sigma=S^{1}_{\phi}\times\Omega
be a strictly stationary slab equipped with a form-realized reflecting domain
V_{\rm full}\subset H^{1}(\Sigma)
as in Section
19
. For every initial datum

(u_{0},u_{1})\in V_{\rm full}\times L^{2}(\Sigma)

(10.51)

there exists a unique energy solution of (
10.3
), equivalently of (
10.21
), such that

\displaystyle u\in C(\mathbb{R};V_{\rm full})

\displaystyle u_{t}\in C(\mathbb{R};L^{2}(\Sigma))

\displaystyle u_{tt}\in L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R};V_{\rm full}^{\prime}).

(10.52)

The weak energy equality of Theorem
10.5
holds for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and the solution maps

\mathcal{S}(t):(u_{0},u_{1})\longmapsto(u(t),u_{t}(t))

(10.53)

form a strongly continuous group on
\mathcal{X}_{\rm full}:=V_{\rm full}\times L^{2}(\Sigma)
.

Proof.

We give the construction in the main text because this is the analytic point at which the boundary realization, the gyroscopic sign, and the threshold constant all enter.

Equip
V_{\rm full}
with the Hilbert norm

\|u\|_{V_{\rm full}}^{2}:=\int_{\Sigma}A|u|^{2}+q[u,u].

(10.54)

By strict stationarity this norm is equivalent to the inherited
H^{1}(\Sigma)
norm. Since
V_{\rm full}
is dense in
L^{2}(\Sigma)
and contains
1
, choose a Hilbert basis
\{w_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
of
V_{\rm full}
with
w_{1}
proportional to
1
. Let
V_{N}=\mathrm{span}\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{N}\}
and choose data
u_{0,N},u_{1,N}\in V_{N}
converging to
u_{0}
in
V_{\rm full}
and to
u_{1}
in
L^{2}(\Sigma)
.

Seek
u_{N}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}d_{j}^{(N)}(t)w_{j}
satisfying, for every
\eta\in V_{N}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}A(u_{N})_{tt}\overline{\eta}+q[u_{N},\eta]+\mathfrak{c}[(u_{N})_{t},\eta]

\displaystyle=0.

(10.55)

In coordinates this is

M_{N}\ddot{d}_{N}+C_{N}\dot{d}_{N}+K_{N}d_{N}=0,

(10.56)

where
M_{N}
is Hermitian positive definite,
K_{N}
is Hermitian nonnegative, and
C_{N}
is skew-Hermitian. The last assertion follows from (
10.23
); explicitly,

(C_{N})_{ij}=\mathfrak{c}[w_{j},w_{i}],\qquad(C_{N})_{ij}=-\overline{(C_{N})_{ji}}.

(10.57)

Thus the finite-dimensional system has a global smooth solution. Multiplying by
\dot{d}_{N}^{*}
gives

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\dot{d}_{N}^{*}M_{N}\dot{d}_{N}+d_{N}^{*}K_{N}d_{N}\bigr)=0,

(10.58)

which is exactly the Galerkin energy identity

E_{N}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}A|(u_{N})_{t}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}q[u_{N},u_{N}]=E_{N}(0).

(10.59)

Testing (
10.55
) with the constant basis vector gives the exact affine law

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\int_{\Sigma}Au_{N}(t)=0.

(10.60)

Combining this affine law with the weighted Poincaré inequality for the mean-zero part gives, on every compact interval
[-T,T]
,

\displaystyle\sup_{|t|\leq T}\bigl(\|u_{N}(t)\|_{V_{\rm full}}+\|(u_{N})_{t}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C_{T}\bigl(\|u_{0,N}\|_{V_{\rm full}}+\|u_{1,N}\|_{L^{2}}\bigr).

(10.61)

The equation itself gives a uniform bound for
(u_{N})_{tt}
in
L^{\infty}([-T,T];V_{\rm full}^{\prime})
, because
q
is bounded on
V_{\rm full}
and
\mathfrak{c}
is bounded from
L^{2}(\Sigma)\times V_{\rm full}
to
\mathbb{C}
.

Banach-Alaoglu gives a subsequence, still denoted
u_{N}
, and a function
u
such that

\displaystyle u_{N}\rightharpoonup^{*}u\text{ in }L^{\infty}([-T,T];V_{\rm full})

\displaystyle(u_{N})_{t}\rightharpoonup^{*}u_{t}\text{ in }L^{\infty}([-T,T];L^{2})

(10.62)

and

(u_{N})_{tt}\rightharpoonup^{*}u_{tt}\text{ in }L^{\infty}([-T,T];V_{\rm full}^{\prime}).

(10.63)

The standard Lions-Magenes compactness argument gives weak continuity of
u
with values in
V_{\rm full}
and of
u_{t}
with values in
L^{2}
. Passing to the limit in (
10.55
) gives (
10.21
). The recovery argument in Lemma
10.8
, together with the weak energy identity, upgrades the convergence to strong convergence in the augmented energy norm. In particular,

(u(t),u_{t}(t))\in V_{\rm full}\times L^{2}(\Sigma)

(10.64)

for every
t
, the map
t\mapsto(u(t),u_{t}(t))
is strongly continuous, and the initial data are attained in the energy topology.

Uniqueness is an energy argument. If
w
is the difference of two energy solutions with zero initial data, Theorem
10.5
gives
E_{\rm full}[w,w_{t}](t)=0
. The weighted average also obeys the affine law and is zero initially. Hence
w_{t}=0
,
q[w,w]=0
, and
\Pi_{0}w=0
; Proposition
10.10
then gives
w=0
.

The same estimate applied to the difference of two solutions gives continuous dependence in the augmented norm

\|(u,v)\|_{*}^{2}:=2E_{\rm full}[u,v]+|\Pi_{0}u|^{2}+|\Pi_{0}v|^{2},

(10.65)

which is equivalent to
V_{\rm full}\times L^{2}
by Proposition
10.11
. The group law follows from uniqueness by restarting the solution at time
s
, and strong continuity follows from the continuity already obtained for
u
and
u_{t}
. Since
T
was arbitrary, the construction is global on
\mathbb{R}
.
∎

10.4.  Kernel, affine average, and weighted Poincaré inequality

Define the full spatial quadratic form

\displaystyle q[u]

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Sigma}\left(\Phi|u_{\phi}|^{2}+\Delta_{r}|u_{r}|^{2}+\Delta_{x}|u_{x}|^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\phi\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x.

(10.66)

It is nonnegative, and its nullspace is again one-dimensional.

Proposition 10.10

(Kernel of the full spatial form)
.

Assume that
\Sigma
is strictly stationary, connected, periodic in
\phi
, and equipped with reflecting boundary conditions admitting the constant function. Then

q[u]=0\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad u\in\mathrm{span}\{1\}.

(10.67)

In particular, the kernel of the full bounded-slab dynamics is generated by constants.

Proof.

If
u
is constant, then clearly
q[u]=0
. Conversely, if
q[u]=0
, then the strict positivity of
\Phi
,
\Delta_{r}
, and
\Delta_{x}
implies

u_{\phi}=u_{r}=u_{x}=0

(10.68)

almost everywhere on
\Sigma
. Because
\Sigma
is connected,
u
is constant.
∎

Let

\displaystyle\Pi_{0}f

\displaystyle:=\frac{\int_{\Sigma}Af\,\mathrm{d}\phi\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x}{\int_{\Sigma}A\,\mathrm{d}\phi\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x}\,1

(10.69)

denote the
A
-weighted projection onto constants.

Proposition 10.11

(Weighted Poincaré inequality on
\Sigma
)
.

There exists
C_{P}>0
such that

\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\leq C_{P}\,q[u]

(10.70)

for every
u\in H^{1}(\Sigma)
satisfying
\Pi_{0}u=0
.

Proof.

Suppose the conclusion were false. Then there would exist
u_{n}\in H^{1}(\Sigma)
with
\Pi_{0}u_{n}=0
,

\|u_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}=1,\qquad q[u_{n}]\to 0.

(10.71)

Since the coefficients in
q
are positive and bounded below,
\{u_{n}\}
is bounded in
H^{1}(\Sigma)
. By Rellich compactness, after passing to a subsequence we have

u_{n}\to u_{\infty}\quad\text{strongly in }L^{2}(\Sigma).

(10.72)

The convergence
q[u_{n}]\to 0
implies
\nabla u_{n}\to 0
in
L^{2}(\Sigma)
, hence
u_{\infty}
is constant. The weighted mean-zero condition passes to the limit because
A
is bounded, so
\Pi_{0}u_{\infty}=0
. Therefore
u_{\infty}=0
, contradicting
\|u_{\infty}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}=1
.
∎

The weighted spatial average obeys the same affine law as in the axisymmetric problem.

Lemma 10.12

(Affine evolution of the weighted average)
.

Let
u
be a sufficiently regular solution of (
10.3
) on a strictly stationary slab with periodic/reflection boundary conditions. Then

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\int_{\Sigma}A\,u(t,\phi,r,x)\,\mathrm{d}\phi\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x=0.

(10.73)

Equivalently,

\Pi_{0}u(t)=\Pi_{0}u(0)+t\,\Pi_{0}u_{t}(0).

(10.74)

Proof.

Integrate (
10.3
) over
\Sigma
. The
\phi
-derivative terms vanish by periodicity, and the
(r,x)
-divergence terms vanish by the reflecting boundary conditions. The result is

-\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\int_{\Sigma}A\,u=0.

(10.75)

Dividing by
\int_{\Sigma}A
gives the projector identity.
∎

Definition 10.13

(Closed-form generator of the full slab equation)
.

Set

H_{A}=L^{2}(\Sigma,A\,\mathrm{d}\phi\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x).

(10.76)

Define the mass map
M_{A}
by

\langle M_{A}f,\eta\rangle=\int_{\Sigma}Af\overline{\eta}.

(10.77)

Let
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{full}}:V_{\mathrm{full}}\to V_{\mathrm{full}}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{full}}:L^{2}(\Sigma)\to V_{\mathrm{full}}^{\prime}
be defined by

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{full}}u,\eta\rangle

\displaystyle=q[u,\eta]

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{full}}v,\eta\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{c}[v,\eta].

(10.78)

The generator on the energy space
\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{full}}=V_{\mathrm{full}}\times H_{A}
is

\mathcal{G}(u,v)=\left(v,-M_{A}^{-1}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{full}}u+\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{full}}v)\right)

(10.79)

with domain

\displaystyle\operatorname{Dom}(\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle=\left\{(u,v)\in V_{\mathrm{full}}\times V_{\mathrm{full}}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{full}}u+\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{full}}v\in M_{A}(H_{A})\right\}.

(10.80)

This definition is exactly the first-order form of (
10.21
).

Proposition 10.14

(Operator-theoretic threshold classification)
.

For the generator in Definition
10.13
,

\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{G}=\mathrm{span}\{(1,0)\},\qquad\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{G}^{2}=\mathrm{span}\{(1,0),(0,1)\},

(10.81)

and

\bigcup_{N\geq 1}\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{G}^{N}=\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{G}^{2}.

(10.82)

Proof.

If
(u,v)\in\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{G}
, then
v=0
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{full}}u=0
. Testing the latter with
u
gives
q[u,u]=0
, so Proposition
10.10
gives
u
constant. Hence
\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{G}=\mathrm{span}\{(1,0)\}
.

If
(u,v)\in\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{G}^{2}
, then
\mathcal{G}(u,v)\in\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{G}
. Thus
\mathcal{G}(u,v)=(c,0)
for a constant
c
. The first component gives
v=c
, and the second gives

\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{full}}u+\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{full}}c=0.

(10.83)

Since a constant has zero
\phi
-derivative,
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{full}}c=0
. Testing with
u
gives
q[u,u]=0
, hence
u
is constant. This proves the formula for
\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{G}^{2}
.

For
N\geq 3
, let
(u_{0},u_{1})\in\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{G}^{N}
. The corresponding solution is a polynomial in
t
with values in the energy space. Subtract its affine
A
-average part using Lemma
10.12
. The remainder lies in the threshold complement for all
t
, and the energy equivalence on that complement follows directly from Proposition
10.11
and the explicit constants (
10.37
), so this polynomial is uniformly bounded in
V_{\mathrm{full}}\times L^{2}
. A bounded Hilbert-space polynomial is constant; substituting the constant remainder into the weak equation gives zero spatial form, hence the remainder vanishes by Proposition
10.10
and the zero average. Therefore no generalized threshold vectors beyond order two occur.
∎

10.5.  Verification of the abstract conditions and proof of Theorem
1.3

By Theorem
10.9
, the full equation determines a strongly continuous solution group

\mathcal{S}(t):(u_{0},u_{1})\longmapsto(u(t),u_{t}(t))

(10.84)

on the energy space

\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{full}}:=V_{\mathrm{full}}\times L^{2}(\Sigma).

(10.85)

We now identify the threshold projector and the coercive threshold complement.

Proposition 10.15

(Concrete threshold projection on the Carter slab)
.

Define

\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}(u_{0},u_{1}):=\bigl(\Pi_{0}u_{0},\Pi_{0}u_{1}\bigr)

(10.86)

on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{full}}
, where
\Pi_{0}
is the
A
-weighted projection onto constants from (
10.69
). Then the following statements hold.

(i)

\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
is a bounded projection with range

\operatorname{Ran}\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}=\mathrm{span}\{(1,0),(0,1)\}.

(10.87)

(ii)

\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
commutes with the solution group
\mathcal{S}(t)
.

(iii)

\operatorname{Ran}\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
is the full zero-frequency threshold space of the full bounded-slab evolution, as classified operator-theoretically in Proposition
10.14
. Equivalently, the associated threshold solutions are precisely the affine functions

u(t,\phi,r,x)=c_{0}+c_{1}t.

(10.88)

(iv)

On
\operatorname{Ker}\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
, the conserved energy
E_{\mathrm{full}}
is equivalent to the
\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{full}}
-norm with the explicit constants in (
10.37
).

Proof.

Item (i) is immediate from the definition of
\Pi_{0}
.

For item (ii), let
u
be the solution with data
(u_{0},u_{1})
. Lemma
10.12
gives

\Pi_{0}u(t)=\Pi_{0}u_{0}+t\,\Pi_{0}u_{1},\qquad\Pi_{0}u_{t}(t)=\Pi_{0}u_{1}.

(10.89)

Thus

\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}\mathcal{S}(t)(u_{0},u_{1})=\mathcal{S}(t)\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}(u_{0},u_{1}),

(10.90)

so the projection commutes with the full evolution.

We next prove item (iv). Let
(u,u_{t})\in\operatorname{Ker}\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
. Then
\Pi_{0}u=0
and
\Pi_{0}u_{t}=0
. By Proposition
10.11
,

\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\leq C_{P}\,q[u].

(10.91)

Since
A,\Phi,\Delta_{r},\Delta_{x}
are positive and bounded above and below on the slab, the conserved energy (
10.16
) controls
\|u_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}
and the
L^{2}
-norms of
u_{\phi},u_{r},u_{x}
. Combining this with the weighted Poincaré inequality yields

\displaystyle c\Bigl(\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma)}^{2}+\|u_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq E_{\mathrm{full}}[u](t)\leq C\Bigl(\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma)}^{2}+\|u_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\Bigr)

(10.92)

for all
(u,u_{t})\in\operatorname{Ker}\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
.

Finally, we prove item (iii). The solutions generated by
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
are
1
and
t
, so
\operatorname{Ran}\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
lies in the threshold space. Conversely, let
u(t,\phi,r,x)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}t^{j}\psi_{j}(\phi,r,x)
be a polynomial-in-
t
finite-energy solution. Set

c_{0}:=\Pi_{0}u(0),\qquad c_{1}:=\Pi_{0}u_{t}(0),\qquad v(t):=u(t)-c_{0}-c_{1}t.

(10.93)

Then
v
is still a polynomial solution and, by Lemma
10.12
,

\Pi_{0}v(t)=0,\qquad\Pi_{0}v_{t}(t)=0

(10.94)

for all
t
. The energy estimate proved in item (iv) and conservation of
E_{\mathrm{full}}
imply that

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\|v(t)\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma)}+\|v_{t}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\Bigr)<\infty.

(10.95)

Since
v
is a polynomial in
t
with values in the Hilbert space
H^{1}(\Sigma)
and
v_{t}
is a polynomial in
t
with values in
L^{2}(\Sigma)
, this bound forces all positive powers of
t
in
v
to vanish. Thus
v(t)=v_{0}
and
v_{t}(t)=0
. Substituting this time-independent solution into the weak form of (
10.3
) and testing with
v_{0}
gives
q[v_{0}]=0
. Proposition
10.10
therefore implies that
v_{0}
is constant. Since
\Pi_{0}v_{0}=0
, we get
v_{0}=0
. Hence every threshold solution is affine in
t
, and the full zero-frequency threshold space is exactly
\mathrm{span}\{1,t\}
, equivalently
\operatorname{Ran}\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}=\mathrm{span}\{(1,0),(0,1)\}
in the energy space.
∎

Proposition 10.16

(Direct exclusion of upper-half-plane modes)
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
1.3
, there is no nonzero finite-energy solution of the form

u(t,\phi,r,x)=e^{\lambda t}\psi(\phi,r,x),\qquad\Re\lambda>0.

(10.96)

Equivalently, with the convention
u=e^{-\mathrm{i}\sigma t}\psi
, there is no nonzero mode with
\operatorname{Im}\sigma>0
.

Proof.

For a mode
u=e^{\lambda t}\psi
, the conserved energy has the scaling

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{full}}[u](t)

\displaystyle=e^{2\Re\lambda t}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\left(A|\lambda\psi|^{2}+\Phi|\psi_{\phi}|^{2}+\Delta_{r}|\psi_{r}|^{2}+\Delta_{x}|\psi_{x}|^{2}\right).

(10.97)

Since the energy is constant in
t
and
\Re\lambda>0
, the integral in parentheses must vanish. Positivity of the coefficients gives
\lambda\psi=0
,
\psi_{\phi}=0
,
\psi_{r}=0
, and
\psi_{x}=0
. Since
\lambda\neq 0
, this forces
\psi=0
. The relation with the
e^{-\mathrm{i}\sigma t}
convention follows from
\lambda=-\mathrm{i}\sigma
, so
\Re\lambda=\operatorname{Im}\sigma
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Apply Theorem
1.2
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{full}}

\displaystyle e^{t\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}(t)

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}

\displaystyle=E_{\mathrm{full}}

(10.98)

and
\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
as in Proposition
10.15
. The proposition verifies Assumption
B.1
, so every solution decomposes uniquely into a threshold part and a uniformly bounded threshold-complement part.

By Proposition
10.15
(iii), the threshold part is the affine solution

c_{0}+c_{1}t,\qquad c_{0}=\Pi_{0}u(0),\qquad c_{1}=\Pi_{0}u_{t}(0).

(10.99)

Set

v(t,\phi,r,x):=u(t,\phi,r,x)-c_{0}-c_{1}t.

(10.100)

Then

\Pi_{0}v(t)=0,\qquad\Pi_{0}v_{t}(t)=0

(10.101)

for all
t
, and Proposition
10.15
(iv) together with Theorem
1.2
yields

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\|v(t)\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma)}+\|v_{t}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C\Bigl(\|v(0)\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma)}+\|v_{t}(0)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\Bigr).

(10.102)

This is the claimed boundedness modulo
\mathrm{span}\{1,t\}
.

Uniqueness of the decomposition follows from uniqueness of the abstract threshold splitting and from the explicit identification of the threshold space in Proposition
10.15
(iii).

Finally, Theorem
1.2
rules out nontrivial exponentially growing finite-energy modes on
\operatorname{Ker}\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
, while Proposition
10.15
(iii) shows that the threshold space itself consists only of affine functions of
t
. Hence there is no nontrivial mode

u=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sigma t}\psi(\phi,r,x),\qquad\operatorname{Im}\sigma>0.

(10.103)

This proves the theorem.
∎

11.  Verification of the bounded-slab conditions

This section verifies the abstract conditions used in Theorem
1.2
for the positive-energy Carter slabs. We record first two elementary Hilbert-space lemmas and then apply them to the closed form realization of the full
k=0
equation. The outcome is that the only generalized zero modes are the affine functions
1
and
t
, and the bounded-slab theorem follows directly from the abstract result.

Lemma 11.1

(Weighted Poincaré with an arbitrary positive smooth weight)
.

Let
\Sigma
be a bounded connected Lipschitz domain or product slab and let
W\in L^{\infty}(\Sigma)
satisfy
W\geq W_{-}>0
. Then there is a constant
C_{P,W}
such that

\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\leq C_{P,W}\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}

(11.1)

for every
f\in H^{1}(\Sigma)
satisfying
\int_{\Sigma}Wf=0
.

Proof.

Assume the estimate is false. Then there are
f_{n}\in H^{1}(\Sigma)
with
\int Wf_{n}=0
,
\|f_{n}\|_{L^{2}}=1
, and
\|\nabla f_{n}\|_{L^{2}}\to 0
. The sequence is bounded in
H^{1}
. By Rellich compactness, after passing to a subsequence,
f_{n}\to f_{\infty}
strongly in
L^{2}
and weakly in
H^{1}
. The weak derivative of
f_{\infty}
is zero, hence
f_{\infty}
is constant because
\Sigma
is connected. The weighted mean condition passes to the limit since
W\in L^{\infty}
and
f_{n}\to f_{\infty}
in
L^{2}
, so the constant is zero. This contradicts
\|f_{\infty}\|_{L^{2}}=1
.
∎

Lemma 11.2

(Bounded Hilbert-space polynomials are constant)
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space and let

P(t)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}t^{j}h_{j},\qquad h_{j}\in H.

(11.2)

If
\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\|P(t)\|_{H}<\infty
, then
h_{j}=0
for every
j\geq 1
.

Proof.

Assume that some positive coefficient is nonzero and let
m\geq 1
be the largest index with
h_{m}\neq 0
. Then

t^{-m}P(t)=h_{m}+\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}t^{j-m}h_{j}\longrightarrow h_{m}\quad\text{in }H

(11.3)

as
t\to+\infty
. Hence
\|P(t)\|_{H}\sim t^{m}\|h_{m}\|_{H}
, contradicting boundedness.
∎

Proposition 11.3

(Explicit energy equivalence on the threshold complement)
.

Let
a_{-}
,
a_{+}
,
m_{\nabla}
, and
M_{\nabla}
be the coefficient constants in (
10.34
), and let
C_{P,A}
be the weighted Poincaré constant for the weight
A
. If
(u,v)\in\operatorname{Ker}\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
, then

\displaystyle\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma)}^{2}+\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(C_{P,A}+m_{\nabla}^{-1}+a_{-}^{-1}\right)\,2E_{\mathrm{full}}[u,v],

(11.4)

\displaystyle 2E_{\mathrm{full}}[u,v]

\displaystyle\leq\max\{a_{+},M_{\nabla}\}\left(\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma)}^{2}+\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\right).

(11.5)

Proof.

Since
(u,v)\in\operatorname{Ker}\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
, one has
\int_{\Sigma}Au=0
. Lemma
11.1
gives

\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C_{P,A}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(11.6)

The spatial part of the energy satisfies
q[u,u]\geq m_{\nabla}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
, and the kinetic part satisfies
\int A|v|^{2}\geq a_{-}\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
. Therefore

\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq(C_{P,A}+m_{\nabla}^{-1})q[u,u]+a_{-}^{-1}\int A|v|^{2},

(11.7)

which is bounded by the right side of (
11.4
). The upper bound (
11.5
) follows immediately from
A\leq a_{+}
,
\Phi\leq M_{\nabla}
,
\Delta_{r}\leq M_{\nabla}
, and
\Delta_{x}\leq M_{\nabla}
.
∎

Theorem 11.4

(Closure of the bounded-slab conditions)
.

Assume the full bounded-slab conditions
H1
-
H5
of Assumption
B.2
. Then the full
k=0
Carter slab evolution satisfies Assumption
B.1
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{full}}

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}

\displaystyle=E_{\mathrm{full}}

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}(u_{0},u_{1})

\displaystyle=(\Pi_{0}u_{0},\Pi_{0}u_{1}).

(11.8)

Moreover,

\mathcal{T}_{0}=\mathrm{span}\{(1,0),(0,1)\},

(11.9)

so the corresponding threshold solutions are exactly
c_{0}+c_{1}t
.

Proof.

We prove the three parts of Assumption
B.1
.

First, the evolution is a strongly continuous group. The direct Galerkin construction in Theorem
10.9
solves the weak problem in
V_{\mathrm{full}}\times L^{2}(\Sigma)
, proves uniqueness, conserves
E_{\mathrm{full}}
, and gives strong continuity of the solution map. Uniqueness and time translation give the group law.

Second, the threshold projection is bounded and commutes with the group. Since
A
is smooth and bounded above and below by positive constants, the functional
f\mapsto\int_{\Sigma}Af
is bounded on
L^{2}(\Sigma)
, and hence
\Pi_{0}
is a bounded projection onto constants. Therefore
\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
is bounded on
V_{\mathrm{full}}\times L^{2}(\Sigma)
. Integrating the equation over
\Sigma
, equivalently testing (
10.21
) with
1\in V_{\mathrm{full}}
, gives

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\int_{\Sigma}Au(t)=0.

(11.10)

Thus

\Pi_{0}u(t)=\Pi_{0}u(0)+t\Pi_{0}u_{t}(0),\qquad\Pi_{0}u_{t}(t)=\Pi_{0}u_{t}(0),

(11.11)

which is exactly
\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}\mathcal{S}(t)=\mathcal{S}(t)\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
.

Third, the energy is coercive on the complement. If
(u,v)\in\operatorname{Ker}\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
, then
\Pi_{0}u=0
and
\Pi_{0}v=0
. The weighted Poincaré inequality on the connected bounded slab gives

\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\leq Cq[u,u],\qquad q[u,u]=\int_{\Sigma}(\Phi|u_{\phi}|^{2}+\Delta_{r}|u_{r}|^{2}+\Delta_{x}|u_{x}|^{2}).

(11.12)

Uniform positivity of
A,\Phi,\Delta_{r},\Delta_{x}
then yields constants
c,C>0
such that

\displaystyle c\bigl(\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma)}^{2}+\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 2E_{\mathrm{full}}[u,v]\leq C\bigl(\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma)}^{2}+\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\bigr)

(11.13)

for every
(u,v)\in\operatorname{Ker}\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
.

It remains to identify the full generalized zero-frequency space. The vectors
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
generate the solutions
1
and
t
, so they lie in
\mathcal{T}_{0}
. Conversely, take
U_{0}\in\mathcal{T}_{0}
. Then
\mathcal{G}^{N}U_{0}=0
for some
N
, and therefore

\mathcal{S}(t)U_{0}=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{t^{j}}{j!}\mathcal{G}^{j}U_{0}

(11.14)

is a polynomial in
t
with values in the energy space. Subtract its affine average part, obtaining a polynomial solution
V(t)=(v(t),v_{t}(t))\in\operatorname{Ker}\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
. The coercive estimate just proved and conservation of energy show that
V(t)
is uniformly bounded for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Lemma
11.2
implies that
V(t)
is constant. The weak equation then gives
q[v_{0},\varphi]=0
for every admissible
\varphi
; taking
\varphi=v_{0}
yields
q[v_{0},v_{0}]=0
. By Proposition
10.10
,
v_{0}
is constant. Since
\Pi_{0}v_{0}=0
, it follows that
v_{0}=0
. Thus no further generalized zero mode exists.
∎

Proposition 11.5

(Full bounded-slab theorem from the verified conditions)
.

Under
H1
-
H5
of Assumption
B.2
, the full
\phi
-dependent bounded-slab conclusion of Theorem
1.3
holds.

Proof.

Step 1: closed equation.
By H1 and the conformal computation in Section
5
, the linearized scalar-curvature equation in the conformal sector is
\Box_{g}u=0
. Proposition
5.2
rewrites this equation on the Carter slab as

-Au_{tt}+2Bu_{t\phi}+\Phi u_{\phi\phi}+\partial_{r}(\Delta_{r}u_{r})+\partial_{x}(\Delta_{x}u_{x})=0,

(11.15)

and the rigorous boundary realization is the weak identity

\int_{\Sigma}Au_{tt}\overline{\eta}+q[u,\eta]+\mathfrak{c}[u_{t},\eta]=0,\qquad\eta\in V_{\mathrm{full}}.

(11.16)

Here H4 supplies the closed form domain and H5 fixes the complex sesquilinear convention.

Step 2: well-posedness.
Theorem
10.9
constructs the solution by Galerkin approximation in
V_{\mathrm{full}}\times L^{2}(\Sigma)
. The finite-dimensional mass matrices are positive because
A\geq a_{-}>0
by H3, the stiffness matrices are nonnegative because
\Phi
,
\Delta_{r}
, and
\Delta_{x}
are positive by H2-H3, and the gyroscopic matrices are skew-Hermitian because
B
is independent of
\phi
and the
\phi
-direction is periodic. Passing to the limit gives

u\in C(\mathbb{R};V_{\mathrm{full}}),\qquad u_{t}\in C(\mathbb{R};L^{2}(\Sigma)),

(11.17)

with uniqueness and a strongly continuous group.

Step 3: conserved positive energy.
The weak energy equality, Theorem
10.5
, gives for every
t

E_{\mathrm{full}}[u,u_{t}](t)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}A|u_{t}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}q[u,u]=E_{\mathrm{full}}[u,u_{t}](0).

(11.18)

The skew term has zero real part, so no indefinite mixed contribution appears in the energy. Positivity of
A,\Phi,\Delta_{r},\Delta_{x}
gives the two-sided gradient estimate (
10.34
).

Step 4: exact affine average.
Since
1\in V_{\mathrm{full}}
by H4, testing the weak equation with
\eta=1
is legitimate. Because
q[u,1]=0
and
\mathfrak{c}[u_{t},1]=0
, one obtains

\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\int_{\Sigma}Au(t)=0.

(11.19)

Therefore

\Pi_{0}u(t)=\Pi_{0}u(0)+t\Pi_{0}u_{t}(0),\qquad\Pi_{0}u_{t}(t)=\Pi_{0}u_{t}(0).

(11.20)

This proves the formula for the affine coefficients

c_{0}=\frac{\int_{\Sigma}Au_{0}}{\int_{\Sigma}A},\qquad c_{1}=\frac{\int_{\Sigma}Au_{1}}{\int_{\Sigma}A}.

(11.21)

Step 5: coercivity after removing the affine threshold.
Define
v=u-c_{0}-c_{1}t
. Then
\Pi_{0}v(t)=\Pi_{0}v_{t}(t)=0
for every
t
. Proposition
10.11
gives
\|v(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C_{P}q[v(t),v(t)]
. Combining this with the coefficient lower bounds gives

\|v(t)\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|v_{t}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq c_{\mathrm{low}}^{-1}E_{\mathrm{full}}[v,v_{t}](t),

(11.22)

while the upper coefficient bounds give

E_{\mathrm{full}}[v,v_{t}](0)\leq C_{\mathrm{high}}\left(\|v(0)\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|v_{t}(0)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right).

(11.23)

Energy conservation yields the claimed estimate with the explicit constant

C_{\mathrm{stab}}=(C_{\mathrm{high}}/c_{\mathrm{low}})^{1/2}.

(11.24)

Step 6: uniqueness of the threshold splitting.
Proposition
10.14
identifies the full generalized zero-frequency space of the closed generator as

\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{G}^{2}=\mathrm{span}\{(1,0),(0,1)\},\qquad\bigcup_{N\geq 1}\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{G}^{N}=\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{G}^{2}.

(11.25)

Thus the only polynomial threshold solutions are
1
and
t
, and the decomposition
u=c_{0}+c_{1}t+v
is unique.

Step 7: exclusion of exponentially growing modes.
If
u=e^{\lambda t}\psi
with
\Re\lambda>0
, Proposition
10.16
applies the conserved positive energy to show that
\psi=0
. With the convention
u=e^{-\mathrm{i}\sigma t}\psi
, the condition is
\operatorname{Im}\sigma>0
. This proves every assertion of Theorem
1.3
.
∎

Proposition 11.6

(Separation is not used in the full theorem)
.

The proof of Theorem
1.3
does not use Carter separation, Fourier decomposition in
