\phi
, or self-adjointness of a separated radial or angular operator. It uses only the divergence form (
10.3
), the positivity constants in (
10.34
), the skew identity (
10.23
), the weighted Poincaré inequality, and the affine average law.

Proof.

The energy identity is obtained from (
10.21
) by choosing
\eta=u_{t}
after time mollification in Theorem
10.5
. The mixed term is eliminated by (
10.23
). Coercivity on the complement follows from Proposition
10.11
and (
10.37
). The threshold space is identified by subtracting the affine average and applying Lemma
11.2
to the energy-bounded complement. None of these steps decomposes the solution into modes or invokes the separated ODEs. The separated analysis is used only for the later axisymmetric spectral refinement and for comparison with classical Carter theory.
∎

Proposition 11.7

(Boundary terms in the form realization)
.

Under the closed form-domain hypothesis H4, every nonperiodic boundary term used in the rigorous proof of Theorem
1.3
is encoded by the closed form
q
. No proof step requires an unproved classical trace identity at
r=r_{\pm}
or
x=x_{\pm}
.

Proof.

The weak equation is (
10.9
). Its spatial term is
q[u,\eta]
by definition. The gyroscopic term is integrated by parts only in the periodic
\phi
-variable, where there is no boundary. The average law tests against
1\in V_{\mathrm{full}}
, for which
q[u,1]=0
and
\mathfrak{c}[v,1]=0
. The Poincare and kernel arguments use weak derivatives inside
\Sigma
, not boundary traces. Hence all nonperiodic boundary information is carried by the closed form domain.
∎

12.  Axisymmetric spectral refinement on bounded slabs

12.1.  Reduction to the axisymmetric
k=0
equation

Theorem
1.2
already gives boundedness modulo the full zero-frequency threshold space, and Theorem
1.3
verifies that statement concretely on strictly stationary Carter slabs. We now impose

\partial_{\phi}u=0

(12.1)

for a different reason: in axisymmetry the gyroscopic term disappears identically, the evolution becomes genuinely self-adjoint after weighting by
A
, and one can obtain a more detailed spectral description than the general energy estimate alone provides. Thus Theorem
1.6
is both an axisymmetric special case of Theorem
1.2
and a refinement of it.
Since
k=0
, the conformal master equation (
5.28
) becomes

\displaystyle-A(r,x)\,\partial_{t}^{2}u+\partial_{r}(\Delta_{r}\partial_{r}u)+\partial_{x}(\Delta_{x}\partial_{x}u)

\displaystyle=0,

(12.2)

where

A(r,x):=-\rho^{2}g^{tt}=\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}{\Delta_{r}}-\frac{a^{2}(1-x^{2})^{2}}{\Delta_{x}}.

(12.3)

The coefficient
A
is the only genuinely dynamical quantity needed for the energy method. In the regions relevant to this paper we assume that it is strictly positive.

Definition 12.1

(Regular timelike slab)
.

A
regular timelike slab
is a bounded connected set

\Omega=[r_{-},r_{+}]\times[x_{-},x_{+}]\subset\{(r,x):\rho^{2}>0,\ \Delta_{r}>0,\ \Delta_{x}>0,\ A>0\}

(12.4)

with smooth coefficient functions
A,\Delta_{r},\Delta_{x}
on a neighborhood of
\overline{\Omega}
.

The boundedness assumption is not merely technical. It isolates the local geometric mechanism that comes purely from the coefficients and avoids any ambiguity about horizon fluxes, asymptotically flat ends, or radiative boundary conditions. Later sections explain how one should think about those more global issues in the
k=0
regime.

Reflecting boundary conditions and conserved energy

We call boundary conditions
reflecting
if the boundary term produced by integrating the spatial operator by parts vanishes on the domain of the spatial problem. This includes the familiar Dirichlet and Neumann conditions, as well as a wide class of real Robin conditions and regularity conditions at smooth endpoints. For the main theorem we are especially interested in reflecting conditions that admit the constant function
1
.

Theorem 12.2

(Conserved axisymmetric energy)
.

Let
u
be a sufficiently regular axisymmetric solution of (
12.2
) on
[0,T]\times\Omega
, where
\Omega
is a regular timelike slab and the boundary conditions are reflecting. Then

\displaystyle E_{0}[u](t)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\left(A|u_{t}|^{2}+\Delta_{r}|u_{r}|^{2}+\Delta_{x}|u_{x}|^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x

(12.5)

is independent of
t
.

Proof.

Multiply (
12.2
) by
u_{t}
. Since
A,\Delta_{r},\Delta_{x}
are independent of
t
,

\displaystyle-Au_{tt}u_{t}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}(Au_{t}^{2}),

\displaystyle\partial_{r}(\Delta_{r}u_{r})u_{t}

\displaystyle=\partial_{r}(\Delta_{r}u_{r}u_{t})-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}(\Delta_{r}u_{r}^{2}),

\displaystyle\partial_{x}(\Delta_{x}u_{x})u_{t}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}(\Delta_{x}u_{x}u_{t})-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}(\Delta_{x}u_{x}^{2}).

(12.6)

Integration over
\Omega
gives

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}E_{0}[u](t)=\int_{\partial\Omega}\mathcal{F}[u],

(12.7)

where
\mathcal{F}[u]
is the boundary flux. By the reflecting hypothesis the boundary flux vanishes. Hence
E_{0}[u](t)
is conserved.
∎

The crucial point is that
E_{0}
is nonnegative but not coercive on the full
H^{1}\times L^{2}
space. The energy controls
u_{t}
,
u_{r}
, and
u_{x}
, but not the
L^{2}
-size of
u
itself. That loss of coercivity is what makes the
k=0
problem a borderline case.

12.2.  The spatial quadratic form

We define the spatial quadratic form

q_{0}[u]:=\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta_{r}|u_{r}|^{2}+\Delta_{x}|u_{x}|^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x.

(12.8)

The axisymmetric equation may be written as

\displaystyle Au_{tt}+\mathcal{H}_{0}u=0

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{0}:=-\partial_{r}(\Delta_{r}\partial_{r})-\partial_{x}(\Delta_{x}\partial_{x})

(12.9)

and
q_{0}
is the closed quadratic form associated with
\mathcal{H}_{0}
.

The natural Hilbert space is the weighted space

L_{A}^{2}(\Omega),\qquad\langle f,g\rangle_{A}:=\int_{\Omega}Afg\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x,

(12.10)

with norm
\|f\|_{A}^{2}=\langle f,f\rangle_{A}
. Since
A
is positive and bounded above and below on the bounded slab,
L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)
is equivalent as a Banach space to the usual
L^{2}(\Omega)
, but the weighted formulation is the one naturally adapted to self-adjointness.

Proposition 12.3

(Self-adjoint spatial operator)
.

Let
\Omega
be a regular timelike slab with reflecting boundary conditions. Then the quadratic form
q_{0}
is densely defined, closed, and nonnegative on
L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)
. It therefore defines a unique nonnegative self-adjoint operator
L_{0}
on
L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)
satisfying

q_{0}[u,v]=\langle L_{0}u,v\rangle_{A}

(12.11)

for
u
in the operator domain of
L_{0}
and
v
in the form domain. Equivalently,

L_{0}=A^{-1}\mathcal{H}_{0}

(12.12)

with domain determined by the reflecting boundary conditions.

Proof.

Because
\Omega
is bounded and
A,\Delta_{r},\Delta_{x}
are smooth and strictly positive, the form

u\mapsto\|u\|_{A}^{2}+q_{0}[u]

(12.13)

is equivalent to the
H^{1}
-norm. The reflecting boundary conditions ensure that the spatial integration-by-parts identity has no boundary contribution, hence the form is symmetric. Nonnegativity is immediate from (
12.8
). Closedness follows from the
H^{1}
-equivalence. The Friedrichs representation theorem then yields the self-adjoint operator
L_{0}
.
∎

Kernel and weighted Poincaré inequality

The lack of coercivity is entirely concentrated in the kernel of
L_{0}
.

Proposition 12.4

(Kernel of the bounded-slab operator)
.

Assume that
\Omega
is connected and that the reflecting boundary conditions admit the constant function. Then

\operatorname{Ker}L_{0}=\mathrm{span}\{1\}.

(12.14)

Proof.

Since the constant function has vanishing derivatives, it belongs to the kernel. Conversely, if
u\in\operatorname{Ker}L_{0}
, then

0=\langle L_{0}u,u\rangle_{A}=q_{0}[u]=\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta_{r}|u_{r}|^{2}+\Delta_{x}|u_{x}|^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x.

(12.15)

Because
\Delta_{r}
and
\Delta_{x}
are strictly positive on
\Omega
, we conclude that
u_{r}=u_{x}=0
almost everywhere. Since
\Omega
is connected,
u
is constant.
∎

Let

\Pi_{0}f:=\frac{\langle f,1\rangle_{A}}{\langle 1,1\rangle_{A}}\,1

(12.16)

be the
L_{A}^{2}
-orthogonal projection onto the constant function. On the orthogonal complement of
\Pi_{0}
, the quadratic form becomes coercive.

Proposition 12.5

(Weighted Poincaré inequality)
.

There exists
C_{P}>0
such that

\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq C_{P}\,q_{0}[u]

(12.17)

for every
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
satisfying
\Pi_{0}u=0
.

Proof.

Since
A
is bounded above and below by positive constants, the condition
\Pi_{0}u=0
is equivalent to the vanishing of a weighted mean. On a bounded connected domain all such mean-zero conditions yield a Poincaré inequality equivalent to the standard one. Because
q_{0}[u]
is equivalent to
\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
on
\Omega
, the result follows.
∎

This proposition is the precise analytic manifestation of the statement that the only obstruction to coercivity is the constant mode. Once that mode is removed, the conserved energy controls the full
H^{1}\times L^{2}
norm.

Well-posedness and higher regularity

The abstract wave equation

u_{tt}+L_{0}u=0

(12.18)

on
L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)
is a standard nonnegative self-adjoint wave equation. Consequently the bounded-slab axisymmetric problem is globally well posed.

Theorem 12.6

(Well-posedness on bounded slabs)
.

Let
\Omega
be a regular timelike slab with reflecting boundary conditions. For every initial datum

(u_{0},u_{1})\in H^{1}(\Omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega)

(12.19)

compatible with the boundary conditions, there exists a unique weak solution of (
12.2
) such that

u\in C(\mathbb{R};H^{1}(\Omega)),\qquad u_{t}\in C(\mathbb{R};L^{2}(\Omega)).

(12.20)

Moreover
E_{0}[u](t)
is conserved for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and the solution map depends continuously on the data.

Proof.

Appendix
D
constructs the compact resolvent of
L_{0}
directly and produces an
L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)
-orthonormal eigenbasis
\{\psi_{j}\}_{j\geq 0}
with eigenvalues
0=\lambda_{0}\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\cdots\nearrow+\infty
. Expanding the data in that basis yields the explicit solution formula

\displaystyle u(t)

\displaystyle=a_{0}\psi_{0}+b_{0}t\,\psi_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(a_{j}\cos(\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}t)+b_{j}\frac{\sin(\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}t)}{\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}\right)\psi_{j},

(12.21)

which converges in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{1}(\Omega))\cap C^{1}(\mathbb{R};L^{2}(\Omega))
. Termwise differentiation shows that the series solves (
12.2
), and the conservation of
E_{0}
follows mode by mode. Uniqueness is obtained by applying Theorem
12.2
to the difference of two solutions.
∎

Proposition 12.7

(Graph-norm higher regularity)
.

Let
m\geq 0
be an integer. If

(u_{0},u_{1})\in\operatorname{Dom}((1+L_{0})^{(m+1)/2})\times\operatorname{Dom}((1+L_{0})^{m/2}),

(12.22)

then the solution of (
12.2
) satisfies

u\in C\bigl(\mathbb{R};\operatorname{Dom}((1+L_{0})^{(m+1)/2})\bigr),\qquad u_{t}\in C\bigl(\mathbb{R};\operatorname{Dom}((1+L_{0})^{m/2})\bigr).

(12.23)

If, in addition, the chosen reflecting realization has the elliptic regularity and compatibility properties identifying these graph domains with the corresponding Sobolev spaces, then the usual Sobolev statement follows. Without that extra smooth-boundary regularity input, the graph-norm statement is the theorem-level assertion.

Proof.

Use the spectral resolution from Appendix
D
. Writing

\displaystyle u(t)

\displaystyle=a_{0}\psi_{0}+b_{0}t\psi_{0}+\sum_{j\geq 1}\left(a_{j}\cos(\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}t)+b_{j}\frac{\sin(\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}t)}{\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}\right)\psi_{j},

(12.24)

the graph-norm assumptions are exactly

\sum_{j}(1+\lambda_{j})^{m+1}|a_{j}|^{2}<\infty,\qquad\sum_{j}(1+\lambda_{j})^{m}|b_{j}|^{2}<\infty.

(12.25)

The displayed solution formula preserves these weighted square sums uniformly on compact time intervals, and the differentiated formula preserves the second weighted square sum. This proves the graph-domain continuity. The final Sobolev statement is a corollary only under the additional elliptic regularity and boundary compatibility assumptions stated there.
∎

13.  Mode analysis of the scalar wave operator

13.1.  Separated axisymmetric modes

For the bounded-slab axisymmetric problem, a separated mode of time frequency
\sigma\in\mathbb{C}
has the form

u(t,r,x)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sigma t}\psi(r,x).

(13.1)

Substituting this into (
12.2
) gives

\mathcal{H}_{0}\psi=\sigma^{2}A\psi,\qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad L_{0}\psi=\sigma^{2}\psi.

(13.2)

Thus the time-frequency analysis reduces to the spectral analysis of the nonnegative self-adjoint operator
L_{0}
.

Proposition 13.1

(Mode stability away from zero)
.

Assume the conditions of Section
12
. Let
\psi
solve (
13.2
) with reflecting boundary conditions. If
\psi\neq 0
, then
\sigma^{2}\in[0,\infty)
. In particular, there are no nontrivial separated modes with
\operatorname{Im}\sigma>0
.

Proof.

Pair (
13.2
) with
\psi
in
L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)
:

q_{0}[\psi]=\langle L_{0}\psi,\psi\rangle_{A}=\sigma^{2}\|\psi\|_{A}^{2}.

(13.3)

The left-hand side is real and nonnegative by Proposition
12.3
. Hence
\sigma^{2}
is real and nonnegative. If
\operatorname{Im}\sigma>0
, then
\sigma^{2}
cannot lie in
[0,\infty)
unless
\psi=0
.
∎

This is the bounded-slab form of mode stability. It is considerably easier than the corresponding statements in black-hole scattering theory because all non-self-adjoint boundary phenomena have been excluded by the reflecting setup.

Zero modes

The zero-frequency equation is

L_{0}\psi=0.

(13.4)

By Proposition
12.4
, the only solutions are constants. We record this as a separate proposition because it is the fundamental algebraic input for the generalized zero-mode analysis.

Proposition 13.2

(Zero modes)
.

On a bounded connected reflecting slab admitting constants,

\operatorname{Ker}L_{0}=\mathrm{span}\{1\}.

(13.5)

Equivalently, the only stationary axisymmetric conformal solutions are constant rescalings of the metric.

Proof.

This is Proposition
12.4
.
∎

The weighted projection
\Pi_{0}
defined in (
12.16
) is therefore the spectral projection onto the zero eigenspace. It follows immediately from the wave equation that

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\langle u(t),1\rangle_{A}=-\langle L_{0}u(t),1\rangle_{A}=0.

(13.6)

Hence the weighted average of any solution is affine in time. This is the first indication that one should expect the generalized zero-mode space to be two-dimensional.

Growing zero modes

Equation (
13.6
) shows that the constant function generates not only a stationary mode but also a linearly growing solution. Indeed, both

u\equiv 1,\qquad u=t

(13.7)

solve (
12.2
). The second solution is the bounded-slab analogue of the growing zero modes that appear in low-frequency analyses of wave-type systems on stationary backgrounds.

Proposition 13.3

(Affine-in-time zero modes)
.

The functions
1
and
t
are linearly independent axisymmetric solutions of (
12.2
). More generally, if
c_{0},c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
, then

u(t,r,x)=c_{0}+c_{1}t

(13.8)

solves the equation and satisfies

E_{0}[u](t)=\frac{1}{2}|c_{1}|^{2}\int_{\Omega}A\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x.

(13.9)

Proof.

Both functions have vanishing spatial derivatives, and their second time derivatives are zero. Thus they solve (
12.2
). The energy formula is obtained by substituting
u_{t}=c_{1}
and
u_{r}=u_{x}=0
into (
12.5
).
∎

This proposition explains why a boundedness theorem cannot hold on the full energy space without qualification. The linearly growing mode
t
has finite conserved energy but unbounded
L^{2}
-size in time.

13.2.  Generalized zero modes

We now prove that the affine family from Proposition
13.3
exhausts all polynomial-in-time solutions. The argument is deliberately elementary: it uses only the self-adjointness and nonnegativity of the spatial operator, the fact that constants are its entire kernel, and the vanishing of the weighted mean on the threshold complement. In particular, no decay theorem and no exterior scattering input is being smuggled into the compact zero-mode classification.

Theorem 13.4

(Classification of generalized zero modes)
.

Let

u(t,r,x)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}t^{j}\psi_{j}(r,x)

(13.10)

be a sufficiently regular axisymmetric solution of (
12.2
) on a bounded connected reflecting slab admitting constants. Then
N\leq 1
and
\psi_{0},\psi_{1}
are constant. In particular, the generalized zero-mode space is exactly

\mathrm{span}\{1,t\}.

(13.11)

Proof.

Rewrite the wave equation as
u_{tt}+L_{0}u=0
. Substituting the polynomial ansatz and comparing coefficients of
t^{j}
yields the recurrence

L_{0}\psi_{j}+(j+2)(j+1)\psi_{j+2}=0,\qquad 0\leq j\leq N,

(13.12)

with the convention
\psi_{N+1}=\psi_{N+2}=0
. In particular,

L_{0}\psi_{N}=0.

(13.13)

By Proposition
13.2
,
\psi_{N}
is constant.

Suppose
N\geq 2
. Taking
j=N-2
in (
13.12
) and pairing with
1
in
L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)
, we obtain

0=\langle L_{0}\psi_{N-2},1\rangle_{A}+N(N-1)\langle\psi_{N},1\rangle_{A}.

(13.14)

Since
L_{0}
is self-adjoint and
L_{0}1=0
, the first term vanishes, hence

N(N-1)\langle\psi_{N},1\rangle_{A}=0.

(13.15)

But
\psi_{N}
is constant, so
\langle\psi_{N},1\rangle_{A}=0
implies
\psi_{N}=0
, contradicting the definition of
N
. Therefore
N\leq 1
.

If
N=1
, then
L_{0}\psi_{1}=0
and
L_{0}\psi_{0}=0
, so both
\psi_{0}
and
\psi_{1}
are constant. If
N=0
, then
\psi_{0}
is constant by the same argument. This proves the theorem.
∎

Remark 13.5
.

The proof uses only self-adjointness, nonnegativity, and the fact that the constant mode is nonorthogonal to itself in the weighted inner product. In particular it is robust under smooth perturbations of the coefficients as long as the bounded-slab geometry and the reflecting boundary conditions are preserved.

Generalized modes at nonzero frequency

For completeness we record the corresponding statement away from zero. Since
L_{0}
is self-adjoint, its eigenspaces are semisimple.

Proposition 13.6

(Absence of Jordan blocks for
\sigma\neq 0
)
.

Let
\sigma\neq 0
. Any generalized eigenspace of
L_{0}
at eigenvalue
\sigma^{2}
coincides with the ordinary eigenspace. Equivalently, any generalized time-frequency solution of the form

u(t)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sigma t}\sum_{j=0}^{N}t^{j}\psi_{j}

(13.16)

with
N\geq 1
is trivial.

Proof.

A self-adjoint operator on a Hilbert space is diagonalizable. Hence every eigenvalue is semisimple and there are no nontrivial Jordan chains. Applied to
L_{0}
, this shows that the only time-harmonic solutions at frequency
\sigma\neq 0
are genuine separated modes
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sigma t}\psi
.
∎

Together, Propositions
13.1
and
13.6
show that the only obstruction to uniform boundedness comes from the zero eigenspace and its associated affine-in-time dynamics.

14.  Structure of the resolvent near zero

Because
\Omega
is bounded and
L_{0}
is a nonnegative self-adjoint second-order operator with compact form embedding, its spectrum is discrete.

Proposition 14.1

(Discrete spectrum)
.

There exists an
L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)
-orthonormal basis
\{\psi_{j}\}_{j\geq 0}
of eigenfunctions of
L_{0}
with

0=\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\cdots\nearrow+\infty,

(14.1)

each eigenvalue repeated according to multiplicity. Moreover
\psi_{0}
may be chosen to be the normalized constant function

\psi_{0}=\frac{1}{\|1\|_{A}}.

(14.2)

Proof.

Compactness of the embedding
H^{1}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)
implies compactness of the resolvent of
L_{0}
. The spectral theorem for compact resolvents then gives the discrete spectrum and an orthonormal eigenbasis. Proposition
12.4
identifies the zero eigenspace and shows that it is one-dimensional.
∎

The key point is the spectral gap

\lambda_{1}>0.

(14.3)

This is equivalent to the weighted Poincaré inequality from Proposition
12.5
. In particular, on the mean-zero subspace

\mathcal{H}_{\perp}:=(I-\Pi_{0})L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)

(14.4)

the operator
L_{0}
is strictly positive and hence invertible.

Resolvent decomposition

Define the resolvent

R(\sigma):=(L_{0}-\sigma^{2})^{-1},

(14.5)

initially for
\sigma^{2}\notin\operatorname{spec}(L_{0})
. Because zero is a simple eigenvalue, the behavior of
R(\sigma)
near
\sigma=0
can be described explicitly.

Theorem 14.2

(Laurent expansion at zero)
.

There exists
\varepsilon>0
such that for
0<|\sigma|<\varepsilon
,

(L_{0}-\sigma^{2})^{-1}=-\sigma^{-2}\Pi_{0}+R_{\mathrm{reg}}(\sigma),

(14.6)

where
R_{\mathrm{reg}}(\sigma)
is holomorphic in
\sigma
as a bounded operator on
L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)
. Equivalently, the resolvent has a simple pole in the variable
\sigma^{2}
and a double pole in the variable
\sigma
, with residue determined by the projection onto constants.

Proof.

Decompose the Hilbert space as

L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)=\mathrm{span}\{1\}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{\perp}.

(14.7)

On
\mathrm{span}\{1\}
,
L_{0}
acts as zero, hence

(L_{0}-\sigma^{2})^{-1}|_{\mathrm{span}\{1\}}=-\sigma^{-2}\Pi_{0}.

(14.8)

On
\mathcal{H}_{\perp}
, the operator
L_{0}
is strictly positive by (
14.3
). Therefore
L_{0}-\sigma^{2}
is invertible on
\mathcal{H}_{\perp}
for
|\sigma|<\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}/2
, and the inverse depends holomorphically on
\sigma
by the analytic Fredholm theorem or directly by the convergent Neumann series

(L_{0}-\sigma^{2})^{-1}=L_{0}^{-1}(I-\sigma^{2}L_{0}^{-1})^{-1}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sigma^{2m}L_{0}^{-m-1}

(14.9)

on
\mathcal{H}_{\perp}
. Combining the two subspaces yields (
14.6
).
∎

The theorem is the precise operator-theoretic explanation for the appearance of the modes
1
and
t
. A double pole in
\sigma
corresponds, after inverse Fourier transform in time, to an affine function of
t
.

The wave-type resolvent

It is sometimes convenient to work with the wave-type frequency-domain operator

P_{0}(\sigma):=\mathcal{H}_{0}-\sigma^{2}A=A(L_{0}-\sigma^{2}).

(14.10)

Since multiplication by
A
is invertible on
L^{2}(\Omega)
, the previous theorem immediately yields the corresponding expansion for
P_{0}(\sigma)^{-1}
.

Corollary 14.3

(Frequency-domain expansion for the wave operator)
.

For
0<|\sigma|<\varepsilon
,

P_{0}(\sigma)^{-1}=-\sigma^{-2}\Pi_{0}A^{-1}+\widetilde{R}_{\mathrm{reg}}(\sigma),

(14.11)

where
\widetilde{R}_{\mathrm{reg}}(\sigma)
is holomorphic in
\sigma
as an operator on
L^{2}(\Omega)
.

Proof.

Since
P_{0}(\sigma)=A(L_{0}-\sigma^{2})
,

P_{0}(\sigma)^{-1}=(L_{0}-\sigma^{2})^{-1}A^{-1}.

(14.12)

Substitute (
14.6
).
∎

A direct spectral formula

Using the eigenbasis from Proposition
14.1
, the resolvent can be written explicitly as

\displaystyle(L_{0}-\sigma^{2})^{-1}f

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\langle f,\psi_{j}\rangle_{A}}{\lambda_{j}-\sigma^{2}}\,\psi_{j}.

(14.13)

The
j=0
term is the singular piece

-\sigma^{-2}\Pi_{0}f.

(14.14)

All higher modes are regular at
\sigma=0
because
\lambda_{j}>0
for
j\geq 1
.

Formula (
14.13
) is the bounded-slab analogue of the low-frequency resolvent expansions used in far more delicate black-hole scattering theories. Here the geometry is simple enough that the entire zero-frequency structure can be read directly from the spectrum. Later, in the proof of Theorem
1.6
, the same decomposition will lead to the explicit time-domain representation of solutions.

Orthogonal complement and regularized inverse

The projection
\Pi_{0}
allows one to isolate the regular part of the inverse. Define

L_{0,\perp}:=L_{0}|_{\mathcal{H}_{\perp}}.

(14.15)

Then
L_{0,\perp}
is positive self-adjoint and invertible. In particular,

G_{\perp}:=L_{0,\perp}^{-1}

(14.16)

is a bounded operator from
\mathcal{H}_{\perp}
to the domain of
L_{0}
. The operator
G_{\perp}
is the regularized Green operator for the zero-frequency problem. It is the unique solution operator for

L_{0}u=f,\qquad\Pi_{0}u=0,\qquad\Pi_{0}f=0.

(14.17)

This regularized inverse is precisely what replaces coercivity in the
k=0
regime. On the full space
L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)
,
L_{0}
is not invertible because of the constant kernel. On the orthogonal complement, however, it has a bounded inverse and therefore behaves just like a uniformly elliptic positive operator. All boundedness estimates for the dynamical problem will be formulated on this complement.

Interpretation in time

The average-affine law (
13.6
) and the resolvent expansion (
14.6
) encode the same phenomenon. The spectral projection onto the constant mode determines the coefficients

c_{0}=\Pi_{0}u(0),\qquad c_{1}=\Pi_{0}u_{t}(0),

(14.18)

and the remainder lives in
\mathcal{H}_{\perp}
, where the dynamics are oscillatory rather than growing. Thus the true
k=0
neutral-stability statement is not “all solutions are bounded”, but rather “all solutions are bounded modulo the explicit generalized zero-mode space generated by
1
and
t
”.

15.  Proof of the axisymmetric spectral refinement

Let
\{\psi_{j}\}_{j\geq 0}
and
\{\lambda_{j}\}_{j\geq 0}
be the eigenbasis and eigenvalues from Proposition
14.1
, with
\psi_{0}=\|1\|_{A}^{-1}
. Expand the initial data as

u_{0}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}a_{j}\psi_{j},\qquad u_{1}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}b_{j}\psi_{j},

(15.1)

with convergence in
H^{1}(\Omega)
and
L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)
, respectively. The solution of the wave equation then has the explicit form

\displaystyle u(t)

\displaystyle=a_{0}\psi_{0}+b_{0}t\,\psi_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(a_{j}\cos(\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}t)+b_{j}\frac{\sin(\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}t)}{\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}\right)\psi_{j}.

(15.2)

The
j=0
term is precisely the generalized zero-mode contribution, and the sum over
j\geq 1
is purely oscillatory.

Formula (
15.2
) follows directly from the spectral theorem for
L_{0}
. Each eigenspace solves an ordinary differential equation

c_{j}^{\prime\prime}(t)+\lambda_{j}c_{j}(t)=0.

(15.3)

For
j\geq 1
this gives oscillatory behavior; for
j=0
it gives the affine family
a_{0}+b_{0}t
.

Proof of the decomposition statement

We now prove Theorem
1.6
. Set

c_{0}:=\Pi_{0}u(0),\qquad c_{1}:=\Pi_{0}u_{t}(0),\qquad v(t):=(I-\Pi_{0})u(t).

(15.4)

By (
13.6
),

\Pi_{0}u(t)=c_{0}+c_{1}t.

(15.5)

Hence

u(t)=c_{0}+c_{1}t+v(t),

(15.6)

which is the desired decomposition. By construction,

\Pi_{0}v(t)=0,\qquad\Pi_{0}v_{t}(t)=0

(15.7)

for all
t
.

It remains to show that
v
is uniformly bounded in the natural energy norm. Since
v
evolves entirely in the orthogonal complement
\mathcal{H}_{\perp}
, its spectral expansion is

\displaystyle v(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(a_{j}\cos(\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}t)+b_{j}\frac{\sin(\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}t)}{\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}\right)\psi_{j}.

(15.8)

The corresponding energy is

E_{0}[v](t)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(|b_{j}|^{2}+\lambda_{j}|a_{j}|^{2}\right),

(15.9)

which is independent of
t
. By Proposition
12.5
,
q_{0}[v]
controls
\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
, hence

\|v(t)\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|v_{t}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq CE_{0}[v](t)=CE_{0}[v](0)

(15.10)

for all
t
. This proves the uniform boundedness estimate in Theorem
1.6
.

Uniqueness of the decomposition

The decomposition from Theorem
1.6
is unique. Indeed, suppose

u(t)=c_{0}+c_{1}t+v(t)=\tilde{c}_{0}+\tilde{c}_{1}t+\tilde{v}(t)

(15.11)

with
\Pi_{0}v=\Pi_{0}v_{t}=\Pi_{0}\tilde{v}=\Pi_{0}\tilde{v}_{t}=0
. Applying
\Pi_{0}
yields

c_{0}+c_{1}t=\tilde{c}_{0}+\tilde{c}_{1}t

(15.12)

for all
t
, hence
c_{0}=\tilde{c}_{0}
and
c_{1}=\tilde{c}_{1}
. Then
v=\tilde{v}
. Thus the generalized zero-mode part and the bounded oscillatory part are intrinsically defined by the solution.

Proof of mode stability

Item (iv) of Theorem
1.6
is Proposition
13.1
. Nevertheless it is worth restating its consequence in the language of evolution.

Corollary 15.1

(No exponentially growing axisymmetric modes)
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
1.6
, there is no nontrivial axisymmetric solution of the form

u(t,r,x)=\mathrm{e}^{\gamma t}\psi(r,x),\qquad\gamma>0,

(15.13)

satisfying the reflecting boundary conditions.

Proof.

Such a solution corresponds to the separated frequency
\sigma=\mathrm{i}\gamma
, which satisfies
\operatorname{Im}\sigma>0
. Proposition
13.1
therefore forces
\psi=0
.
∎

The simpler case when constants are removed

If the boundary conditions do not admit the constant function, or if one imposes an explicit normalization such as
\Pi_{0}u=0
and
\Pi_{0}u_{t}=0
at
t=0
, then the generalized zero-mode space disappears. In that case the conserved energy becomes fully coercive.

Corollary 15.2

(Coercive boundedness after removing constants)
.

Assume the conditions of Theorem
1.6
. If in addition either

\Pi_{0}u(0)=0,\qquad\Pi_{0}u_{t}(0)=0,

(15.14)

or the boundary conditions eliminate constants from the domain of
L_{0}
, then

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\|u(t)\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}+\|u_{t}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C\Bigl(\|u(0)\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}+\|u_{t}(0)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\Bigr).

(15.15)

Proof.

In the first case
c_{0}=c_{1}=0
, so
u=v
. In the second case
\operatorname{Ker}L_{0}=\{0\}
, and the same Poincare argument applies without projection. The conclusion then follows from the conserved energy.
∎

A unitary reformulation

It is useful to rephrase the proof in semigroup language. On the mean-zero subspace
\mathcal{H}_{\perp}
, the operator
L_{0,\perp}
is positive self-adjoint. Therefore

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(t)

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}\cos(t\sqrt{L_{0,\perp}})&L_{0,\perp}^{-1/2}\sin(t\sqrt{L_{0,\perp}})\\[3.99994pt]
-\sqrt{L_{0,\perp}}\sin(t\sqrt{L_{0,\perp}})&\cos(t\sqrt{L_{0,\perp}})\end{pmatrix}

(15.16)

defines a unitary group on the energy space

H^{1}_{\perp}(\Omega)\times L_{A}^{2}{}_{\perp}(\Omega),

(15.17)

where the subscript
\perp
denotes
L_{A}^{2}
-orthogonality to constants. The entire stability statement of this paper may therefore be summarized as follows:
the
k=0
axisymmetric conformal dynamics are unitary on the orthogonal complement of the explicit two-dimensional generalized zero-mode space
.

This is the precise bounded-slab counterpart of the more global mantra in black-hole stability theory: once one isolates the finite-dimensional space of zero modes and gauge modes, the remaining evolution is controlled by a positive energy and a self-adjoint or approximately self-adjoint dynamics.

End of the proof of Theorem
1.6

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

Items (i)-(iv) of Theorem
1.6
are Proposition
12.3
, Theorem
13.4
, the decomposition and boundedness argument above, and Proposition
13.1
. The uniqueness paragraph above proves that the affine threshold part and the oscillatory complement are intrinsically determined by the solution. The conserved spectral energy and Proposition
12.5
give the claimed uniform estimate on the threshold complement. Corollary
15.1
records the corresponding no-growing-mode statement, and Corollary
15.2
gives the normalized/coercive variant. Thus items (ii)-(iii) are the explicit axisymmetric realization of Theorem
1.2
, while item (i) and the resolvent analysis sharpen that abstract theorem by exploiting self-adjointness. This proves every assertion of Theorem
1.6
.
∎

16.  Axisymmetric black-hole exterior after red-shift and mode stability

The bounded-slab theorem is a compact positive-energy result. This section records the exterior problem and its notation, while Section
19
contains the complete exterior ledger, the concrete verification for the slowly rotating weakly charged Kerr-Newman wall family, the full asymptotically flat Kerr-Newman verification based on scalar-wave stability, and the proof of the black-hole theorem from the verified package. This separation is intentional. The red-shift estimate, the limiting absorption principle, high-frequency propagation, and the zero-frequency expansion are geometry-dependent exterior estimates; they are not formal consequences of the compact bounded-slab argument. Theorem
1.7
is therefore unconditional for the stated Kerr-Newman wall family, conditional on the cited scalar-wave theorem for the full asymptotically flat subextremal Kerr-Newman scalar exterior, and an abstract exterior theorem for any further axisymmetric black-hole exterior for which the same package is later verified. Extremal Kerr-Newman belongs to a separate branch: its horizon is degenerate, so Section
19
proves an Aretakis-type charge and obstruction instead of applying the nondegenerate package.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{\rm ext}
be the domain of outer communications of a stationary axisymmetric member of the
k=0
family, restricted to the axisymmetric sector
\partial_{\phi}u=0
. The radial interval may now have horizon endpoints, noncompact ends, or conformal radial boundaries. The basic evolution is still

\Box_{g}u=0,

(16.1)

but the compact energy proof from Section
10
no longer applies by itself: the Killing energy degenerates at horizons, compact Sobolev embedding is lost at infinity, and real-frequency threshold states can occur.

We write
U=(u,\partial_{t}u)
and denote by
\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}
the closed generator of the axisymmetric exterior evolution on the Hilbert scale
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}
introduced in Assumption
B.3
. The local-energy norm
LE^{s}
contains three pieces: red-shift control near each horizon, a compact interaction-region norm, and the outgoing, dissipative, or reflecting component dictated by the asymptotic boundary condition. The exact norm is part of the exterior package because it depends on the chosen end and on the trapping structure.

For the time-Fourier transformed problem we use the notation

P(\sigma)\widehat{u}(\sigma)=\widehat{F}(\sigma),

(16.2)

where
P(\sigma)
is obtained from
\Box_{g}
after replacing
D_{t}
by
\sigma
. The horizon condition is regular ingoing behavior in a horizon-regular chart. The radial-end condition is the asymptotic condition in
BH2
. In the axisymmetric sector the horizon frequency
\sigma-m\Omega_{h}
reduces to
\sigma
, so the only possible superradiant threshold left in this theorem is the zero-frequency threshold.

Section
19
does four things. First, it states the definitive bounded-slab and exterior hypotheses used by the main results. Second, it records how the exterior package
BH1
-
BH9
is verified rather than assumed in the two Kerr-Newman applications. Third, it proves
BH1
-
BH9
for the slowly rotating weakly charged Kerr-Newman wall exterior by red-shift, mode-stability, nontrapping, limiting-absorption, and zero-frequency arguments. Fourth, it verifies
BH1
-
BH9
for the full subextremal asymptotically flat Kerr-Newman scalar exterior by combining the scalar Kerr-Newman stability theorem with the direct geometry, radiation-domain, and zero-frequency proofs written below, proves the separate extremal Kerr-Newman horizon charge and obstruction theorem, and then proves Theorem
1.7
from the nondegenerate package.

The proof in Section
19
follows the same algebraic skeleton as the compact result,

\text{solution}=\text{threshold part}+\text{controlled dispersive part},

(16.3)

but the analytic tools are different. The finite-dimensional threshold projection is the low-frequency data-space projection specified in
BH8
; when zero is embedded at the edge of the continuous spectrum it should not be confused with an ordinary isolated spectral Riesz projection. The dispersive complement is controlled by red-shift/local-energy estimates, real-axis limiting absorption, high-frequency propagation estimates, and the absence of outgoing upper-half-plane modes. The local decay statement is then a Riemann-Lebesgue consequence of the local absolutely continuous spectral representation supplied by
BH7
.

17.  Radial asymptotics and geometric boundary conditions

When
k=0
, the radial coefficient is

\Delta_{r}(r)=\alpha_{2}r^{2}+\alpha_{1}r+\alpha_{0}.

(17.1)

This is the decisive simplification of the zero-curvature regime. The large-
r
geometry is no longer governed by quartic growth but by a quadratic polynomial whose sign and discriminant determine the available radial ends.

Three basic possibilities occur.

(i)

Kerr-like or weakly confining end:

\alpha_{2}>0
. Then
\Delta_{r}(r)\sim\alpha_{2}r^{2}
for large
r
, and the metric coefficients have the same qualitative scale as in standard Carter geometries.

(ii)

Borderline linear/constant end:

\alpha_{2}=0
. Then the radial structure is determined by the lower-order terms and may lead to a finite interval or an asymptotically cylindrical-type end.

(iii)

Finite outer boundary:

\alpha_{2}<0
. In this case
\Delta_{r}
cannot stay positive for all large
r
, so any regular exterior patch must terminate at a finite outer root.

The bounded-slab theorem of this paper is intentionally formulated so that it is insensitive to which of these cases occurs globally. The theorem uses only positivity of
\Delta_{r}
on the chosen interval. Nonetheless, the classification is useful because it explains why there can be no one-size-fits-all exterior theorem in the
k=0
family without additional geometric conditions.

Large-
r
asymptotics of the radial ODE

For fixed separated parameters
(\sigma,m,\lambda)
, the radial equation is

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\Bigl(\Delta_{r}R^{\prime}\Bigr)+\left[\frac{((r^{2}+a^{2})\sigma-am)^{2}}{\Delta_{r}}-\lambda\right]R

\displaystyle=0.

(17.2)

If
\alpha_{2}>0
, then at large
r

\frac{((r^{2}+a^{2})\sigma-am)^{2}}{\Delta_{r}}=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\alpha_{2}}r^{2}+O(r),

(17.3)

so the leading asymptotics are oscillatory or exponential depending on the sign of
\sigma^{2}/\alpha_{2}
and on the way one chooses a radial phase function. In the Kerr corner
\alpha_{2}=1
, and one recovers the familiar massless-wave large-
r
behavior. If
\alpha_{2}=0
, the asymptotics are more delicate and depend on the lower-order structure of
\Delta_{r}
. These variations are precisely why the present paper isolates the bounded-domain problem before discussing unbounded ends.

Boundary conditions in the
k=0
problem

In the zero-curvature regime, the radial boundary conditions that arise in the analysis fall into four natural classes.

The first class consists of reflecting boundary conditions on bounded intervals. This is the compact, positive-energy setting used in the main theorem. In this case the radial operator is realized through a closed symmetric form, and the resulting problem has the self-adjoint structure needed for the threshold decomposition and energy argument.

The second class consists of regularity conditions at a simple zero of
\Delta_{r}
. These endpoints model horizon-type boundaries. The admissible solutions are selected not by imposing an artificial boundary value, but by requiring regular extension in a horizon-regular chart. Equivalently, in the separated radial equation this selects the appropriate Frobenius branch; this point is analyzed in Section
18
.

The third class consists of decay or radiation conditions at an unbounded Kerr-like end. These conditions belong to the scattering problem rather than to the compact spectral problem. In particular, they remove the spatially constant mode from the finite-energy radiation space, and the zero-frequency analysis must then be formulated in terms of outgoing resolvents rather than compact self-adjoint spectral theory.

The fourth class consists of mixed conditions on truncated exterior regions. These are useful in numerical work and in perturbative approximations to exterior black-hole problems. Typically one combines a regular or horizon-compatible condition at the inner boundary with a reflecting, absorbing, or approximate radiation condition at the outer boundary. Such problems are analytically meaningful, but their stability properties depend on the precise realization and should not be confused with the closed reflecting slab used in the main theorem.

The essential point is that linear stability is never a statement about the differential operator alone. It is always a statement about the operator together with the boundary geometry.

A
k=0
boundary dictionary

For later reference, it is useful to keep the main radial regimes separate.

On a bounded radial interval with reflecting ends, the problem is a compact self-adjoint spectral problem. This is the setting of the exact bounded-slab theorem proved in Sections
12
-
15
. The closed reflecting form provides the correct functional framework, the constant mode is retained, and the threshold space can be identified explicitly.

When an endpoint is a simple zero of
\Delta_{r}
, the radial equation has a horizon-type regular singular point. The correct boundary condition is then not a Dirichlet or Neumann prescription imposed by hand, but regularity in a horizon-adapted coordinate system. In separated variables this distinction appears through the Frobenius exponents, which separate the ingoing and outgoing branches.

At an unbounded radial end with
\alpha_{2}>0
, the geometry has Kerr-like or weakly confining asymptotics. This is no longer a compact spectral problem. Exterior boundedness must be formulated in a radiation or scattering space, and one must impose the appropriate decay or outgoing condition at infinity. In particular, the constant spatial mode is no longer part of the finite-energy radiation space.

If instead the outer radial endpoint is a finite root with
\alpha_{2}<0
, the geometry naturally produces a truncated exterior region. In that case the analysis depends on the physical and functional choice made at the outer boundary. Reflecting, absorbing, or transmissive realizations lead to genuinely different problems, and the stability statement must specify which realization is being used.

Thus the bounded-slab theorem is universal only within the closed reflecting slab framework. Once one passes to an exterior problem, the radial end becomes part of the theorem: the boundary condition, radiation condition, or horizon regularity assumption is not a technical afterthought, but a structural ingredient of the stability theory.

18.  Near-horizon behavior and endpoint regularity

Let
r_{h}
be a simple zero of
\Delta_{r}
. The coefficient of the radial derivative in (
9.1
) then vanishes linearly, so the ODE has a regular singular point. Writing

\Delta_{r}(r)=\kappa_{h}(r-r_{h})+O((r-r_{h})^{2}),

(18.1)

and inserting a Frobenius ansatz
R\sim(r-r_{h})^{\nu}
yields the indicial equation

\kappa_{h}^{2}\nu^{2}+((r_{h}^{2}+a^{2})\Omega-am)^{2}=0,

(18.2)

whence the exponents

\nu_{\pm}=\pm\mathrm{i}\frac{(r_{h}^{2}+a^{2})\Omega-am}{\kappa_{h}}.

(18.3)

These exponents are purely imaginary for real
\Omega
, corresponding to oscillatory behavior in the logarithmic variable
\log|r-r_{h}|
.

The same computation underlies the usual distinction between ingoing and outgoing modes. If one introduces a tortoise-type coordinate
r_{*}
satisfying
\mathrm{d}r_{*}/\mathrm{d}r\sim 1/\Delta_{r}
, then the two local branches become
\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}((r_{h}^{2}+a^{2})\Omega-am)r_{*}}
. Their fluxes have opposite signs. This is the horizon structure one expects from a separated scalar wave equation on a stationary axisymmetric background.

Regularity in horizon-penetrating coordinates

The singular oscillation in the coordinate
r
is only a coordinate effect. Introducing an ingoing coordinate

v=t+r_{*},\qquad\widetilde{\phi}=\phi+\Omega_{h}r_{*},\qquad\Omega_{h}=\frac{a}{r_{h}^{2}+a^{2}},

(18.4)

transforms the ingoing branch into a function smooth in
(v,\widetilde{\phi},r)
, while the outgoing branch remains singular across the future horizon. Thus the geometric boundary condition at a future horizon is regularity of the mode in the ingoing chart, equivalently selection of the ingoing Frobenius exponent.

This observation matters even though the present paper focuses mainly on bounded slabs. It shows that the reduced conformal problem is compatible with the usual causal interpretation of horizon boundary conditions. Any future global theory for Theorem 1 exteriors with horizons should therefore be able to import the standard toolkit of red-shift and horizon-regularity arguments developed for scalar waves on Kerr-type backgrounds.

Angular endpoint behavior

At angular endpoints the behavior depends on whether
\Delta_{x}
stays positive or vanishes. If
\Delta_{x}(x_{\pm})>0
, then the angular equation is regular and there is no singular behavior. If
\Delta_{x}
has a simple zero at an endpoint
x_{\pm}
, then the angular equation develops a regular singular point. In many geometrically regular axisymmetric models one finds local behavior of the form

S(x)\sim(x-x_{\pm})^{|m|/2}\times(\text{smooth}),

(18.5)

which is the familiar polar regularity of azimuthal modes. The exact exponent depends on the chosen angular normalization, but the qualitative point is universal: regularity suppresses the singular branch.

Since the Theorem 1 family is broader than the standard Kerr family, we do not impose a single universal endpoint model. Instead we retain the endpoint convention in
H7
of Assumption
B.2
, which asks only that the full mode be smooth in any regular endpoint chart available in the chosen global realization of the metric. This flexible formulation is sufficient for all separation and energy arguments in the paper.

Compatibility of time-domain and mode-theoretic boundary conditions

One advantage of having both time-domain and separated formulations is that each clarifies the other. Reflecting boundary conditions in the time domain correspond to vanishing separated Wronskian at the relevant endpoint. Ingoing horizon conditions correspond to selecting the Frobenius branch with nonnegative flux into the horizon. Regularity at angular endpoints corresponds to the vanishing of the angular Wronskian. Thus the language of energy flux and the language of mode asymptotics are entirely consistent.

This compatibility is important because many stability questions are naturally formulated in the time domain, while many mode-theoretic questions are most transparent in the separated picture. The reduced conformal problem is analytically tractable precisely because it allows one to move freely between these two viewpoints.

19.  Black-hole package, Kerr-Newman verifications, and exterior proofs

This section is the main-body verification and assembly ledger for the exterior results. The formal bounded-slab hypotheses, the black-hole package
BH1
-
BH9
, the full Kerr-Newman external scalar scattering package, the Aretakis input, and the mutually exclusive Kerr-Newman regimes are collected in Appendix
B
. The subsections below do not restate that list; they verify it for the finite-wall family, invoke External Assumption
B.5
for the full subextremal radiation problem, prove the extremal horizon-charge obstruction, and then assemble the exterior main theorems. The wall verification is restricted to a subphoton finite collar; the full asymptotically flat verification is restricted to the neutral axisymmetric scalar sector; the extremal result is an obstruction theorem rather than a nondegenerate decay theorem.

19.1.  Axis regularity for the Kerr-Newman spherical variables

The Kerr-Newman applications use the true rotational axes
\theta=0,\pi
. The bounded Carter slab theorem avoids axes by assuming
\Delta_{x}>0
in an interior product patch, but the black-hole exterior applications are spherical and must use the regular axis domain. We record the exact functional convention here so that no hidden boundary term at the poles is used later.

Definition 19.1

(Axisymmetric Sobolev space on the sphere)
.

For an integer
s\geq 0
, define

H^{s}_{\rm ax}(\mathbb{S}^{2}):=\{f\in H^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{2}):\partial_{\phi}f=0\}.

(19.1)

Equivalently,
f\in H^{s}_{\rm ax}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
is represented by a function of
\theta
only whose pullback to the smooth sphere is an
H^{s}
-function. The first-order norm is equivalent to

\|f\|_{H^{1}_{\rm ax}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}\simeq\int_{0}^{\pi}\bigl(|f(\theta)|^{2}+|\partial_{\theta}f(\theta)|^{2}\bigr)\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta.

(19.2)

Lemma 19.2

(No pole boundary term for regular axisymmetric functions)
.

If
u,v\in H^{1}_{\rm ax}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
and

\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{\rm ax}u:=\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\,\partial_{\theta}u)

(19.3)

is interpreted distributionally, then

\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{\rm ax}u\,\overline{v}\,\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta=-\int_{0}^{\pi}\partial_{\theta}u\,\overline{\partial_{\theta}v}\,\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta

(19.4)

whenever the left side is defined by duality. In particular, smooth axisymmetric functions on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
satisfy

\lim_{\theta\downarrow 0}\sin\theta\,\partial_{\theta}u(\theta)=\lim_{\theta\uparrow\pi}\sin\theta\,\partial_{\theta}u(\theta)=0,

(19.5)

and no boundary term is present at either pole.

Proof.

For smooth axisymmetric functions on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, the identity is the global Green identity for the Laplace-Beltrami operator on the compact boundaryless manifold
\mathbb{S}^{2}
:

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}u\,\overline{v}\,\mathrm{d}\omega=-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\nabla_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}u\cdot\overline{\nabla_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}v}\,\mathrm{d}\omega.

(19.6)

Restricting to
\partial_{\phi}u=\partial_{\phi}v=0
gives (
19.4
). The boundary expression
\sin\theta\,\partial_{\theta}u
must therefore have zero endpoint contribution. Smoothness in Cartesian coordinates near each pole gives
u(\theta)=u(0)+O(\theta^{2})
near
0
and
u(\theta)=u(\pi)+O((\pi-\theta)^{2})
near
\pi
, so the displayed limits vanish separately. For
H^{1}_{\rm ax}
functions, approximate by smooth axisymmetric functions using spherical convolution, or equivalently truncate the spherical-harmonic expansion to the zonal harmonics
Y_{\ell 0}
. Passing to the limit in the
H^{1}
-pairing proves the distributional identity.
∎

Lemma 19.3

(Legendre decomposition of the regular axisymmetric angular operator)
.

The self-adjoint realization of
-\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{\rm ax}
on
L^{2}((0,\pi),\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta)
with the regular-axis domain has eigenfunctions
P_{\ell}(\cos\theta)
,
\ell\geq 0
, and eigenvalues
\ell(\ell+1)
. Thus every regular axisymmetric finite-energy function has the expansion

f(\theta)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}f_{\ell}P_{\ell}(\cos\theta),

(19.7)

with convergence in the corresponding Sobolev space.

Proof.

The full spherical Laplacian has eigenfunctions
Y_{\ell m}
with eigenvalues
\ell(\ell+1)
. The axisymmetric subspace is the closed invariant subspace
m=0
, and
Y_{\ell 0}
is a constant multiple of
P_{\ell}(\cos\theta)
. The spectral theorem on the compact smooth sphere gives the expansion and the Sobolev convergence. Lemma
19.2
identifies this spectral realization with the one-dimensional regular-axis Sturm-Liouville expression.
∎

19.2.  Kerr and Reissner-Nordström as principal exterior applications

This subsection promotes the two classical one-parameter black-hole families to main applications. The point of doing so is not to reprove the full scalar scattering theory for Kerr or Reissner-Nordström. Instead, we isolate the exact conformal-sector consequences of those scalar theories, verify the zero-frequency threshold statement directly, and then apply the abstract exterior theorem. The Schwarzschild endpoint is deliberately not placed here; it is treated in Appendix
A
.

For Kerr we write

g_{M,a}=g_{M,a,0},\qquad\Delta_{\rm Kerr}=r^{2}-2Mr+a^{2},\qquad r_{\pm}=M\pm\sqrt{M^{2}-a^{2}}.

(19.8)

The subextremal genuine Kerr range used for the main theorem is
0<|a|<M
. The scalar problem in this paper is the neutral axisymmetric conformal scalar,

\Box_{g_{M,a}}u=0,\qquad\partial_{\phi}u=0,

(19.9)

and
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm Kerr}
denotes the decaying red-shift/radiation Hilbert scale obtained from
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{{\rm KN},{\rm af}}
by setting
Q=0
.

For Reissner-Nordström we write

\displaystyle g_{M,Q}

\displaystyle=-D(r)\mathrm{d}t^{2}+D(r)^{-1}\mathrm{d}r^{2}+r^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega^{2},

(19.10)

\displaystyle D(r)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{2M}{r}+\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}},\qquad r_{\pm}=M\pm\sqrt{M^{2}-Q^{2}}.

The genuine charged subextremal range is
0<|Q|<M
. Since the background is spherically symmetric and the scalar is neutral, the angular modes decouple and no axial symmetry restriction is needed in the Reissner-Nordström statement. The space
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm RN}
is the usual decaying red-shift/radiation energy space with a nondegenerate horizon norm and an asymptotically flat Hardy term at infinity.

Theorem 19.4

(Verification of the exterior package for subextremal Kerr and Reissner-Nordström)
.

Assume External Assumption
B.4
. Then the following hold.

(a)

For every compact genuine subextremal Kerr parameter set
\mathscr{K}^{\rm Kerr}_{\delta,M_{0},M_{1},a_{*}}
, the neutral axisymmetric conformal scalar on Kerr satisfies Assumption
B.3
on
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm Kerr}
for all
s\geq s_{\rm Kerr}
. The zero-frequency threshold in the decaying radiation space is trivial:

\mathcal{T}_{0,{\rm Kerr}}=\{0\},\qquad\Pi_{0,{\rm Kerr}}=0.

(b)

For every compact genuinely charged subextremal Reissner-Nordström parameter set
\mathscr{K}^{\rm RN}_{\delta,M_{0},M_{1},Q_{*}}
, the neutral scalar wave on Reissner-Nordström satisfies the same black-hole package, with the same threshold conclusion,

\mathcal{T}_{0,{\rm RN}}=\{0\},\qquad\Pi_{0,{\rm RN}}=0.

In the Reissner-Nordström case the statement holds after summing over all spherical harmonics, not merely in the axisymmetric sector.

Proof.

For Kerr,
|a|<M
gives a simple event-horizon root and positive surface gravity

\kappa_{+}=\frac{r_{+}-r_{-}}{2(r_{+}^{2}+a^{2})}>0.

(19.11)

The future horizon is smooth in ingoing Kerr coordinates, and the outgoing end is the standard asymptotically flat radiation end. Thus
BH1
-
BH3
and
BH9
are the same horizon-domain and cutoff statements as in Lemma
19.16
, specialized to
Q=0
. The red-shift, integrated-local-energy, high-frequency, limiting-absorption, smoothing, spectral-density, and mode-stability pieces are supplied by the Kerr part of External Assumption
B.4
. This verifies
BH4
-
BH7
.

It remains to identify zero frequency. At
\sigma=0
, regular axisymmetric separation gives

\bigl(\Delta_{\rm Kerr}R_{\ell}^{\prime}\bigr)^{\prime}-\ell(\ell+1)R_{\ell}=0.

(19.12)

With
\alpha=\sqrt{M^{2}-a^{2}}
and
x=(r-M)/\alpha
, this is the Legendre equation

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\left((x^{2}-1)\frac{\mathrm{d}R_{\ell}}{\mathrm{d}x}\right)-\ell(\ell+1)R_{\ell}=0.

(19.13)

The
Q_{\ell}
-branch is logarithmically singular at the horizon, while the regular
P_{\ell}
-branch grows like
x^{\ell}
for
\ell\geq 1
; the
\ell=0
regular branch is constant. The decaying radiation energy at infinity excludes both the growing branches and the nonzero constant. Hence no zero state remains. The same integration or parameter-differentiation argument used in Lemma
19.18
excludes a regular generalized zero state. Thus
BH8
holds with
\Pi_{0,{\rm Kerr}}=0
.

For Reissner-Nordström the proof is identical but simpler. The metric (
19.10
) has positive surface gravity
\kappa_{+}=(r_{+}-r_{-})/(2r_{+}^{2})
in the subextremal range. The external Reissner-Nordström scalar package gives the red-shift, local-energy, limiting-absorption, smoothing, spectral-density, and mode-stability estimates. At
\sigma=0
, the separated equation for every spherical harmonic is

\bigl(\Delta_{\rm RN}R_{\ell}^{\prime}\bigr)^{\prime}-\ell(\ell+1)R_{\ell}=0,\qquad\Delta_{\rm RN}=r^{2}-2Mr+Q^{2}.

(19.14)

The Legendre classification with
\alpha=\sqrt{M^{2}-Q^{2}}
again leaves only a horizon-regular growing branch for
\ell\geq 1
and a horizon-regular constant for
\ell=0
, all excluded by the decaying radiation energy at infinity. Since the spherical harmonics diagonalize the angular operator, the conclusion sums over all angular modes. Therefore the Reissner-Nordström package holds with trivial zero projection.
∎

19.3.  Concrete slowly rotating weakly charged Kerr-Newman wall exterior

We next verify the same black-hole package for a genuinely rotating and charged family. The verification is intentionally made for a finite wall exterior below the photon region. This is the largest Kerr-Newman statement that is proved here without importing the full asymptotically flat Kerr-Newman scattering theory: the horizon is real and nondegenerate, the metric is genuinely rotating when
a\neq 0
, the metric is genuinely charged when
Q\neq 0
, and every item
BH1
-
BH9
is checked inside this section for the neutral axisymmetric conformal scalar.

Fix
M>0
and

2M<R_{\rm w}<\frac{8M}{3}.

(19.15)

There is a number
\varepsilon_{\rm KN}=\varepsilon_{\rm KN}(M,R_{\rm w})>0
such that the following argument applies whenever

|a|+|Q|\leq\varepsilon_{\rm KN}M,\qquad a^{2}+Q^{2}<M^{2}.

(19.16)

Set

\Delta=(r-r_{+})(r-r_{-})=r^{2}-2Mr+a^{2}+Q^{2},\qquad r_{\pm}=M\pm\sqrt{M^{2}-a^{2}-Q^{2}},

(19.17)

\Sigma=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta,\qquad\mathcal{A}=(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-a^{2}\Delta\sin^{2}\theta.

(19.18)

For
\varepsilon_{\rm KN}
small,
r_{+}<2M<R_{\rm w}
, and the horizon is uniformly subextremal. The standard Kerr-Newman metric is

\displaystyle g_{M,a,Q}={}

\displaystyle-\frac{\Delta-a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}t^{2}-\frac{2a(2Mr-Q^{2})\sin^{2}\theta}{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\phi+\frac{\Sigma}{\Delta}\,\mathrm{d}r^{2}+\Sigma\,\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}

(19.19)

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathcal{A}\sin^{2}\theta}{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}\phi^{2}.

It has scalar curvature zero, and it is the
k=0
Einstein-Maxwell Carter member. The scalar field in this paper is neutral; the charge
Q
enters only through the background metric. We restrict to
\partial_{\phi}u=0
. For axisymmetric functions the wave equation reduces exactly to

\Sigma\Box_{g_{M,a,Q}}u=-A_{\rm KN}\partial_{t}^{2}u+\partial_{r}(\Delta\partial_{r}u)+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}u),

(19.20)

where

A_{\rm KN}(r,\theta)=\frac{\mathcal{A}}{\Delta}=\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}{\Delta}-a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta>0\qquad(r>r_{+}).

(19.21)

The absence of the mixed
t
-
\phi
term in (
19.20
) is the analytic reason why this section can avoid superradiance: the horizon frequency is
\sigma-m\Omega_{H}=\sigma
because
m=0
.

Proposition 19.5

(Axisymmetric regularity at the Kerr-Newman axis)
.

Let

H^{s}_{\rm ax}(S^{2})=\{f\in H^{s}(S^{2}):\partial_{\phi}f=0\}.

(19.22)

If
u,v\in H^{1}_{\rm ax}(S^{2})
and
u
is additionally smooth for the displayed integration-by-parts identity, then

\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\,\partial_{\theta}u)\,\overline{v}\,\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta=-\int_{0}^{\pi}\partial_{\theta}u\,\overline{\partial_{\theta}v}\,\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta.

(19.23)

No boundary term is present at
\theta=0,\pi
. Equivalently, the axisymmetric angular operator

-\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta})

(19.24)

is the Friedrichs self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}((0,\pi),\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta)
whose eigenfunctions are the axisymmetric spherical harmonics
Y_{\ell 0}
.

Proof.

For smooth axisymmetric functions on
S^{2}
, regularity at the poles means that
u(\theta)=U(\cos\theta)
, with
U
smooth near
x=\pm 1
. Hence

\sin\theta\,\partial_{\theta}u=-\sin^{2}\theta\,U^{\prime}(\cos\theta)\to 0\qquad(\theta\to 0,\pi).

(19.25)

Thus the boundary term in the one-dimensional integration by parts vanishes and (
19.23
) follows for smooth functions. Density of smooth axisymmetric functions in
H^{1}_{\rm ax}(S^{2})
extends the identity to the Friedrichs form domain. In the variable
x=\cos\theta
, the operator becomes
-\partial_{x}((1-x^{2})\partial_{x})
, whose regular eigenfunctions are the Legendre polynomials, equivalently the
Y_{\ell 0}
. This proves the proposition.
∎

Let

\frac{\mathrm{d}r_{*}}{\mathrm{d}r}=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta},\qquad\frac{\mathrm{d}\phi_{*}}{\mathrm{d}r}=\frac{a}{\Delta},\qquad v=t+r_{*},\qquad\varphi=\phi+\phi_{*},\qquad\tau=v-r.

(19.26)

The coordinates
(v,r,\theta,\varphi)
are smooth at the future horizon;
\partial_{\tau}=\partial_{t}
. The horizon angular velocity and surface gravity are

\Omega_{H}=\frac{a}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},\qquad\kappa_{H}=\frac{r_{+}-r_{-}}{2(r_{+}^{2}+a^{2})}>0.

(19.27)

We work on the future collar

\mathcal{M}_{M,a,Q,R_{\rm w}}=\{\tau\geq 0,\\
r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm w},\\
(\theta,\varphi)\in S^{2}\}.

(19.28)

At
r=r_{+}
we impose only smoothness in the future-horizon chart. At the wall we impose the geometric Neumann condition associated with the conormal
\mathrm{d}r
:

\mathcal{N}_{\rm w}u:=\partial_{r}u\big|_{t,\theta,\phi}=0\qquad(r=R_{\rm w}).

(19.29)

Equivalently, if
H(r)=r_{*}(r)-r
, then in the
\tau
-chart

\mathcal{N}_{\rm w}u=(\partial_{r}+H^{\prime}(R_{\rm w})\partial_{\tau})u=0,\qquad H^{\prime}(r)=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}-1=\frac{2Mr-Q^{2}}{\Delta}.

(19.30)

This wall condition is reflecting for the stationary energy current. Indeed, the wall flux is a positive multiple of
\Re(\partial_{t}u\,\overline{\partial_{r}u})
, hence vanishes under (
19.29
).

Lemma 19.6

(Kerr-Newman geometry, horizon, wall, and energy scale)
.

For
\varepsilon_{\rm KN}
sufficiently small, the Kerr-Newman wall family satisfies
BH1
-
BH3
. The future horizon is nondegenerate, the wave operator is smooth in the ingoing chart, the wall condition (
19.29
) is closed and reflecting, and the axisymmetric Cauchy problem has a closed generator
\mathcal{G}_{\rm KN}
on a Sobolev red-shift energy scale
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm KN}
.

Proof.

The root
r=r_{+}
is simple because
r_{+}-r_{-}>0
, and (
19.27
) gives
\kappa_{H}>0
. Substitution of (
19.26
) into (
19.19
) removes every factor
\Delta^{-1}
from the metric components, so the coefficients of
\Box_{g_{M,a,Q}}
are smooth at
r=r_{+}
. The horizon generator is
K_{H}=\partial_{v}+\Omega_{H}\partial_{\varphi}
; on axisymmetric functions this is just
\partial_{v}
.

The red-shift multiplier used here is written near the horizon as

N=K_{H}+\chi_{H}(r)Y,

(19.31)

where
Y
is future-directed and transverse to the horizon. Since
\kappa_{H}>0
, a direct computation in the smooth frame
\{K_{H},Y,\partial_{\theta},(\sin\theta)^{-1}\partial_{\varphi}\}
gives

Q_{\mu\nu}[u]\nabla^{(\mu}N^{\nu)}\geq c_{H}\left(|K_{H}u|^{2}+|Yu|^{2}+|\nabla_{S^{2}}u|^{2}\right)

(19.32)

inside a smaller horizon collar. Here
Q_{\mu\nu}
is the scalar stress tensor. Away from the horizon the positive energy coming from (
19.20
) is equivalent to

\int\left(A_{\rm KN}|\partial_{t}u|^{2}+\Delta|\partial_{r}u|^{2}+|\partial_{\theta}u|^{2}\right)\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}\theta.

(19.33)

Combining this with the red-shift energy in the horizon collar defines
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm KN}
after commuting with
\partial_{\tau}
and angular derivatives.

The wall is timelike because
\Delta(R_{\rm w})>0
. The trace map on
r=R_{\rm w}
is continuous, so the homogeneous condition (
19.29
) defines a closed form domain. Smooth axisymmetric functions satisfying the compatibility conditions are dense by ordinary mollification in the regular horizon chart, spherical smoothing at
\theta=0,\pi
, and reflection in a wall collar using the boundary operator (
19.30
). Energy estimates for a normally hyperbolic equation on a compact collar with one future outflow horizon and one reflecting wall give a closed first-order generator and a strongly continuous future semigroup. This proves
BH1
-
BH3
.
∎

Lemma 19.7

(Subphoton nontrapping for the axisymmetric principal symbol)
.

For
\varepsilon_{\rm KN}
sufficiently small, the axisymmetric null bicharacteristics of the principal symbol

p_{\rm KN}=\Delta\xi_{r}^{2}+\xi_{\theta}^{2}-A_{\rm KN}(r,\theta)\tau^{2}

(19.34)

are nontrapping in
r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm w}
. More precisely,

\partial_{r}A_{\rm KN}(r,\theta)<0\qquad(r_{+}<r\leq R_{\rm w})

(19.35)

for all
\theta
.

Proof.

Since

A_{\rm KN}=\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}{\Delta}-a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta,

(19.36)

its radial derivative is independent of the last term and equals

\partial_{r}A_{\rm KN}=\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})\bigl(4r\Delta-(r^{2}+a^{2})\Delta^{\prime}\bigr)}{\Delta^{2}},

(19.37)

where the numerator factor satisfies the identity

4r\Delta-(r^{2}+a^{2})\Delta^{\prime}=2\left(r^{2}(r-3M)+a^{2}(r+M)+2Q^{2}r\right).

(19.38)

On
[r_{+},R_{\rm w}]
, the Schwarzschild part
2r^{2}(r-3M)
is bounded above by a negative constant depending on
M
and
R_{\rm w}<8M/3
. The terms containing
a^{2}
and
Q^{2}
are
O(\varepsilon_{\rm KN}^{2}M^{3})
. Reducing
\varepsilon_{\rm KN}
gives (
19.35
).

If a null bicharacteristic had an interior radial critical point, then
\dot{r}=2\Delta\xi_{r}=0
, so
\xi_{r}=0
, and

\dot{\xi}_{r}=-\partial_{r}p_{\rm KN}=\partial_{r}A_{\rm KN}\tau^{2}<0

(19.39)

unless
\tau=0
. If
\tau=0
, then
p_{\rm KN}=0
forces
\xi_{\theta}=0
, hence the zero covector. Thus there is no interior trapped radial critical ray. A ray reaching the future horizon exits through the absorbing horizon, while a ray reaching the wall reflects once by reversing
\xi_{r}
; monotonicity prevents a second reflecting barrier from forming. Hence the collar is nontrapping.
∎

Proposition 19.8

(Exact wall-collar radial escape identity)
.

Let

p_{\rm KN}=\Delta\xi_{r}^{2}+\xi_{\theta}^{2}-A_{\rm KN}(r,\theta)\tau^{2}

(19.40)

be the axisymmetric principal symbol in the Kerr-Newman wall collar, and assume
\partial_{r}A_{\rm KN}<0
on
r_{+}<r\leq R_{\rm w}
. For every compact radial subcollar
I\Subset(r_{+},R_{\rm w})
there is a smooth real function
b
with
b\leq 0
, supported in a slightly larger subcollar and increasing on
I
, such that, on
I
,

H_{p_{\rm KN}}(b\xi_{r})\geq c_{I}\bigl(\Delta\xi_{r}^{2}+\xi_{\theta}^{2}+A_{\rm KN}\tau^{2}\bigr)-C_{I}|p_{\rm KN}|.

(19.41)

In particular the sign of the time-frequency part is
b\,\partial_{r}A_{\rm KN}\tau^{2}
, not
-b\,\partial_{r}A_{\rm KN}\tau^{2}
.

Proof.

For the escape function
a=b(r)\xi_{r}
, the Hamiltonian vector field gives the exact identity

\displaystyle H_{p_{\rm KN}}a

\displaystyle=\partial_{\xi_{r}}p_{\rm KN}\,\partial_{r}(b\xi_{r})-\partial_{r}p_{\rm KN}\,\partial_{\xi_{r}}(b\xi_{r})

\displaystyle=(2\Delta b^{\prime}-b\Delta^{\prime})\xi_{r}^{2}+b\,\partial_{r}A_{\rm KN}\,\tau^{2}.

(19.42)

Here
b\leq 0
and
\partial_{r}A_{\rm KN}<0
, so the coefficient
b\partial_{r}A_{\rm KN}
is nonnegative. On the characteristic set
p_{\rm KN}=0
,

A_{\rm KN}\tau^{2}=\Delta\xi_{r}^{2}+\xi_{\theta}^{2}.

(19.43)

Choose
b
increasing on
I
and, if necessary, shrink the positive constant so that
2\Delta b^{\prime}-b\Delta^{\prime}
is bounded below there after adding the positive contribution coming from
b\partial_{r}A_{\rm KN}\tau^{2}
and the characteristic identity. Away from the characteristic set, subtracting a sufficiently large multiple of
|p_{\rm KN}|
gives (
19.41
). The cutoffs outside
I
produce only compactly supported lower-order errors; these are precisely the compact interaction-region errors allowed in
BH4
and later removed by
BH7
.
∎

Proposition 19.9

(Neumann wall reflection estimate below the photon region)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{\rm w}=\{R_{\rm w}-\delta_{0}<r<R_{\rm w}\}
be a sufficiently small wall collar, and let
P_{\rm KN}(\sigma)
be the stationary axisymmetric Kerr-Newman wall operator with Neumann wall condition
\partial_{r}u=0
at
r=R_{\rm w}
. If (
19.15
)-(
19.16
) hold, then for every cutoff
\chi_{\rm w}
supported in
\mathcal{C}_{\rm w}
and equal to one near the wall there are a smaller interior cutoff
\chi_{\rm int}
, constants
C,N
, and, in semiclassical notation
h=\langle\sigma\rangle^{-1}
, an estimate

\|\chi_{\rm w}u\|_{H^{1}_{h}(\mathcal{C}_{\rm w})}\leq C\Bigl(\|P_{\rm KN}(\sigma)u\|_{L^{2}_{h}(\mathcal{C}_{\rm w})}+\|\chi_{\rm int}u\|_{H^{1}_{h}}+h^{N}\|u\|_{L^{2}}\Bigr)

(19.44)

for all
|\sigma|\geq\sigma_{0}
and all wall-admissible
u
. Thus the wall-glancing region not controlled by an interior escape function is propagated into the interior collar and does not create a trapped high-frequency obstruction.

Proof.

Near
r=R_{\rm w}
, introduce the boundary normal variable
y=R_{\rm w}-r
. The Neumann condition is
\partial_{y}u=0
at
y=0
. Evenly extend
u
across
y=0
. The principal coefficients of the stationary operator have smooth even extensions modulo lower-order collar errors, and the extended principal symbol is the specular reflection of
p_{\rm KN}
. The Neumann trace condition removes the odd boundary defect in the Green identity, so the reflected equation has an error supported in the collar and bounded by the first term on the right side of (
19.44
) plus controlled lower-order commutators.

For the reflected symbol, Lemma
19.7
gives
\partial_{r}A_{\rm KN}<0
. Therefore every reflected null bicharacteristic issued from the wall collar reaches the interior cutoff region in uniformly bounded bicharacteristic time, unless it is the zero covector. Semiclassical propagation of singularities for the reflected smooth operator gives (
19.44
). The lower-order errors produced by the even extension and cutoffs are absorbed for
|\sigma|\geq\sigma_{0}
, after increasing
N
and shrinking the collar if necessary. The construction preserves axisymmetry and the Neumann wall domain.
∎

Lemma 19.10

(Red-shift, Morawetz, and high-frequency estimates)
.

For
\varepsilon_{\rm KN}
sufficiently small, the Kerr-Newman wall exterior satisfies
BH4
and
BH6
. The high-frequency estimate is nontrapping, and the finite commutator loss is absorbed by taking the package Sobolev index
s_{0}
above a finite wall threshold.

Proof.

The red-shift part is (
19.32
). Integrating the current identity for the multiplier
N
gives nondegenerate control in a horizon collar; the future horizon flux has the favorable sign and the wall flux vanishes by (
19.29
). Commuting with
\partial_{\tau}
and angular derivatives preserves axisymmetry and the wall domain, up to lower-order wall-compatible terms.

In the compact interaction region use the radial escape multiplier from Proposition
19.8
. Its principal symbol is
b(r)\xi_{r}
, with
b\leq 0
and with the sign chosen so that the exact Hamiltonian identity

H_{p_{\rm KN}}(b\xi_{r})=(2\Delta b^{\prime}-b\Delta^{\prime})\xi_{r}^{2}+b\,\partial_{r}A_{\rm KN}\,\tau^{2}

(19.45)

has a positive time-frequency term because
b\leq 0
and
\partial_{r}A_{\rm KN}<0
. On the characteristic set,
A_{\rm KN}\tau^{2}=\Delta\xi_{r}^{2}+\xi_{\theta}^{2}
, so Proposition
19.8
gives the required positive commutator estimate in the compact interaction region, modulo the horizon collar already handled by red-shift, the harmless wall collar, and lower-order compact errors. These lower-order terms are controlled by Cauchy-Schwarz and by the compact zero-threshold error already allowed in
BH4
. The wall boundary contribution vanishes for the chosen wall-compatible multiplier, and the horizon-side contribution is absorbed by the red-shift estimate. This proves (
B.4
) for the Kerr-Newman wall family.

For
|\sigma|\gg 1
, apply the same commutator to the semiclassical stationary operator
h^{2}P_{\rm KN}(\sigma)
,
h=\langle\sigma\rangle^{-1}
. The principal bracket is the positive nontrapping bracket above. Red-shift estimates close the horizon end and Proposition
19.9
closes the reflected wall collar. This yields (
B.6
). Increasing the Sobolev index
s_{0}
so that it dominates the finitely many commutations used in passing from the principal estimate to the displayed Hilbert scale gives (
B.6
). Thus
BH4
and
BH6
hold for all
s_{0}
above a finite wall threshold.
∎

For a stationary ansatz
u=e^{-\mathrm{i}\sigma t}v(r,\theta)
, the axisymmetric stationary operator is

P_{\rm KN}(\sigma)v=\partial_{r}(\Delta\partial_{r}v)+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}v)+\sigma^{2}A_{\rm KN}v.

(19.46)

The outgoing condition at the future horizon is

v(r,\theta)=e^{-\mathrm{i}\sigma r_{*}}b(\theta)+o(1),\qquad r_{*}\to-\infty,

(19.47)

with
b
smooth.

Lemma 19.11

(Mode stability away from zero)
.

For
\varepsilon_{\rm KN}
sufficiently small, the Kerr-Newman wall family satisfies
BH5
: there is no nonzero outgoing axisymmetric solution of
P_{\rm KN}(\sigma)v=0
satisfying (
19.29
) when
\operatorname{Im}\sigma>0
, and there is no real outgoing resonance for
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
.

Proof.

Let
\operatorname{Im}\sigma>0
. The outgoing factor
e^{-\mathrm{i}\sigma r_{*}}
decays exponentially as
r_{*}\to-\infty
. Multiplying
P_{\rm KN}(\sigma)v=0
by
\overline{v}\sin\theta
, integrating over
(r_{+},R_{\rm w})\times(0,\pi)
, using the decay at the horizon and using
\partial_{r}v(R_{\rm w})=0
, gives

\int\left(\Delta|\partial_{r}v|^{2}+|\partial_{\theta}v|^{2}\right)\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}\theta=\sigma^{2}\int A_{\rm KN}|v|^{2}\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}\theta.

(19.48)

The left side is real and nonnegative. If
\operatorname{Re}\sigma\neq 0
, taking imaginary parts and using
A_{\rm KN}>0
gives
v=0
. If
\sigma=\mathrm{i}\gamma
,
\gamma>0
, the right side is nonpositive while the left side is nonnegative, so again
v=0
.

Now let
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
. Integrate by parts from
r_{+}+\epsilon
to
R_{\rm w}
and let
\epsilon\downarrow 0
. All bulk terms are real and the wall term vanishes. From (
19.47
),

\Delta\partial_{r}v=(r^{2}+a^{2})\partial_{r_{*}}v\longrightarrow-\mathrm{i}\sigma(r_{+}^{2}+a^{2})b(\theta).

(19.49)

The imaginary part of the horizon boundary term is therefore a nonzero constant multiple of

\sigma(r_{+}^{2}+a^{2})\int_{0}^{\pi}|b(\theta)|^{2}\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta.

(19.50)

It must vanish, so
b=0
. The regular singular outgoing initial value problem at the horizon then gives
v\equiv 0
, by uniqueness, and elliptic unique continuation gives the same conclusion throughout the collar.

The cutoff resolvent bound on compact intervals
I\Subset\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
follows by contradiction. A failing sequence has normalized localized solutions with sources tending to zero. Local elliptic estimates, the outgoing normal estimate at the horizon, and the compact wall trace estimate give a nonzero limiting outgoing real mode on
I
, contradicting the preceding paragraph. This proves
BH5
.
∎

Lemma 19.12

(Zero frequency and semisimple threshold)
.

At
\sigma=0
, the only regular axisymmetric stationary solution satisfying the Kerr-Newman wall condition is the constant solution. The zero singularity of the cutoff outgoing resolvent is a simple rank-one pole, and there is no regular Jordan companion. Hence

\mathcal{T}_{0,{\rm KN}}=\operatorname{span}\{(1,0)\},\qquad\Pi_{0,{\rm KN}}U=\ell_{\rm KN}(U)(1,0),

(19.51)

with
\ell_{\rm KN}
bounded on
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm KN}
.

Proof.

At
\sigma=0
, (
19.46
) becomes

\partial_{r}(\Delta\partial_{r}v)+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}v)=0.

(19.52)

Multiplying by
\overline{v}\sin\theta
, integrating over the collar, and using regularity at the horizon and
\partial_{r}v(R_{\rm w})=0
, gives

\int_{r_{+}}^{R_{\rm w}}\int_{0}^{\pi}\left(\Delta|\partial_{r}v|^{2}+|\partial_{\theta}v|^{2}\right)\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}r=0.

(19.53)

Thus
v
is constant.

After the outgoing factor at the horizon is removed,
P_{\rm KN}(\sigma)
is an analytic Fredholm family between the outgoing horizon domain with Neumann wall and the compactly supported dual Sobolev spaces. The kernel at
\sigma=0
is one-dimensional by the previous paragraph, and the adjoint kernel is one-dimensional by the same identity for the adjoint boundary condition. Therefore the low-frequency singular part is finite rank. It remains to rule out a generalized zero state.

A Jordan companion to the constant state would give a regular solution

u(\tau,r,\theta)=\tau+\psi(r,\theta)

(19.54)

which satisfies the wall condition (
19.30
). Since
\tau=t+H(r)
, the stationary part in Boyer-Lindquist time is
\chi=H+\psi
. The equation
\Box_{g}(t+\chi)=0
is

\partial_{r}(\Delta\partial_{r}\chi)+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}\chi)=0.

(19.55)

Equivalently, using
\Delta H^{\prime}=2Mr-Q^{2}
,

\partial_{r}\left(2Mr-Q^{2}+\Delta\partial_{r}\psi\right)+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}\psi)=0.

(19.56)

Let
\psi_{0}(r)
be the spherical mean of
\psi
. Averaging (
19.56
) gives

2Mr-Q^{2}+\Delta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(r)=C.

(19.57)

Smoothness of
\psi
at the future horizon forces

C=2Mr_{+}-Q^{2}=r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}.

(19.58)

Taking the spherical mean of the wall condition (
19.30
) gives

\psi_{0}^{\prime}(R_{\rm w})+H^{\prime}(R_{\rm w})=0.

(19.59)

But the equation for
\psi_{0}
and the identity
\Delta H^{\prime}=2Mr-Q^{2}
give

\psi_{0}^{\prime}(R_{\rm w})+H^{\prime}(R_{\rm w})=\frac{C}{\Delta(R_{\rm w})}=\frac{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta(R_{\rm w})}\neq 0.

(19.60)

This contradiction excludes every regular Jordan companion. Hence the zero pole is simple and semisimple. Its residue defines
\Pi_{0,{\rm KN}}
, and local Sobolev mapping of the residue gives boundedness of
\ell_{\rm KN}
. This proves
BH8
.
∎

Lemma 19.13

(Wall limiting absorption and compact smoothing)
.

For
\varepsilon_{\rm KN}
sufficiently small, after applying
I-\Pi_{0,{\rm KN}}
, the Kerr-Newman wall cutoff resolvent has real-axis boundary values on every compact nonzero frequency interval, remains bounded at zero, and satisfies the compact Kato smoothing estimate (
B.8
) for every
s
above a finite wall threshold. The
L^{1}_{\sigma}
local spectral-density part of
BH7
is proved separately in Theorem
19.14
; together the present lemma and that package give the full
BH7
verification for the wall family.

Proof.

The outgoing stationary problem on the collar is Fredholm after imposing the Volterra outgoing model at the future horizon and the Neumann wall condition at
R_{\rm w}
. Lemma
19.11
removes all upper-half-plane poles and all nonzero real poles. Lemma
19.10
gives the high-frequency nontrapping bounds. Lemma
19.12
gives the complete low-frequency Laurent expansion and shows that the only singular term is the rank-one threshold projection. After applying
I-\Pi_{0,{\rm KN}}
, the resolvent is bounded at zero.

These estimates cover the real line by a low-frequency neighborhood, finitely many compact nonzero intervals, and two high-frequency tails. On compact nonzero intervals, the real-axis boundary values follow from Fredholm invertibility and the absence of real outgoing resonances. At zero, the threshold projection cancels the only singular coefficient. At high frequency, the nontrapping commutator and wall-reflection estimates give the semiclassical bound. The usual Fourier-Laplace/Plancherel argument for the cutoff resolvent then gives

\|\chi_{\rm c}U_{\perp}\|_{L^{2}([0,\infty);H^{s}_{\rm loc}\times H^{s-1}_{\rm loc})}\leq C_{s}\|(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm KN}})U_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm KN}},

(19.61)

which is (
B.8
). The derivative loss is finite because the horizon Volterra construction, the compact elliptic wall estimate, and the nontrapping high-frequency estimate each lose only finitely many derivatives on this compact collar. This proves the limiting-absorption and smoothing part of
BH7
. The integrable spectral-density representation is the frequency-splitting statement of Theorem
19.14
, proved next.
∎

Theorem 19.14

(Wall spectral-density bound by frequency splitting)
.

For the Kerr-Newman wall family of Theorem
19.15
, after applying
I-\Pi_{0,{\rm KN}}
and for every compact
K\Subset\{r_{+}<r<R_{\rm w}\}
, the localized spectral density satisfies

\|B_{K}(\cdot)(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm KN}})U_{0}\|_{L^{1}_{\sigma}(H^{1}(K)\times L^{2}(K))}\leq C_{K,s}\|(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm KN}})U_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm KN}}

(19.62)

for all
s\geq s_{\rm wall}
, where
s_{\rm wall}
is a finite integer depending only on the wall commutator, Volterra, and elliptic losses. If, in addition, the differentiated resolvent bounds supplied by the Volterra horizon construction and the nontrapping semiclassical estimate hold up to order
N
, then

\sum_{j=0}^{N}\|\partial_{\sigma}^{j}B_{K}(\cdot)(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm KN}})U_{0}\|_{L^{1}_{\sigma}(H^{1}(K)\times L^{2}(K))}\leq C_{K,N,s}\|(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm KN}})U_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm KN}}

(19.63)

for sufficiently large
s=s(N)
