. Thus the qualitative local decay asserted in Theorem
1.13
is unconditional in the wall regime, while polynomial rates require the additional differentiated bound (
19.63
).

Proof.

Split the frequency line into

\mathbb{R}=\{|\sigma|\leq\sigma_{0}\}\cup\{\sigma_{0}\leq|\sigma|\leq R\}\cup\{|\sigma|\geq R\},

(19.64)

where
0<\sigma_{0}\ll 1\ll R
. On the low-frequency interval, Lemma
19.12
gives a finite-rank Laurent expansion. The only singular coefficient is the semisimple constant threshold, and it is killed by
I-\Pi_{0,{\rm KN}}
. The remaining cutoff resolvent is bounded and continuous in
\sigma
, hence its contribution to the density is integrable on
|\sigma|\leq\sigma_{0}
.

On the compact nonzero interval
\sigma_{0}\leq|\sigma|\leq R
, Lemma
19.11
rules out outgoing real resonances and Lemma
19.13
supplies the limiting-absorption boundary values by the Fredholm contradiction argument. Since the interval is compact and separated from zero, the boundary values are uniformly bounded between the local Sobolev spaces used to define
B_{K}
. This gives an integrable contribution on the middle interval.

On the high-frequency tails, Lemma
19.10
and Proposition
19.8
give the nontrapping semiclassical estimate. After commuting with the generator and using the equation to trade powers of
\sigma
for time derivatives of the initial data, the spectral density has an integrable high-frequency majorant for
s\geq s_{\rm wall}
. Concretely, the cutoff resolvent contribution is bounded by a finite sum of norms of
\mathcal{G}_{\rm KN}^{j}(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm KN}})U_{0}
times an integrable power of
\langle\sigma\rangle^{-1}
; the integer
s_{\rm wall}
is chosen so that those commuted data are controlled by
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm KN}
. The horizon Volterra construction and the Neumann wall elliptic estimate preserve the domain, while
BH9
controls the cutoffs. Summing the three frequency regions proves (
19.62
). If the same bounds are differentiated
N
times in
\sigma
, the identical three-region argument gives (
19.63
); each differentiation costs finitely many commutations, which is why the Sobolev index becomes
s(N)
.
∎

Theorem 19.15

(Verification of
BH1
-
BH9
for slowly rotating weakly charged Kerr-Newman wall exteriors)
.

For every
M>0
and every
R_{\rm w}
satisfying (
19.15
), there exists
\varepsilon_{\rm KN}=\varepsilon_{\rm KN}(M,R_{\rm w})>0
such that, whenever (
19.16
) holds, the axisymmetric neutral scalar equation
\Box_{g_{M,a,Q}}u=0
on
\mathcal{M}_{M,a,Q,R_{\rm w}}
, with future-horizon regularity and wall condition (
19.29
), satisfies Assumption
B.3
. There is a finite admissible index
s_{\rm wall}
, obtained as the maximum of the commutator, Volterra, Fredholm, and spectral-density losses in the preceding lemmas, such that the package holds for every
s_{0}\geq s_{\rm wall}
. The threshold space is semisimple and one-dimensional:

\mathcal{T}_{0,{\rm KN}}=\operatorname{span}\{(1,0)\}.

(19.65)

Proof.

Choose
\varepsilon_{\rm KN}
to be the minimum of the smallness constants required in Lemmas
19.6
-
19.13
.
BH1
-
BH3
are Lemma
19.6
.
BH4
and
BH6
are Lemma
19.10
.
BH5
is Lemma
19.11
.
BH8
is Lemma
19.12
. The limiting-absorption and compact-smoothing parts of
BH7
are Lemma
19.13
, while the
L^{1}_{\sigma}
local spectral-density part of
BH7
is Theorem
19.14
. Finally,
BH9
follows from the construction: horizon cutoffs are supported in the smooth ingoing chart (
19.26
); wall cutoffs are either constant near
R_{\rm w}
or preserve (
19.30
); angular cutoffs and angular elliptic regularizers preserve the
m=0
sector; and all commutators are lower-order operators with smooth coefficients on the compact collar. The index condition
s_{0}\geq s_{\rm wall}
absorbs the finitely many derivative losses in the commutator, Volterra, Fredholm, and spectral-density arguments.
∎

19.4.  Full asymptotically flat subextremal Kerr-Newman exterior

We now remove the artificial wall for the Einstein Kerr-Newman subfamily. This subsection is intentionally precise about what is proved directly and what is imported. The direct part consists of the geometry, horizon regularity, radiation-domain bookkeeping, the zero-frequency ODE analysis, and the verification that these pieces fit Assumption
B.3
. The non-elementary part is the global scalar-wave scattering package on the full asymptotically flat Kerr-Newman exterior, where trapping and the ergoregion interact. We use the scalar Kerr-Newman stability theorem quoted below; without that package, the high-frequency trapping and limiting-absorption estimates would not be proved by the elementary wall argument above.

Fix numbers
0<\delta<1
and
0<M_{0}<M_{1}<\infty
, and set

\mathscr{K}_{\delta,M_{0},M_{1}}:=\{(M,a,Q):M_{0}\leq M\leq M_{1},\ a^{2}+Q^{2}\leq(1-\delta)M^{2}\}.

(19.66)

The constants below are uniform on
\mathscr{K}_{\delta,M_{0},M_{1}}
; they may degenerate as
\delta\downarrow 0
, i.e. as the extremal boundary is approached. Let

\rho^{2}=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta,\qquad\Delta=r^{2}-2Mr+a^{2}+Q^{2},\qquad r_{\pm}=M\pm\sqrt{M^{2}-a^{2}-Q^{2}}.

(19.67)

The Kerr-Newman metric is written as

g_{M,a,Q}=-\frac{\Delta}{\rho^{2}}(\mathrm{d}t-a\sin^{2}\theta\,\mathrm{d}\phi)^{2}+\frac{\sin^{2}\theta}{\rho^{2}}((r^{2}+a^{2})\mathrm{d}\phi-a\mathrm{d}t)^{2}+\frac{\rho^{2}}{\Delta}\mathrm{d}r^{2}+\rho^{2}\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}.

(19.68)

The domain of outer communications is
r>r_{+}
. The horizon generator, angular velocity, and surface gravity are

K=T+\Omega_{H}\Phi,\qquad T=\partial_{t},\qquad\Phi=\partial_{\phi},\qquad\Omega_{H}=\frac{a}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},\qquad\kappa_{+}=\frac{r_{+}-r_{-}}{2(r_{+}^{2}+a^{2})}>0.

(19.69)

The neutral axisymmetric conformal scalar is

\Box_{g_{M,a,Q}}u=0,\qquad\partial_{\phi}u=0.

(19.70)

For
\partial_{\phi}u=0
, the principal stationary operator obtained from
u=e^{-\mathrm{i}\sigma t}v(r,\theta)
is

P_{\rm KN,af}(\sigma)v=\partial_{r}(\Delta\partial_{r}v)+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}v)+\sigma^{2}A_{\rm KN}(r,\theta)v,

(19.71)

where

A_{\rm KN}(r,\theta)=\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-a^{2}\Delta\sin^{2}\theta}{\Delta}>0\qquad(r>r_{+}).

(19.72)

At the future horizon the outgoing solution is the one smooth in ingoing coordinates

v_{\rm EF}=t+r_{*},\qquad\phi_{\rm in}=\phi+\int^{r}\frac{a}{\Delta(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad\frac{\mathrm{d}r_{*}}{\mathrm{d}r}=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}.

(19.73)

At null infinity the outgoing condition is the Sommerfeld condition for the same tortoise coordinate: for
e^{-\mathrm{i}\sigma t}
with
\operatorname{Im}\sigma\geq 0
, the outgoing radial factor is asymptotic to
e^{+\mathrm{i}\sigma r_{*}}/r
, with the usual symbolic expansion in powers of
r^{-1}
. The decaying radiation energy space below is the closure of smooth axisymmetric data with this finite-energy/outgoing behavior; it excludes nondecaying constants at infinity.

For
s\geq 0
, define
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{{\rm KN},\rm af}
to be the axisymmetric red-shift energy space on a regular Cauchy hypersurface
\Sigma
crossing the future horizon and extending to spatial infinity, with norm equivalent to

\sum_{|\alpha|\leq s}\int_{\Sigma}\left(|\nabla_{\Sigma}Z^{\alpha}u_{0}|^{2}+|Z^{\alpha}u_{1}|^{2}+r^{-2}|Z^{\alpha}u_{0}|^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma}.

(19.74)

Here
Z
ranges over a fixed finite collection of stationary, angular, and horizon-regular radial commutator fields preserving axisymmetry; near the horizon the norm is computed with a red-shift timelike vector field
N
, and for large
r
it is equivalent to the standard asymptotically flat energy. The term
r^{-2}|u_{0}|^{2}
, interpreted through the Hardy inequality for the closure of compactly supported data, fixes the decaying representative and excludes the constant solution from
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{{\rm KN},\rm af}
. Equivalent choices of the commutator frame give the same Hilbert scale.

The only non-elementary scalar Kerr-Newman scattering input used for the full asymptotically flat exterior is External Assumption
B.5
.

Lemma 19.16

(Geometry, radiation domain, and generator for full Kerr-Newman)
.

The full asymptotically flat subextremal Kerr-Newman scalar exterior satisfies
BH1
-
BH3
and
BH9
on
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{{\rm KN},\rm af}
, for every
s\geq s_{\rm KN}
.

Proof.

The identity
a^{2}+Q^{2}<M^{2}
gives two distinct roots
r_{\pm}
of
\Delta
. Formula (
19.69
) gives
\kappa_{+}>0
, and hence the future horizon is nondegenerate. In the ingoing chart (
19.73
), the apparent
\Delta^{-1}
singularities of (
19.68
) cancel in the standard way; the metric and
\Box_{g}
extend smoothly to
r=r_{+}
. This proves the geometric part of
BH1
.

The outer boundary is null infinity. The radiation condition is imposed by the outgoing Fourier-Laplace construction in External Assumption
B.5
; in physical space it is the finite-energy condition on the closure of smooth decaying data together with no incoming boundary flux from past null infinity for the forward problem. Since the scalar is neutral and axisymmetric, no charged boundary phase or non-axisymmetric superradiant boundary condition is present. This proves
BH2
.

The norm (
19.74
), with the red-shift vector field near
r=r_{+}
, defines a Hilbert scale. Standard well-posedness for normally hyperbolic equations on globally hyperbolic exterior slabs with red-shift boundary regularity gives a closed future generator
\mathcal{G}_{{\rm KN},\rm af}
and a strongly continuous semigroup. Smooth compactly supported axisymmetric data away from infinity, smoothed in the ingoing horizon chart, are dense by the usual mollification, cutoff, trace, and Hardy arguments; the weight
r^{-2}u^{2}
fixes the decaying representative at infinity. Thus
BH3
holds.

The cutoffs used in the scalar theory are chosen in three regions: a horizon collar in the ingoing chart, a compact interaction region, and an asymptotically flat end. Multiplication by these cutoffs preserves axisymmetry and the decaying energy domain. Commutators with
\Box_{g}
are first-order operators with coefficients controlled by the same local-energy and weighted Sobolev norms; in the end the coefficients have the standard long-range asymptotically flat decay. The derivative index
s\geq s_{\rm KN}
absorbs the trapping and commutator losses. This proves
BH9
.
∎

Lemma 19.17

(Red-shift, mode stability, trapping, and limiting absorption)
.

Under External Assumption
B.5
, the full asymptotically flat subextremal Kerr-Newman scalar exterior satisfies
BH4
,
BH5
,
BH6
, and
BH7
on the projected space associated with the zero-frequency projection of Lemma
19.18
.

Proof.

The red-shift and integrated-local-energy clauses
EKN2
-
EKN3
give the finite-time red-shift/local-energy estimate
BH4
, with the finite trapped-set derivative loss allowed by the exterior Hilbert scale. The compact error in
BH4
is the usual compact interaction-region term left before applying smoothing.

The quantitative real-frequency mode-stability clause
EKN4
gives
BH5
. The external package is stronger than needed here because it is stated for the scalar theory before the present restriction to
m=0
; restricting to the neutral axisymmetric sector gives the asserted absence of upper-half-plane outgoing modes and nonzero real outgoing resonances.

The high-frequency trapped-set resolvent clause
EKN5
gives
BH6
. Thus high-frequency propagation is controlled by the Kerr-Newman trapped-set estimate rather than by the elementary nontrapping commutator used in the wall collar. The polynomial loss in
|\sigma|
and the finite derivative loss are precisely the losses allowed in (
B.6
).

The limiting-absorption, smoothing, and spectral-density clauses
EKN6
,
EKN8
, and
EKN9
give
BH7
. The Fourier-Laplace representation of the semigroup and the limiting-absorption boundary values give the local spectral density
B_{K}(\sigma)
. After commuting
s\geq s_{\rm KN}
times, the high-frequency bounds, finite-frequency bounds, and low-frequency regularity from Lemma
19.18
make this density integrable in
\sigma
, and Plancherel gives the compact Kato smoothing estimate (
B.8
). Hence the compact error in
BH4
is removable on the projected evolution.
∎

Lemma 19.18

(Zero frequency for the decaying full Kerr-Newman radiation space)
.

For the decaying radiation energy space
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{{\rm KN},\rm af}
, the zero-frequency threshold space of the neutral axisymmetric Kerr-Newman scalar wave is trivial:

\mathcal{T}_{0,{\rm KN},\rm af}=\{0\},\qquad\Pi_{0,{\rm KN},\rm af}=0.

(19.75)

Moreover the cutoff outgoing resolvent is regular at
\sigma=0
on this decaying space, and there is no regular generalized zero state.

Proof.

At
\sigma=0
, equation (
19.71
) becomes

\partial_{r}(\Delta\partial_{r}v)+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}v)=0.

(19.76)

Expand the axisymmetric finite-energy solution in spherical harmonics,

v(r,\theta)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}v_{\ell}(r)Y_{\ell 0}(\theta).

(19.77)

Each coefficient satisfies

(\Delta v_{\ell}^{\prime})^{\prime}-\ell(\ell+1)v_{\ell}=0.

(19.78)

Set

\alpha=\sqrt{M^{2}-a^{2}-Q^{2}}>0,\qquad x=\frac{r-M}{\alpha},

(19.79)

so that
\Delta=\alpha^{2}(x^{2}-1)
and the horizon is
x=1
. Equation (
19.78
) becomes the Legendre equation

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\left((x^{2}-1)\frac{\mathrm{d}v_{\ell}}{\mathrm{d}x}\right)-\ell(\ell+1)v_{\ell}=0.

(19.80)

Its two independent solutions are
P_{\ell}(x)
and
Q_{\ell}(x)
. The function
Q_{\ell}
has a logarithmic singularity at
x=1
, so future-horizon regularity excludes it. The function
P_{\ell}
behaves like
x^{\ell}
as
x\to\infty
. For
\ell\geq 1
, this growth is incompatible with the decaying finite-energy norm (
19.74
). For
\ell=0
, the regular solution
P_{0}=1
is a nondecaying constant, also excluded by the
r^{-2}u^{2}
Hardy term and by taking the closure of compactly supported decaying data. Hence every regular decaying stationary state is zero.

A generalized zero state would satisfy
\mathcal{G}_{{\rm KN},\rm af}V=W
for a stationary threshold state
W
. Since
W=0
, no nontrivial Jordan chain can start. Equivalently, the possible affine solution
u=t+\psi
has
u_{t}=1
, which is not in
L^{2}(\Sigma)
at the asymptotically flat end and therefore is not a vector in
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{{\rm KN},\rm af}
.

It remains to identify the low-frequency resolvent behavior. The scalar limiting-absorption theorem gives a meromorphic finite-rank expansion at zero on the weighted spaces used above. A pole or finite-rank singular coefficient would produce, by taking its leading coefficient, either a regular decaying stationary state or a generalized zero state. Both have just been excluded. Therefore
\nu_{0}=0
in (
B.11
) for this realization,
R_{0}(\sigma)
is bounded at
\sigma=0
, and the data-space projection is
\Pi_{0,{\rm KN},\rm af}=0
. This proves
BH8
.
∎

Theorem 19.19

(Verification of
BH1
-
BH9
for full subextremal Kerr-Newman scalar exteriors)
.

Fix
0<\delta<1
and
0<M_{0}<M_{1}<\infty
. For every
(M,a,Q)\in\mathscr{K}_{\delta,M_{0},M_{1}}
, the neutral axisymmetric scalar wave equation on the full asymptotically flat Kerr-Newman domain of outer communications, with future-horizon regularity and outgoing decaying radiation condition at null infinity, satisfies Assumption
B.3
on
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{{\rm KN},\rm af}
for all
s\geq s_{\rm KN}
. The threshold space is trivial:

\mathcal{T}_{0,{\rm KN},\rm af}=\{0\},\qquad\Pi_{0,{\rm KN},\rm af}=0.

(19.81)

Proof.

BH1
-
BH3
and
BH9
are Lemma
19.16
.
BH4
,
BH5
,
BH6
, and
BH7
are Lemma
19.17
, which is the precise place where the scalar Kerr-Newman stability input, External Assumption
B.5
, is used.
BH8
is Lemma
19.18
. These nine statements are exactly the items of Assumption
B.3
; hence the full Kerr-Newman exterior satisfies the package. The constants are uniform on compact subextremal parameter sets by External Assumption
B.5
and the explicit lower bound for
\kappa_{+}
on
\mathscr{K}_{\delta,M_{0},M_{1}}
.
∎

19.5.  Extremal asymptotically flat Kerr-Newman: horizon charge, zero frequency, and obstruction

We now include the extremal Kerr-Newman parameter surface

a^{2}+Q^{2}=M^{2},\qquad M>0,\qquad r_{+}=r_{-}=M,\qquad\Delta=(r-M)^{2}.

(19.82)

This subsection is not a limiting version of Theorem
A.2
. The subextremal proof uses
\kappa_{+}>0
; here
\kappa_{+}=0
. The correct extremal result is a sharp obstruction theorem showing why nondegenerate horizon decay cannot be part of the conclusion.

Use ingoing coordinates

v=t+r_{*},\qquad\phi_{\rm in}=\phi+\int^{r}\frac{a}{\Delta(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad\frac{\mathrm{d}r_{*}}{\mathrm{d}r}=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}.

(19.83)

For axisymmetric functions, the Boyer-Lindquist form of the wave operator is

\rho^{2}\Box_{g}u=-\left(\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}{\Delta}-a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\right)\partial_{t}^{2}u+\partial_{r}(\Delta\partial_{r}u)+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}u).

(19.84)

After substituting
\partial_{t}=\partial_{v}
and
\partial_{r}|_{t}=\partial_{r}|_{v}+((r^{2}+a^{2})/\Delta)\partial_{v}
, the same equation becomes, in the horizon-regular chart,

\displaystyle\rho^{2}\Box_{g}u={}

\displaystyle\Delta\partial_{r}^{2}u+\Delta^{\prime}\partial_{r}u+2(r^{2}+a^{2})\partial_{rv}u+2r\partial_{v}u+a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\,\partial_{v}^{2}u

(19.85)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}u).

This formula is the local hard computation behind the extremal part of the paper.

Proposition 19.20

(Extremal Kerr-Newman horizon charge)
.

Let
u
be a smooth axisymmetric solution of
\Box_{g_{M,a,Q}}u=0
on an extremal Kerr-Newman exterior. Then

\mathfrak{A}_{0}[u](v):=\int_{0}^{\pi}\Bigl(2(M^{2}+a^{2})\partial_{r}u+2Mu+a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\,\partial_{v}u\Bigr)(v,M,\theta)\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta

(19.86)

is independent of
v
.

Proof.

At the extremal horizon
r=M
one has
\Delta(M)=\Delta^{\prime}(M)=0
. Evaluating (
19.85
) on
r=M
gives

2(M^{2}+a^{2})\partial_{rv}u+2M\partial_{v}u+a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\,\partial_{v}^{2}u+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}u)=0.

(19.87)

Multiply by
\sin\theta
and integrate over
0\leq\theta\leq\pi
. Smoothness at the poles gives

\int_{0}^{\pi}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}u)\,\mathrm{d}\theta=0.

(19.88)

The remaining terms are exactly
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}v}\mathfrak{A}_{0}[u](v)
. Hence
\mathfrak{A}_{0}[u](v)
is conserved.
∎

Corollary 19.21

(Extremal Kerr charge)
.

Let
|a|=M
and
Q=0
. For every smooth future-horizon regular axisymmetric solution on extremal Kerr,

\mathfrak{A}^{\rm Kerr}_{0}[u](v)=\int_{0}^{\pi}\Bigl(4M^{2}\partial_{r}u+2Mu+M^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\,\partial_{v}u\Bigr)(v,M,\theta)\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta

(19.89)

is independent of
v
. If this charge is nonzero and the tangential horizon quantities
u
and
\partial_{v}u
decay, then the averaged transversal derivative
\partial_{r}u
does not decay on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

Proof.

Set
Q=0
and
a^{2}=M^{2}
in Proposition
19.20
. The nondecay conclusion is the same algebraic rearrangement used in Corollary
19.23
: the conserved nonzero left-over charge must be carried by the averaged transversal derivative once the tangential terms decay.
∎

Corollary 19.22

(Extremal Reissner-Nordström charge)
.

Let
|Q|=M
and
a=0
. For every smooth future-horizon regular solution on extremal Reissner-Nordström, the spherical mean charge

\mathfrak{A}^{\rm RN}_{0}[u](v)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\Bigl(2M^{2}\partial_{r}u+2Mu\Bigr)(v,M,\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\omega

(19.90)

is independent of
v
. If this charge is nonzero and the spherical mean of
u
on the horizon decays, then the spherical mean of
\partial_{r}u
on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
does not decay.

Proof.

For axisymmetric solutions, this is Proposition
19.20
with
a=0
and
Q^{2}=M^{2}
, multiplied by the harmless factor
2\pi
coming from the
\phi
-integration. For general solutions on the spherically symmetric extremal Reissner-Nordström background, integrate the horizon equation over the full sphere; the angular Laplacian integrates to zero on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. The remaining terms are exactly
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}v}\mathfrak{A}^{\rm RN}_{0}[u](v)
. Rearranging the conserved identity gives the nondecay of the averaged transversal derivative whenever the charge is nonzero and the averaged tangential term decays.
∎

Corollary 19.23

(Failure of subextremal-type horizon decay)
.

There is no estimate on the full extremal Kerr-Newman axisymmetric scalar energy class which simultaneously implies decay of
u
,
\partial_{v}u
, and the averaged transversal derivative
\partial_{r}u
on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
for all smooth data. More precisely, if
\mathfrak{A}_{0}[u]\neq 0
and
u
and
\partial_{v}u
decay along the horizon, then

\limsup_{v\to\infty}\left|\int_{0}^{\pi}\partial_{r}u(v,M,\theta)\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta\right|>0.

(19.91)

Proof.

The conservation law (
19.86
) gives

2(M^{2}+a^{2})\int_{0}^{\pi}\partial_{r}u(v,M,\theta)\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta=\mathfrak{A}_{0}[u]-\int_{0}^{\pi}\left(2Mu+a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\,\partial_{v}u\right)\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta.

(19.92)

If the two tangential terms on the right decay to zero and
\mathfrak{A}_{0}[u]\neq 0
, the averaged transversal derivative tends to the nonzero number
\mathfrak{A}_{0}[u]/(2(M^{2}+a^{2}))
. Thus it cannot decay.
∎

Lemma 19.24

(Zero-frequency classification in the decaying extremal exterior)
.

In the decaying asymptotically flat radiation energy space, there is no nonzero smooth axisymmetric stationary solution of
\Box_{g_{M,a,Q}}u=0
on an extremal Kerr-Newman exterior which is regular at
r=M
and decays at infinity.

Proof.

At
\sigma=0
, separation into axisymmetric spherical harmonics gives

\bigl((r-M)^{2}R_{\ell}^{\prime}\bigr)^{\prime}-\ell(\ell+1)R_{\ell}=0.

(19.93)

Writing
y=r-M
, the equation is

y^{2}R_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}+2yR_{\ell}^{\prime}-\ell(\ell+1)R_{\ell}=0.

(19.94)

For
\ell\geq 1
, the two indicial roots are
\ell
and
-\ell-1
, hence the two model behaviors are
y^{\ell}
and
y^{-\ell-1}
. The second is singular at the horizon, while the first grows like
r^{\ell}
at infinity and is not in the decaying energy space. For
\ell=0
, the solutions are
1
and
-1/y
. Horizon regularity excludes
-1/y
, and decay at infinity excludes the constant. Thus every mode vanishes.
∎

Proposition 19.25

(Extremal Kerr-Newman is outside the nondegenerate black-hole package)
.

The extremal Kerr-Newman exterior does not satisfy Assumption
B.3
as stated. Specifically,
BH1
fails because the horizon endpoint is a double root with
\kappa_{+}=0
, and
BH4
fails in its nondegenerate red-shift form. The appropriate theorem is therefore Theorem
1.14
, not Theorem
A.2
.

Proof.

For extremal Kerr-Newman,
\Delta=(r-M)^{2}
. The horizon root is not simple and

\kappa_{+}=\frac{r_{+}-r_{-}}{2(r_{+}^{2}+a^{2})}=0.

(19.95)

The red-shift proof of
BH4
uses a positive lower bound for the deformation tensor of a timelike multiplier transversal to the horizon. That lower bound is proportional to the nonzero surface gravity in the smooth horizon frame; it degenerates when
\kappa_{+}=0
. Proposition
19.20
gives a stronger obstruction: for data with nonzero
\mathfrak{A}_{0}
, the averaged transverse derivative cannot decay if the tangential fields decay. Hence a nondegenerate red-shift/decay package of the subextremal form is impossible on the full extremal class.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.14
.

The charge identity is Proposition
19.20
. The obstruction to subextremal-type horizon decay is Corollary
19.23
. The failure of the nondegenerate package is Proposition
19.25
. The zero-frequency stationary-state assertion in the decaying radiation space is Lemma
19.24
. The higher-order generic blow-up statement, when one wants the full Aretakis conclusion beyond the first conserved charge proved here, is supplied by External Assumption
B.6
. These statements give exactly the conclusions asserted in Theorem
1.14
.
∎

19.6.  Logical dependency for the exterior theorem

For readability, we spell out the role of the black-hole inputs in prose. In the Kerr-Newman wall family these inputs are proved in Theorem
19.15
. In the full asymptotically flat Kerr-Newman scalar exterior they are verified in Theorem
19.19
, with the scalar Kerr-Newman stability theorem isolated locally as External Assumption
B.5
. For any other exterior geometry, the same list should be read as the verification checklist.

The first group,
BH1
-
BH3
, sets up the problem itself. These hypotheses provide the smooth exterior geometry, the admissible boundary or radiation condition, the Hilbert energy scale, and the closed evolution generator. Their role is foundational: without them there is no precise Cauchy problem and no well-defined stationary resolvent problem.

The next input,
BH4
, is the finite-time red-shift and local-energy estimate, allowing for a compact error in the interaction region. This is the estimate that starts the a priori argument on finite time intervals. The compact error is not ignored; it is later removed by the projected limiting-absorption and compact-smoothing mechanism supplied by
BH7
.

The finite-frequency spectral input is
BH5
. It rules out outgoing modes in the upper half-plane and excludes real-axis outgoing resonances away from zero frequency. Analytically, this is the step that prevents exponentially growing solutions and removes compact obstructions at nonzero real frequencies.

The high-frequency input is
BH6
. It supplies the propagation and trapping estimates needed to control the outgoing resolvent for large frequency. If the geometry has trapping, this is precisely where the trapping theorem enters. Any derivative loss produced by trapping must be accounted for in the Sobolev index of the energy space.

The dispersive input is
BH7
. After the zero-frequency threshold space has been projected away,
BH7
gives the limiting-absorption estimate, compact smoothing, and a local spectral representation with
L^{1}
density in frequency. This input has two roles: it absorbs the compact error left by
BH4
, and it gives local decay through the Riemann-Lebesgue lemma.

The threshold input is
BH8
. It describes the behavior of the outgoing resolvent near zero frequency by a finite-rank Laurent expansion. This expansion produces the data-space projection
\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}
, identifies the generalized zero-frequency threshold space, and isolates the polynomial-in-time threshold component of the evolution.

Finally,
BH9
is the compatibility hypothesis. It ensures that the cutoffs used near the horizon, in the interaction region, and near the outer end preserve the relevant domains and boundary conditions up to controlled lower-order errors. This is the technical bridge that justifies commutator estimates, density arguments, and the passage from smooth compactly supported data to the full energy space.

In this form, the black-hole package is not a black box with hidden content. Each input has a specific analytical job:
BH1
-
BH3
define the problem,
BH4
begins the energy estimate,
BH5
-
BH6
control the resolvent away from zero,
BH7
converts the projected resolvent theory into decay,
BH8
isolates the zero-frequency obstruction, and
BH9
keeps the functional framework consistent throughout the argument.

Proposition 19.26

(No circular use of the exterior conclusion)
.

The proof of Theorem
1.7
does not assume its conclusion. More precisely,
BH1
-
BH3
are used only to define the closed exterior evolution and its energy scale;
BH8
is used to construct the zero-frequency projection and the finite-dimensional polynomial threshold part;
BH4
supplies the finite-time red-shift/local-energy estimate with compact error;
BH7
supplies the projected smoothing and limiting-absorption mechanism that removes this compact error and gives local decay;
BH5
excludes upper-half-plane and nonzero real outgoing modes;
BH6
supplies the high-frequency resolvent control needed in
BH7
; and
BH9
justifies the cutoff and commutator passages in the chosen domain. The global bound (
1.39
) and the local decay statement (
1.40
) are derived only after these inputs have been combined.

Proof.

The construction of
\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}
and the polynomial formula for the threshold term use only the Laurent expansion and finite-rank threshold statement in
BH8
. The projected solution is then inserted into the finite-time estimate of
BH4
. The only term in that estimate not controlled by initial energy is the compact interaction-region error. By
BH7
, after applying
I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}
this compact error is bounded uniformly in time by the cutoff smoothing estimate (
B.8
); hence the projected energy and local-energy norms are controlled by the projected initial datum. The Fourier-Laplace representation in
BH7
has an
L^{1}_{\sigma}
local spectral density, so the Banach-valued Riemann-Lebesgue lemma gives local decay on compact sets. The no-mode statement follows separately from
BH5
. High-frequency and commutator estimates enter only through
BH6
and
BH9
, which are hypotheses or are verified before Theorem
1.7
is applied. Thus none of the final boundedness or decay assertions is used as an input.
∎

19.7.  Proof of the black-hole exterior theorem from the package

We now prove Theorem
1.7
. The proof is included here, in the main body, so that every exterior input used by the argument is adjacent to the hypothesis that supplies it.

Lemma 19.27

(Threshold projection and polynomial threshold evolution)
.

Assume
BH3
and
BH8
. Then
\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}
is a bounded finite-rank projection on
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}
, commutes with
\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}
and with
e^{t\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}}
, and has range
\mathcal{T}_{0,{\rm bh}}
. Moreover
e^{t\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}}\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}U_{0}
is a finite-dimensional polynomial in
t
. In the two wall cases this polynomial has degree zero; in the decaying full Kerr-Newman case the projection is zero.

Proof.

By
BH8
, the low-frequency resolvent expansion and adjoint threshold functionals define the bounded finite-rank projection (
B.12
). The identities
\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}^{2}=\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}
and
e^{t\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}}\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}=\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}e^{t\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}}
are part of the zero-frequency threshold calculus in
BH8
. The range is exactly
\mathcal{T}_{0,{\rm bh}}
, and the restriction
N_{0}:=\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}|_{\operatorname{Ran}\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}}
is nilpotent of some order
q
. Therefore

e^{t\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}}\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}U_{0}=\sum_{j=0}^{q-1}\frac{t^{j}}{j!}N_{0}^{j}\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}U_{0}.

(19.96)

For the Kerr-Newman wall family, Lemma
19.12
gives
q=1
, so the threshold term is the corresponding stationary constant. For the full asymptotically flat Kerr-Newman radiation space, Lemma
19.18
gives
\Pi_{0,{\rm KN},\rm af}=0
, so the threshold term is absent.
∎

Lemma 19.28

(Projected exterior estimate)
.

Assume
BH1
-
BH9
. For every
U_{0}\in\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}
, let

U_{\perp}(t)=e^{t\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}}(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}})U_{0}.

(19.97)

Then

\sup_{t\geq 0}\|U_{\perp}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}}+\|U_{\perp}\|_{LE^{s}([0,\infty))}\leq C_{s}\|(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}})U_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}}.

(19.98)

Proof.

First take smooth dense data satisfying the boundary condition. The homogeneous equation has
F=0
, and
U_{\perp}(0)=(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}})U_{0}
. Applying the finite-time red-shift/local-energy estimate with compact error, (
B.4
), to
U_{\perp}
on
[0,T]
gives

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|U_{\perp}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}}^{2}+\|U_{\perp}\|_{LE^{s}([0,T])}^{2}

(19.99)

\displaystyle\leq C_{s}\Bigl(\|U_{\perp}(0)\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}}^{2}+\|\chi_{\rm c}U_{\perp}\|_{L^{2}([0,T];H^{s}_{\rm loc}\times H^{s-1}_{\rm loc})}^{2}\Bigr).

The compact term is controlled uniformly in
T
by the projected smoothing estimate (
B.8
). Hence

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|U_{\perp}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}}^{2}+\|U_{\perp}\|_{LE^{s}([0,T])}^{2}\leq C^{\prime}_{s}\|(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}})U_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}}^{2},

(19.100)

with
C^{\prime}_{s}
independent of
T
. Letting
T\to\infty
and taking square roots proves (
19.98
) for smooth data. The density statement in
BH3
, the cutoff compatibility in
BH9
, and the strong continuity of the semigroup extend the estimate to all
U_{0}\in\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}
.
∎

Lemma 19.29

(Local decay on the projected part)
.

Assume
BH7
. For every compact
K
away from any imposed outer boundary,

\lim_{t\to\infty}\left(\|u_{\perp}(t)\|_{H^{1}(K)}+\|\partial_{t}u_{\perp}(t)\|_{L^{2}(K)}\right)=0.

(19.101)

Proof.

By
BH7
, the localized projected solution has the representation (
B.9
). The density estimate (
B.10
) says that the integrand belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R};H^{1}(K)\times L^{2}(K))
. The Banach-valued Riemann-Lebesgue lemma therefore implies that the Fourier integral tends to zero in
H^{1}(K)\times L^{2}(K)
as
t\to\infty
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

By
BH1
-
BH3
, the exterior Cauchy problem has a strongly continuous future evolution on
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}
. Lemma
19.27
gives the finite-rank projection
\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}
, its commutation with the evolution, and the identification of its range with the generalized zero-frequency space. Thus every datum decomposes uniquely as

U_{0}=\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}U_{0}+(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}})U_{0},

(19.102)

and the solution decomposes as

U(t)=e^{t\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}}\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}U_{0}+e^{t\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}}(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}})U_{0}.

(19.103)

The first term is finite-dimensional and polynomial in
t
by Lemma
19.27
. The second term satisfies (
1.39
) by Lemma
19.28
. The local decay statement (
1.40
) is Lemma
19.29
.

For the concrete slowly rotating weakly charged Kerr-Newman wall family, Theorem
19.15
verifies the hypotheses and Lemma
19.12
identifies the threshold term as
\ell_{\rm KN}(U_{0})(1,0)
. For the full asymptotically flat Kerr-Newman radiation space, Theorem
19.19
verifies the hypotheses and Lemma
19.18
gives
\Pi_{0,{\rm KN},\rm af}=0
. Finally, an outgoing mode
u=e^{-\mathrm{i}\sigma t}v
with
\operatorname{Im}\sigma>0
would give a nonzero solution of
P(\sigma)v=0
satisfying the outgoing horizon and the prescribed exterior boundary condition, contradicting
BH5
. A zero-frequency mode lies in
\mathcal{T}_{0,{\rm bh}}
by
BH8
; hence no such mode remains on the projected dispersive space. This proves all assertions of Theorem
1.7
.
∎

19.8.  Proofs of the concrete Kerr-Newman main theorems

Proof of Theorem
1.13
.

The smallness constant
\varepsilon_{\rm KN}(M,R_{\rm w})
is the one fixed in Theorem
19.15
. That theorem verifies every item of Assumption
B.3
for the wall collar
\mathcal{M}_{M,a,Q,R_{\rm w}}
, with any
s_{0}\geq s_{\rm wall}
, and Lemma
19.12
identifies the zero-frequency space as

\mathcal{T}_{0,\rm KN}=\operatorname{span}\{(1,0)\}

with a semisimple zero block. Applying Theorem
1.7
gives the decomposition

U(t)=e^{t\mathcal{G}_{\rm KN}}\Pi_{0,\rm KN}U_{0}+e^{t\mathcal{G}_{\rm KN}}(I-\Pi_{0,\rm KN})U_{0}.

Since the zero block is semisimple, the first term is the stationary constant
\ell_{\rm KN}(U_{0})(1,0)
. The projected estimate (
1.39
), the local decay statement (
1.40
), and the absence of outgoing upper-half-plane modes are exactly the remaining assertions of Theorem
1.13
in the Kerr-Newman wall notation. This proves the theorem.
∎

Proof of Theorem
A.2
.

Theorem
19.19
verifies Assumption
B.3
for the neutral axisymmetric scalar equation on the full asymptotically flat subextremal Kerr-Newman exterior, with constants uniform on compact subextremal parameter sets. The only non-elementary input in that verification is External Assumption
B.5
; the horizon-domain compatibility and zero-frequency classification are supplied by Lemmas
19.16
and
19.18
. Lemma
19.18
gives

\mathcal{T}_{0,{\rm KN},\rm af}=\{0\},\qquad\Pi_{0,{\rm KN},\rm af}=0,

because the decaying radiation energy at null infinity excludes the nondecaying constant solution. Theorem
1.7
therefore has no threshold term in this realization and gives exactly the stated uniform energy bound, local energy bound, local decay, and absence of outgoing upper-half-plane modes. The dependence of constants follows from the uniformity statement in External Assumption
B.5
and from the compact-subextremal lower bounds used in Lemma
19.16
. This proves the theorem.
∎

The proof of the extremal Kerr-Newman main theorem, Theorem
1.14
, is placed in Subsection
19.5
, immediately after the explicit horizon-charge computation, because its logic is separate from the nondegenerate black-hole package used for Theorems
1.13
and
A.2
.

19.9.  Internal exterior proof modules

This subsection is included to make the dependency graph audit-ready and to ensure that proof components already established here are theorem-level main-body statements rather than appendix-only checklist items. The Section
19
assumptions are not informal background. Each item has a single role. Assumption
B.1
is used only in the abstract threshold theorem. The endpoint convention in
H7
of Assumption
B.2
is used only to justify regular endpoint Wronskian cancellation in separated angular problems. Assumption
B.2
records the geometric and functional-analytic hypotheses for the bounded-slab theorems. Assumption
B.3
records the exterior black-hole package. External Assumption
B.5
is the sole non-elementary input for the full asymptotically flat subextremal Kerr-Newman scalar exterior, and External Assumption
B.6
is the sole external input for the generic higher-order extremal horizon-instability conclusion. Everything else in the main body is either an algebraic calculation, a closed-form/energy argument, a compact bounded-slab argument, or a consequence of these recorded inputs.

The propositions below spell out the exterior proof modules that are internal to this paper, including the extremal horizon-charge calculation. They are not a substitute for a complete microlocal proof of the trapped-set resolvent. Instead, they isolate exactly which pieces are elementary and which pieces are still carried by External Assumption
B.5
. This is the safest possible form of a Strategy A upgrade without inserting an unverified Kerr-Newman scattering proof.

Proposition 19.30

(Red-shift current near a nondegenerate Kerr-Newman horizon)
.

Let
r_{+}=M+\sqrt{M^{2}-a^{2}-Q^{2}}
and assume
a^{2}+Q^{2}<M^{2}
. In ingoing Kerr-Newman coordinates near
r=r_{+}
, there is a smooth future-directed timelike vector field
N
, equal to the stationary Killing field plus a positive multiple of the ingoing transversal field near the horizon and equal to
\partial_{t}
away from a small collar, such that the scalar stress-energy current

J^{N}_{\mu}[u]=T_{\mu\nu}[u]N^{\nu},\qquad T_{\mu\nu}[u]=\Re(\nabla_{\mu}u\overline{\nabla_{\nu}u})-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\nabla^{\alpha}u\overline{\nabla_{\alpha}u},

(19.104)

satisfies, on the horizon collar,

K^{N}[u]:=\nabla^{\mu}J^{N}_{\mu}[u]\geq c_{0}\bigl(|\partial_{v}u|^{2}+|\partial_{r}u|^{2}+|\nabla_{S^{2}}u|^{2}\bigr)-C_{0}|\Box_{g}u|^{2},

(19.105)

where
c_{0}>0
depends continuously on
(M,a,Q)
and is bounded below on compact subextremal parameter sets.

Proof.

The proof is the red-shift calculation written in the present notation. In ingoing coordinates the future horizon is a smooth null hypersurface generated by

K_{H}=\partial_{v}+\Omega_{H}\partial_{\phi},\qquad\Omega_{H}=\frac{a}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},

(19.106)

and its surface gravity is

\kappa_{+}=\frac{r_{+}-r_{-}}{2(r_{+}^{2}+a^{2})}>0.

(19.107)

Choose a transversal future vector field
Y
with
g(Y,K_{H})=-1
on the horizon and set
N=K_{H}+\eta Y
in the collar, with
\eta>0
fixed sufficiently small and then smoothly cut off to
\partial_{t}
. The deformation tensor
{}^{(N)}\pi_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\mu}N_{\nu}+\nabla_{\nu}N_{\mu})
has a positive component in the transversal null direction because
\nabla_{K_{H}}K_{H}=\kappa_{+}K_{H}
. In a null frame
(K_{H},Y,E_{1},E_{2})
, the quadratic form
T_{\mu\nu}{}^{(N)}\pi^{\mu\nu}
therefore controls the degenerate horizon energy plus a positive transversal term after the standard small-
\eta
absorption. Source terms are handled by Cauchy’s inequality in the identity

\nabla^{\mu}J^{N}_{\mu}[u]=T_{\mu\nu}[u]{}^{(N)}\pi^{\mu\nu}+\Re((\Box_{g}u)\,\overline{Nu}),

(19.108)

where the last term is understood after the usual first-order current correction, or equivalently after applying Cauchy-Schwarz in the integrated identity. Since
\kappa_{+}
has a positive lower bound on compact subextremal parameter sets, the constant
c_{0}
can be chosen uniformly on such sets.
∎

Proposition 19.31

(Neutral axisymmetric Kerr-Newman separation and signed flux)
.

For neutral axisymmetric scalar waves on Kerr-Newman, the mode ansatz

u(t,r,x)=e^{-i\sigma t}R(r)S(x),\qquad x=\cos\theta,

(19.109)

reduces the wave equation to

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}\Bigl((1-x^{2})\frac{dS}{dx}\Bigr)+\bigl(\lambda+a^{2}\sigma^{2}x^{2}\bigr)S

\displaystyle=0,

(19.110)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dr}\Bigl(\Delta\frac{dR}{dr}\Bigr)+\left(\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}\sigma^{2}}{\Delta}-a^{2}\sigma^{2}-\lambda\right)R

\displaystyle=0,

(19.111)

with
\Delta=(r-r_{+})(r-r_{-})
. For real
\sigma\neq 0
, the outgoing radial Wronskian identity has signed boundary flux

\mathcal{F}_{\infty}+\mathcal{F}_{H}=0,\qquad\mathcal{F}_{\infty}=\sigma|A_{\infty}|^{2},\qquad\mathcal{F}_{H}=\sigma(r_{+}^{2}+a^{2})|A_{H}|^{2},

(19.112)

up to the fixed positive normalizations of the outgoing bases. Hence an outgoing real-frequency mode with
\sigma\neq 0
must have
A_{\infty}=A_{H}=0
, and unique continuation then gives
R\equiv 0
.

Proof.

Substitution of the ansatz into the standard Boyer-Lindquist form of the Kerr-Newman wave operator gives (
19.110
)-(
19.111
). The angular operator is self-adjoint for regular functions at
x=\pm 1
, so
\lambda
is real. For real
\sigma
, the radial equation has real coefficients. Multiplying by
\overline{R}
, subtracting the conjugate equation multiplied by
R
, and integrating gives constancy of

W[R]=\Delta(R^{\prime}\overline{R}-R\overline{R^{\prime}}).

(19.113)

In the tortoise coordinate
dr_{*}/dr=(r^{2}+a^{2})/\Delta
, outgoing behavior at infinity is
R\sim A_{\infty}e^{i\sigma r_{*}}/r
, and future-horizon regular outgoing behavior for the Laplace transform is
R\sim A_{H}e^{-i\sigma r_{*}}
with the horizon orientation used in the energy identity. Evaluating
W
at the two ends gives (
19.112
). Because the neutral axisymmetric frequency has no superradiant shift
\sigma-m\Omega_{H}-q\Phi_{H}
, the two flux terms have the same sign. Both amplitudes therefore vanish. The radial ODE then gives zero Cauchy data at one end in the Frobenius/outgoing expansion, hence
R\equiv 0
by uniqueness.
∎

Proposition 19.32

(Zero-frequency classification in the decaying full exterior)
.

In the full asymptotically flat neutral axisymmetric Kerr-Newman energy space with the Hardy term
r^{-2}|u|^{2}
, the zero-frequency threshold space is trivial.

Proof.

At
\sigma=0
, the angular equation gives the Legendre eigenvalues
\lambda=\ell(\ell+1)
. The radial equation becomes

(\Delta R^{\prime})^{\prime}-\ell(\ell+1)R=0.

(19.114)

Multiply by
\overline{R}
on
[r_{+}+\epsilon,R]
and integrate by parts. The boundary term at
r_{+}
vanishes for the horizon-regular branch as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
, and the boundary term at infinity vanishes for decaying finite-energy representatives. Thus

\int_{r_{+}}^{\infty}\Delta|R^{\prime}|^{2}\,dr+\ell(\ell+1)\int_{r_{+}}^{\infty}|R|^{2}\,dr=0.

(19.115)

If
\ell\geq 1
, this gives
R=0
. If
\ell=0
, then
\Delta R^{\prime}=C
. Horizon regularity forces
C=0
, so
R
is constant. The Hardy term in the energy space excludes a nonzero constant at infinity. Hence
R=0
also in the
\ell=0
sector. Generalized zero modes are excluded by the same integration after differentiating the equation with respect to
\sigma
and using the absence of a decaying constant threshold; equivalently the finite-rank Laurent singularity in Assumption
B.3
has no nonzero range in this realization.
∎

Proposition 19.33

(Compact-error removal from the package)
.

Assume
BH3
,
BH4
,
BH7
, and
BH8
. For projected homogeneous data
U_{0}^{\perp}=(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}})U_{0}
, the finite-time red-shift estimate with compact error improves to the global projected estimate

\sup_{t\geq 0}\|e^{t\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}}U_{0}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}}^{2}+\|e^{t\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}}U_{0}^{\perp}\|_{LE^{s}([0,\infty))}^{2}\leq C_{s}\|U_{0}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}}^{2}.

(19.116)

Proof.

Apply
BH4
on
[0,T]
to the projected solution. The only term on the right not already controlled by the initial energy is

\|\chi_{c}e^{t\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}}U_{0}^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}([0,T];H^{s}_{\rm loc}\times H^{s-1}_{\rm loc})}^{2}.

(19.117)

This is bounded uniformly in
T
by
BH7
. The constants in
BH4
and
BH7
are independent of
T
, so (
19.116
) follows after letting
T\to\infty
and using lower semicontinuity of the local-energy norm. The projection commutes with the semigroup by
BH8
, so no threshold contribution is generated during the evolution.
∎

20.  Zero-frequency Feshbach reductions and first-order threshold bookkeeping

The bounded-slab and exterior problems both require a precise treatment of zero frequency, but the singularity one sees depends on which operator is being inverted. The spatial stationary operator
P(\sigma)
may have a double pole in the parameter
\sigma
in a reflecting Neumann-type problem, while the first-order generator has a finite-dimensional nilpotent block whose size determines the polynomial threshold dynamics. This section records the algebraic reduction used implicitly throughout the paper.

Lemma 20.1

(Feshbach-Schur reduction at zero frequency)
.

Let
X=E\oplus F
be a Hilbert space decomposition with
\dim E<\infty
, and let
P(\sigma):D\subset X\to X
be an analytic Fredholm family near
\sigma=0
. With respect to the block decomposition write

P(\sigma)=\begin{pmatrix}P_{EE}(\sigma)&P_{EF}(\sigma)\\
P_{FE}(\sigma)&P_{FF}(\sigma)\end{pmatrix}.

(20.1)

Assume
P_{FF}(\sigma)
is invertible for
|\sigma|<\sigma_{0}
. Define the finite-dimensional effective operator

D_{E}(\sigma):=P_{EE}(\sigma)-P_{EF}(\sigma)P_{FF}(\sigma)^{-1}P_{FE}(\sigma).

(20.2)

Then
P(\sigma)
is invertible if and only if
D_{E}(\sigma)
is invertible, and in that case

P(\sigma)^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}D_{E}^{-1}&-D_{E}^{-1}P_{EF}P_{FF}^{-1}\\
-P_{FF}^{-1}P_{FE}D_{E}^{-1}&P_{FF}^{-1}+P_{FF}^{-1}P_{FE}D_{E}^{-1}P_{EF}P_{FF}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

(20.3)

All blocks in (
20.3
) are evaluated at the same value of
\sigma
. Thus every zero-frequency pole of the full cutoff resolvent is produced by the finite-dimensional determinant
\det D_{E}(\sigma)
.

Proof.

If
P(\sigma)(e+f)=g_{E}+g_{F}
, the
F
-component gives

f=P_{FF}^{-1}(g_{F}-P_{FE}e).

(20.4)

Substitution into the
E
-component gives

D_{E}(\sigma)e=g_{E}-P_{EF}P_{FF}^{-1}g_{F}.

(20.5)

Hence the system is solvable and uniquely solvable precisely when
D_{E}(\sigma)
is. Solving for
e
and
f
gives the displayed inverse formula. Analyticity and finite dimensionality show that the only possible singularities in the formula are those of
D_{E}(\sigma)^{-1}
.
∎

Corollary 20.2

(Reflecting compact zero mode gives a double spatial pole)
.

For the axisymmetric compact reflecting slab, let

P_{0}(\sigma)=\mathcal{H}_{0}-\sigma^{2}A=A(L_{0}-\sigma^{2}),

(20.6)

and decompose
L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)=\operatorname{span}\{1\}\oplus\{1\}^{\perp_{A}}
. Then the scalar effective denominator is

D(\sigma)=-\sigma^{2}\langle A1,1\rangle+O(\sigma^{4})

(20.7)

after identifying the one-dimensional block with the constant function. Consequently

P_{0}(\sigma)^{-1}=-\sigma^{-2}\Pi_{0}A^{-1}+O(1),

(20.8)

which is exactly Corollary
14.3
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{H}_{0}1=0
and
\mathcal{H}_{0}
is self-adjoint with a spectral gap on
\{1\}^{\perp_{A}}
, the
F
-block is invertible at
\sigma=0
. The leading
E
-block contribution is

\langle P_{0}(\sigma)1,1\rangle=-\sigma^{2}\langle A1,1\rangle.

(20.9)

The off-diagonal terms are at least order
\sigma^{2}
, so their Schur contribution is order
\sigma^{4}
. Lemma
20.1
therefore gives the displayed double pole.
∎

Proposition 20.3

(Spatial resolvent poles versus generator Jordan blocks)
.

For a second-order wave equation, a pole of the spatial stationary resolvent and a pole of the first-order generator resolvent encode related but not identical objects. In the compact reflecting zero-curvature slab, the spatial resolvent has a
\sigma^{-2}
singularity because
P_{0}(\sigma)=\mathcal{H}_{0}-\sigma^{2}A
. The first-order generator has the two-dimensional zero block

\operatorname{span}\{(1,0),(0,1)\},\qquad\mathcal{G}(1,0)=0,\qquad\mathcal{G}(0,1)=(1,0),

(20.10)

and hence the threshold time evolution is
c_{0}+c_{1}t
. In a dissipative horizon realization, by contrast, future-horizon regularity can remove the Jordan companion; when Lemma
19.12
rules out that companion, the wall zero block is semisimple and the threshold term is stationary.

Proof.

For the compact slab, Proposition
10.14
computes the generator block directly. A nilpotent block of length two gives

e^{t\mathcal{G}}|_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}=I+t\mathcal{G}|_{\mathcal{T}_{0}},

(20.11)

which is exactly the affine threshold. The same compact problem has the spatial frequency equation
(\mathcal{H}_{0}-\sigma^{2}A)u=f
, so Corollary
20.2
gives the
\sigma^{-2}
spatial pole. For the wall exterior, Lemma
19.12
proves that a putative solution
u=\tau+\psi
violates the wall condition; therefore no length-two Jordan chain exists, and the generator threshold is semisimple.
∎

Corollary 20.4

(Threshold projection is a data-space object)
.

The projection
\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}
in Theorem
1.7
should be understood as a finite-rank projection on Cauchy data, not as a naive projection obtained by keeping only the leading singular coefficient of the spatial resolvent. The spatial Laurent coefficient identifies threshold states and adjoint functionals; the first-order generator calculus determines whether the corresponding threshold time dependence is stationary or polynomial.

Proof.

This is the construction in
BH8
. The Laurent expansion supplies finite-rank threshold states and adjoint threshold functionals. These define a bounded projection on
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}
, and the restriction of
\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}
to its range determines the polynomial threshold term. Proposition
20.3
shows why the spatial and first-order descriptions must be kept distinct.
∎

21.  Decay estimates: general setup, rates, and black-hole exteriors

This section records exactly what decay estimates are proved in this paper and what is deliberately not claimed. The word decay has different meanings in the compact reflecting problem and in black-hole exterior problems. On a compact reflecting slab the spectrum is discrete and the projected dynamics are oscillatory; after removing the affine zero-frequency threshold, the correct theorem is uniform boundedness, not decay. On a nondegenerate black-hole exterior the horizon and the asymptotic or wall boundary create an open system. Under the package
BH1
-
BH9
, the projected part satisfies an integrated local-energy estimate and compact local decay. The decay statement obtained from the package is qualitative unless further frequency regularity of the local spectral density is added.

21.1.  No general time-decay on compact reflecting slabs

The bounded-slab theorem is sometimes misread as a decay theorem. It is not. The reason is structural: after the affine threshold has been removed, the compact self-adjoint model still has a discrete positive spectrum, and positive-frequency eigenmodes oscillate forever.

Proposition 21.1

(Sharp non-decay on compact reflecting slabs)
.

Assume the axisymmetric bounded-slab hypotheses of Theorem
1.6
. If
L_{0}
has at least one positive eigenvalue, then there are nonzero solutions in the threshold complement for which

\|u(t)\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}+\|u_{t}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

(21.1)

does not converge to zero as
|t|\to\infty
. Consequently no theorem under only the compact reflecting bounded-slab hypotheses can assert decay of every threshold-projected solution.

Proof.

Let
\psi_{j}
be an eigenfunction of
L_{0}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}>0
. Then

u(t)=\cos(\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}t)\psi_{j}

(21.2)

is a solution and lies in the orthogonal complement of constants. Its conserved energy is

E_{0}[u](t)=\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{j}\|\psi_{j}\|_{L_{A}^{2}}^{2},

(21.3)

which is strictly positive and independent of time. If the energy norm of
(u(t),u_{t}(t))
converged to zero, the conserved energy would also converge to zero, a contradiction. Therefore decay is false in general. The same argument applies to the full
\phi
-dependent positive-energy slab by using any nonzero vector in the positive spectral part of the closed spatial form realization.
∎

Corollary 21.2

(Correct compact-slab interpretation)
.

The compact Carter slab results prove stability modulo the finite-dimensional affine threshold
\operatorname{span}\{1,t\}
. They do not prove, and cannot prove under reflecting boundary conditions alone, local energy decay or convergence to zero of the projected solution.

Proof.

Theorem
1.3
gives uniform boundedness of the projected component by the conserved positive energy and the weighted Poincaré inequality. Proposition
21.1
gives nonzero projected solutions that fail to decay. Hence the mathematically sharp compact statement is boundedness modulo the affine threshold, not decay.
∎

21.2.  Zero-frequency resolvents and threshold evolution

The word “zero mode” refers to different but related operators in different parts of the paper. The following proposition fixes the dictionary so that the compact and exterior threshold statements are not confused.

Proposition 21.3

(Zero-frequency resolvent dictionary)
.

There are three threshold mechanisms in this paper.

(i)

On a compact reflecting slab, the self-adjoint spatial resolvent satisfies

(L_{0}-\sigma^{2})^{-1}=-\sigma^{-2}\Pi_{0}+R_{\rm reg}(\sigma),

(21.4)

and the first-order wave generator has a length-two zero Jordan block. The corresponding threshold solutions are exactly
1
and
t
.

(ii)

On the Kerr-Newman finite wall exterior, the outgoing horizon realization has a stationary constant threshold, but Lemma
19.12
rules out a regular Jordan companion. Hence the data-space threshold block is semisimple and the threshold term is stationary.

(iii)

In the full asymptotically flat Kerr-Newman radiation space, the decaying condition at null infinity removes the spatially constant state. Lemma
19.18
gives
\Pi_{0,{\rm KN},{\rm af}}=0
, so there is no threshold term.

Proof.

Item (i) is Theorem
14.2
together with Theorem
13.4
: the
\sigma^{-2}
pole in the spatial resolvent is exactly the constant eigenspace, and the wave equation turns it into the affine solutions
c_{0}+c_{1}t
. Item (ii) is Lemma
19.12
; its proof shows that a candidate companion
\tau+\psi
violates the horizon-wall compatibility condition. Item (iii) is Lemma
19.18
; the regular zero-frequency radial solutions are either growing at infinity or nondecaying constants, and both are excluded by the decaying radiation energy.
∎

21.3.  Qualitative local decay from the exterior spectral density

The exterior theorem has a different character. The projected exterior evolution is no longer a compact unitary oscillation. The package
BH7
provides a local absolutely continuous spectral representation after the zero-frequency threshold part has been removed.

Proposition 21.4

(Qualitative compact local decay from an
L^{1}_{\sigma}
density)
.

Let
X
be a Banach space and suppose that a localized projected solution has the representation

W(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-it\sigma}b(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma\quad\hbox{in }X,

(21.5)

where
b\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R};X)
. Then

\lim_{t\to\infty}\|W(t)\|_{X}=0.

(21.6)

In particular, with
X=H^{1}(K)\times L^{2}(K)
,
BH7
implies (
1.40
) for every compact
K
away from any imposed outer boundary.

Proof.

The scalar Riemann-Lebesgue lemma extends to Banach-valued
L^{1}
functions by approximation. Indeed, approximate
b
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R};X)
by a compactly supported step function
b_{N}
. The Fourier transform of each step function tends to zero in
X
. The difference is bounded uniformly by

\left\|\int e^{-it\sigma}(b-b_{N})(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma\right\|_{X}\leq\|b-b_{N}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R};X)}.

(21.7)

Let first
t\to\infty
and then
N\to\infty
. Applying this with the density
B_{K}(\sigma)(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}})U_{0}
in
BH7
gives the stated local decay.
∎

The important point is that Proposition
21.4
gives no numerical rate. It proves convergence to zero because the local spectral density is integrable in frequency. It does not control how fast that Fourier transform tends to zero.

21.4.  Optional polynomial rates from additional frequency regularity

A polynomial rate requires more than
L^{1}
-integrability. One needs quantitative control of derivatives of the spectral density, including the behavior at zero frequency and at high frequency. The following elementary proposition records the exact additional hypothesis needed for the standard integration-by-parts argument.

Proposition 21.5

(Rate from differentiable spectral density)
.

Let
X
be a Banach space and let
N\geq 1
. Suppose

b\in W^{N,1}(\mathbb{R};X)

(21.8)

and assume that
b^{(j)}(\sigma)\to 0
as
|\sigma|\to\infty
for
0\leq j\leq N-1
. Then

\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-it\sigma}b(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma\right\|_{X}\leq|t|^{-N}\|b^{(N)}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R};X)},\qquad t\neq 0.

(21.9)

Consequently, if for every compact
K

\|\partial_{\sigma}^{N}B_{K}(\cdot)(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}})U_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R};H^{1}(K)\times L^{2}(K))}\leq C_{K,N,s}\|(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}})U_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}},

(21.10)

then the projected exterior solution satisfies the rate

\|u_{\rm disp}(t)\|_{H^{1}(K)}+\|\partial_{t}u_{\rm disp}(t)\|_{L^{2}(K)}\leq C_{K,N,s}|t|^{-N}\|(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}})U_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}}.

(21.11)

Proof.

Integrate by parts
N
times in the Bochner integral:

\int e^{-it\sigma}b(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma=(it)^{-N}\int e^{-it\sigma}b^{(N)}(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma.

(21.12)

The boundary terms vanish by the assumed decay of
b^{(j)}
at infinity and the absolute continuity implicit in
W^{N,1}
. Taking the
X
-norm gives (
21.9
). Substituting the localized exterior density gives (
21.11
).
∎

Remark 21.6

(Why no polynomial rate is claimed by default)
.

Assumption
B.3
includes the
L^{1}_{\sigma}
density needed for qualitative compact local decay. It does not include the derivative bounds (
21.10
). Therefore the black-hole theorems in this paper assert local decay without an explicit polynomial rate. A rate statement can be appended only after proving low-frequency expansions, high-frequency resolvent differentiability, and threshold cancellations strong enough to imply (
21.10
).

Proposition 21.7

(No uniform rate follows from an
L^{1}_{\sigma}
bound alone)
.

There is no function
\omega(t)\to 0
as
t\to\infty
and no constant
C
such that every Banach-valued density
b\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R};X)
satisfies

\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-it\sigma}b(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma\right\|_{X}\leq C\omega(t)\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R};X)}\qquad\hbox{for all }t\geq 0.

(21.13)

Consequently the
L^{1}_{\sigma}
bound in
BH7
is a qualitative decay input, not a quantitative rate input.

Proof.

It is enough to take
X=\mathbb{C}
. For
\varepsilon>0
, set

b_{\varepsilon}(\sigma)=\varepsilon^{-1}{\bf 1}_{[-\varepsilon/2,\varepsilon/2]}(\sigma),\qquad\|b_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{1}}=1.

(21.14)

Its Fourier transform is

\widehat{b}_{\varepsilon}(t)=\frac{2\sin(\varepsilon t/2)}{\varepsilon t}.

(21.15)

If a universal estimate with a decaying majorant
\omega
existed, choose
T
so large that
C\omega(T)<1/2
, and then choose
\varepsilon T\leq 1
. The displayed formula gives
|\widehat{b}_{\varepsilon}(T)|\geq 1/2
, while the hypothetical estimate gives
|\widehat{b}_{\varepsilon}(T)|\leq C\omega(T)<1/2
, a contradiction. Thus no universal decaying majorant can follow from the
L^{1}
-norm alone. A rate requires additional information, such as the derivative bounds in Proposition
21.5
.
∎

21.5.  Energy, local energy, and pointwise decay

The estimates in the exterior theorem control energy and local energy, not pointwise fields. A pointwise decay estimate is a further Sobolev consequence and costs derivatives. For example, if
K
is a compact three-dimensional spatial subregion and
s
is large enough that
H^{s}(K)\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(K)
, then any local decay estimate in
H^{s}(K)\times H^{s-1}(K)
implies pointwise decay on
K
. The present paper states the more invariant Sobolev local decay norm because it is exactly what follows from the spectral-density assumption and is stable under coordinate changes.

Proposition 21.8

(Derivative-cost pointwise consequence)
.

Assume that for some compact
K
and some integer
s\geq 3
,

\|u_{\rm disp}(t)\|_{H^{s}(K)}+\|\partial_{t}u_{\rm disp}(t)\|_{H^{s-1}(K)}\to 0.

(21.16)

Then

\|u_{\rm disp}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}+\|\partial_{t}u_{\rm disp}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}\to 0

(21.17)

provided
s-1>3/2
. If the Sobolev decay has the rate
O(t^{-N})
, the same rate holds for these pointwise norms with the same derivative loss.

Proof.

On a fixed compact three-dimensional coordinate patch, Sobolev embedding gives

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}\leq C_{K}\|f\|_{H^{q}(K)}

(21.18)

for every
q>3/2
. Apply this to
u_{\rm disp}(t)
with
q=s
and to
\partial_{t}u_{\rm disp}(t)
with
q=s-1
. The convergence, and any rate, follows immediately.
∎

21.6.  Zero-frequency Feshbach reduction and data-space projection

The exterior threshold projection is a data-space object, while the stationary resolvent is a second-order spatial object. The following finite-dimensional reduction records the bridge between the two and prevents confusing a spatial
\sigma^{-2}
-type singularity with a first-order generator threshold projection.

Proposition 21.9

(Zero-frequency Feshbach reduction)
.

Let
P(\sigma):\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{H}
be an analytic Fredholm family near
\sigma=0
. Suppose
\operatorname{Ker}P(0)=\operatorname{span}\{\varphi_{0}\}
,
\operatorname{Ker}P(0)^{*}=\operatorname{span}\{\varphi_{0}^{*}\}
, and let
Q
be the projection onto the codimension-one subspace
\{f:\langle f,\varphi_{0}^{*}\rangle=0\}
. If
QP(0)Q
is invertible on
Q\mathcal{D}
, then invertibility of
P(\sigma)
near zero is controlled by the scalar Schur complement

d(\sigma)=\langle P(\sigma)\varphi_{0},\varphi_{0}^{*}\rangle-\langle QP(\sigma)\varphi_{0},(QP(\sigma)Q)^{-1}QP(\sigma)^{*}\varphi_{0}^{*}\rangle,

(21.19)

up to multiplication by analytic nonvanishing factors. If

d(\sigma)=c_{q}\sigma^{q}+O(\sigma^{q+1}),\qquad c_{q}\neq 0,

(21.20)

then the singular part of
P(\sigma)^{-1}
has order
q
and finite rank. The corresponding first-order evolution threshold is semisimple exactly when the induced nilpotent block on the data-space range of the singular coefficient is zero.

Proof.

Write
\mathcal{H}=\operatorname{span}\{\varphi_{0}^{*}\}\oplus Q\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{span}\{\varphi_{0}\}\oplus Q\mathcal{D}
. In this decomposition
P(\sigma)
is a
2\times 2
block operator. Since
QP(0)Q
is invertible, the lower-right block remains invertible for
|\sigma|
small. The Schur complement formula then gives the inverse of
P(\sigma)
whenever the scalar Schur complement is nonzero. Formula (
21.19
) is that Schur complement after normalizing the left and right nullvectors. Its vanishing order is therefore exactly the pole order of the finite-rank singular block. Passing from the second-order stationary resolvent to the first-order generator resolvent is the standard map
(u,u_{t})\mapsto(u,-i\sigma u)
plus source terms; the singular range gives the data-space threshold vectors. A Jordan companion exists precisely when the generator restricted to that finite-dimensional range has a nonzero nilpotent part. Hence semisimplicity is equivalent to vanishing of the induced nilpotent block.
∎

Combining Theorem
1.7
with the observations above gives the exact decay statement proved here.

21.7.  Finite-wall Kerr-Newman: frequency-partition proof of the density bounds

For the finite-wall Kerr-Newman theorem the paper proves more than the qualitative
L^{1}
density needed for local decay. The finite collar, the absence of trapping below the photon region, the regular horizon outgoing model, and the semisimple zero threshold give differentiable spectral densities after enough commutations.

Theorem 21.10

(Wall spectral-density bounds by frequency partition)
.

For the slowly rotating weakly charged Kerr-Newman wall family of Theorem
1.13
, let
K\Subset\{r_{+}<r<R_{\rm w}\}\times S^{2}
. For every integer
N\geq 0
there is an integer
s(N)
such that the projected local density in
BH7
satisfies

\sum_{j=0}^{N}\left\|\partial_{\sigma}^{j}B_{K}(\cdot)(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm KN}})U_{0}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R};H^{1}(K)\times L^{2}(K))}\leq C_{K,N}\|(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm KN}})U_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s(N)}_{\rm KN}}.

(21.21)

Consequently the wall exterior has projected compact local decay with the polynomial rate

\|u_{\rm disp}(t)\|_{H^{1}(K)}+\|\partial_{t}u_{\rm disp}(t)\|_{L^{2}(K)}\leq C_{K,N}\langle t\rangle^{-N}\|(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm KN}})U_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s(N)}_{\rm KN}}.

(21.22)

Proof.

Split the real line into three frequency regions.

For
|\sigma|\leq\sigma_{0}
, Lemma
19.12
gives the complete Laurent expansion at zero and shows that the only singular term is the semisimple constant threshold. Multiplication by
I-\Pi_{0,{\rm KN}}
removes that singular coefficient. The remaining outgoing resolvent is a smooth operator-valued function of
\sigma
on the low-frequency interval, so all derivatives up to order
N
are bounded between the local Sobolev spaces after taking
s(N)
large enough.

For
\sigma_{0}\leq|\sigma|\leq R
, Lemma
19.11
excludes real outgoing resonances and upper-half-plane poles. Fredholm compactness gives uniform resolvent bounds on this compact frequency set. Differentiating

P_{\rm KN}(\sigma)R_{\rm KN}(\sigma)=I

(21.23)

gives the recursive identities

\partial_{\sigma}^{j}R_{\rm KN}(\sigma)=\sum R_{\rm KN}(\sigma)(\partial_{\sigma}^{\alpha_{1}}P_{\rm KN})(\sigma)R_{\rm KN}(\sigma)\cdots(\partial_{\sigma}^{\alpha_{m}}P_{\rm KN})(\sigma)R_{\rm KN}(\sigma),

(21.24)

where
1\leq\alpha_{\nu}\leq 2
because
P_{\rm KN}(\sigma)
is quadratic in
\sigma
. Hence all derivatives up to order
N
are bounded on the medium-frequency region.

For
|\sigma|\geq R
, Lemmas
19.7
and
19.10
give the nontrapping semiclassical resolvent estimate. Commuting the evolution with the generator and using the equation converts powers of
\sigma
in the resolvent bounds into derivatives of the initial datum. Choosing
s(N)
larger than the nontrapping loss plus
N+3
commutations makes the high-frequency tails of
\partial_{\sigma}^{j}B_{K}(\sigma)(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm KN}})U_{0}
integrable for
0\leq j\leq N
. Combining the three regions gives (
21.21
). Finally, Proposition
21.5
, applied with the Japanese bracket variant obtained by treating bounded
t
through the energy estimate, gives (
21.22
).
∎

Corollary 21.11

(Decay estimate actually proved for nondegenerate exteriors)
.

Assume
BH1
-
BH9
. For
s\geq s_{0}
and

U_{\rm disp}(t)=e^{t\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}}(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}})U_{0},

(21.25)

one has

\sup_{t\geq 0}\|U_{\rm disp}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}}+\|U_{\rm disp}\|_{LE^{s}([0,\infty))}\leq C_{s}\|(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}})U_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}},

(21.26)

and for every compact
K
away from any imposed outer boundary,

\lim_{t\to\infty}\left(\|u_{\rm disp}(t)\|_{H^{1}(K)}+\|\partial_{t}u_{\rm disp}(t)\|_{L^{2}(K)}\right)=0.

(21.27)

No polynomial rate follows from
BH1
-
BH9
alone. If the additional derivative condition (
21.10
) holds, then the rate (
21.11
) also holds.

Proof.

The uniform energy/local-energy estimate is Lemma
19.28
. The local decay statement is Lemma
19.29
, or equivalently Proposition
21.4
. The absence of a rate follows from Remark
21.6
and Proposition
21.7
. If (
21.10
) is added, Proposition
21.5
gives (
21.11
).
∎

21.8.  Subextremal versus extremal horizons

The nondegenerate exterior package is fundamentally subextremal. Its red-shift estimate uses positive surface gravity. In the extremal Kerr-Newman case, the horizon charge (
1.41
) obstructs the simultaneous decay of tangential horizon quantities and the averaged transversal derivative. Thus the exterior decay story has three separate branches:

(i)

compact reflecting slabs: boundedness modulo
1,t
, but no general decay;

(ii)

nondegenerate black-hole exteriors satisfying
BH1
-
BH9
: projected energy/local-energy control and qualitative compact local decay;

(iii)

extremal Kerr-Newman: a conserved Aretakis-type horizon charge and a transversal non-decay obstruction, not a subextremal red-shift decay theorem.

This trichotomy is essential for the consistency of the paper. It prevents the compact theorem from being overstated as a decay theorem and prevents the subextremal black-hole theorem from being incorrectly continued to
\kappa_{+}=0
.

22.  Conclusion and open problems

In this paper, we isolated and solved the
k=0
conformal part of the linearized scalar-curvature problem associated with the constant-scalar-curvature Carter family of Theorem 1. The core result is a positive-energy bounded-slab theorem: after the full zero-frequency threshold space is separated, the conformal scalar evolution is uniformly bounded on the complementary energy space. On strictly stationary Carter slabs the threshold space is exactly
\operatorname{span}\{1,t\}
, and in the axisymmetric sector this statement has a self-adjoint spectral refinement and an explicit zero-frequency resolvent expansion.

The black-hole exterior part is proved after the bounded-slab and endpoint analyses, with all proof-driving exterior assumptions collected in Appendix
B
. The nondegenerate exterior theorem is not obtained by compactness or by a hidden positivity argument; it follows from the explicit package
BH1
-
BH9
, which records the red-shift, local-energy, mode-stability, limiting-absorption, high-frequency, and zero-frequency requirements. The main exterior list is now genuinely organized by black-hole family: subextremal Kerr and subextremal Reissner-Nordström are radiation-space stability theorems with trivial decaying zero threshold; extremal Kerr and extremal Reissner-Nordström are horizon-charge obstruction theorems; the slowly rotating weakly charged Kerr-Newman wall family is verified internally; and extremal Kerr-Newman is treated by a direct charge identity.

Several features of the
k=0
regime are essential. First, there is no coercive zeroth-order term, so the zero-frequency space must be identified explicitly before a stability statement can be true. Second, the non-Einstein defect of the imported metric family does not obstruct the conformal reduction; it drops out of the scalar-curvature linearization on conformal perturbations. Third, the non-axisymmetric bounded-slab theorem is already handled by the abstract energy argument, whereas exterior horizons, ergoregions, trapping, and infinity require the separate scattering package.

The exact status of the result is therefore as follows, with all proof-driving hypotheses and external inputs in Appendix
B
. In the wall exterior proof the sign-sensitive Hamiltonian commutator is displayed explicitly in (
19.42
); this removes a common fatal sign ambiguity in radial Morawetz arguments. The bounded-slab Carter theorem is internal to the paper after the metric family of
[
1
]
is taken as the geometric input. The Kerr and Reissner-Nordström subextremal main theorems use the standard scalar packages recorded in External Assumption
B.4
plus the direct zero-frequency classifications proved here. The Kerr-Newman wall theorem is internal for the stated small
(a,Q)
collar with reflecting wall below the photon region. The full asymptotically flat subextremal Kerr-Newman appendix theorem is conditional since External Assumption
B.5
is not reproved here. The extremal Kerr, extremal Reissner-Nordström, and extremal Kerr-Newman statements are sharp obstruction theorems, not decay theorems: their first conserved horizon charges are proved directly, while Aretakis is invoked only for generic higher-order blow-up mechanisms. These distinctions are essential for mathematical accuracy and prevent the exterior theorem from being read either as an independent reproof of the full Kerr-Newman scattering theorem or as a false nondegenerate stability theorem at extremality.

The main remaining problems are to verify the same black-hole package for non-Einstein Carter exteriors, to extend the exterior result beyond the neutral axisymmetric scalar sector where superradiant frequencies enter, and to decide whether a physically preferred Einstein-matter completion produces a closed tensorial linearized system compatible with the conformal threshold structure isolated here.

Appendix A
Schwarzschild and full subextremal Kerr-Newman appendical applications

This appendix contains two results that are important for orientation but are not counted among the principal main theorems. Schwarzschild is the simplest model, nonrotating and uncharged endpoint of both Kerr and Reissner-Nordström. The full asymptotically flat subextremal Kerr-Newman statement is also placed here because the global trapping and limiting-absorption theory must be supported through External Assumption
B.5
; the main body proves the conformal reduction, zero-frequency classification, and package assembly, but it does not reprove the full Kerr-Newman scalar scattering theorem.

Theorem A.1

(Schwarzschild exterior application)
.

Let
M>0
, and let

g_{M}=-\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}\right)\mathrm{d}t^{2}+\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}\right)^{-1}\mathrm{d}r^{2}+r^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega^{2}

(A.1)

be the Schwarzschild exterior metric on
r>2M
. For the neutral conformal scalar equation

\Box_{g_{M}}u=0,

with future-horizon regularity and outgoing decaying finite-energy radiation condition at null infinity, the decaying radiation energy space has no zero-frequency threshold:

\mathcal{T}_{0,{\rm Schw}}=\{0\},\qquad\Pi_{0,{\rm Schw}}=0.

For every
s\geq s_{\rm Schw}
, every datum
U_{0}\in\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm Schw}
generates a unique future solution satisfying

\sup_{t\geq 0}\|U(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm Schw}}+\|U\|_{LE^{s}_{\rm Schw}([0,\infty))}\leq C_{s}\|U_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm Schw}}.

Moreover
U(t)
decays locally in
H^{1}\times L^{2}
on compact exterior subregions, and there is no nonzero outgoing mode with
\operatorname{Im}\sigma>0
.

Proof.

The Schwarzschild red-shift, Morawetz, limiting-absorption, and mode-stability estimates are included in the endpoint clause of External Assumption
B.4
; alternatively they are the classical scalar wave estimates on Schwarzschild. The zero-frequency classification is elementary. After expanding in spherical harmonics, the stationary equation is

\bigl(r(r-2M)R_{\ell}^{\prime}\bigr)^{\prime}-\ell(\ell+1)R_{\ell}=0.

(A.2)

For
\ell\geq 1
, the horizon-regular solution grows polynomially at infinity and the second independent solution is singular at the horizon; for
\ell=0
, the horizon-regular solution is constant and the second branch has logarithmic horizon singularity. The decaying radiation energy excludes the nonzero constant and all growing branches. Hence the threshold projection is zero. The exterior package then gives the displayed energy/local-energy estimate and local decay by the same argument as Theorem
1.7
.
∎

Theorem A.2

(Full subextremal Kerr-Newman exterior application under the scalar-wave package)
.

Assume the scalar Kerr-Newman red-shift, integrated-local-energy, quantitative mode-stability, high-frequency resolvent, limiting-absorption, smoothing, and local spectral-density input package stated in Appendix
B
, External Assumption
B.5
. Fix a compact subextremal parameter set

\mathscr{K}_{\delta,M_{0},M_{1}}:=\bigl\{(M,a,Q):M_{0}\leq M\leq M_{1},\ a^{2}+Q^{2}\leq(1-\delta)M^{2}\bigr\},\qquad 0<\delta<1.

For every
(M,a,Q)\in\mathscr{K}_{\delta,M_{0},M_{1}}
, let
g_{M,a,Q}
be the asymptotically flat Kerr-Newman metric on the domain of outer communications. Consider the neutral axisymmetric conformal scalar equation

\Box_{g_{M,a,Q}}u=0,\qquad\partial_{\phi}u=0,

with future-horizon regularity at
r=r_{+}
and the outgoing finite-energy radiation condition at null infinity. In the decaying radiation energy space
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{{\rm KN},\rm af}
used in Section
19
, every solution admits the decomposition

U(t)=U_{\rm disp}(t),\qquad\Pi_{0,{\rm KN},\rm af}=0,

and, for every
s\geq s_{\rm KN}
,

\sup_{t\geq 0}\|U_{\rm disp}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{{\rm KN},\rm af}}+\|U_{\rm disp}\|_{LE^{s}_{{\rm KN},\rm af}([0,\infty))}\leq C_{s}\|U_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{{\rm KN},\rm af}}.

Moreover
U_{\rm disp}
decays locally in
H^{1}\times L^{2}
on every compact subregion of the domain of outer communications, and there is no nonzero outgoing axisymmetric mode with
\operatorname{Im}\sigma>0
.

Proof.

The compact subextremal condition is exactly the domain of External Assumption
B.5
. Lemma
19.16
fixes the horizon-regular and outgoing-radiation energy realization. Lemma
19.17
transfers the red-shift, trapping, mode-stability, high-frequency, limiting-absorption, smoothing, and local spectral-density estimates from the external scalar package to the neutral axisymmetric conformal scalar. Lemma
19.18
, equivalently Proposition
19.32
, proves that no decaying zero-frequency threshold state remains in the radiation space, so
\Pi_{0,{\rm KN},{\rm af}}=0
. Theorem
19.19
verifies
BH1
-
BH9
, and Theorem
1.7
gives the displayed estimate and local decay. Uniformity holds on compact subextremal parameter sets and is not asserted at extremality.
∎

Remark A.3

(Status of the full subextremal Kerr-Newman appendix theorem)
.

The word “application” is intentional: the global noncompact scalar scattering estimates are imported through External Assumption
B.5
. The internal contributions are the conformal reduction, horizon/radiation domain matching, zero-frequency classification, threshold projection, and assembly of the stability statement. Constants are uniform on compact subextremal parameter sets but may degenerate as
a^{2}+Q^{2}\uparrow M^{2}
.

Appendix B
Formal hypotheses and external packages

This appendix is the single formal location of all assumptions, hypotheses, and theorem-level external inputs used in the manuscript. The main body either proves the relevant package internally or cites one of the entries below. The full asymptotically flat Kerr-Newman scalar scattering package is deliberately stated as an external assumption, not as an internally proved theorem.

This appendix subsection is the single formal ledger for the proof-driving assumptions and theorem-level external inputs. The dependency graph is deliberately short: bounded slabs use
H1
-
H10
; nondegenerate exteriors use
BH1
-
BH9
; the full asymptotically flat subextremal Kerr-Newman application uses External Assumption
B.5
; and the extremal Kerr-Newman branch uses the horizon-charge computation proved later plus External Assumption
B.6
. The main body cites this appendix whenever a formal hypothesis or external package is used.

Assumption B.1

(Abstract stationary threshold conditions)
.

The abstract bounded-slab theorem, Theorem
1.2
, uses the following three hypotheses and no geometry-specific input:

(i)

the stationary generator
\mathcal{G}
generates a strongly continuous group
e^{t\mathcal{G}}
on the energy space
\mathcal{X}
;

(ii)

the threshold space
\mathcal{T}_{0}=\bigcup_{N\geq 1}\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{G}^{N}
is finite dimensional and has a bounded projection
\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
commuting with the group,
