e^{t\mathcal{G}}\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}=\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}e^{t\mathcal{G}};

(iii)

the conserved energy
\mathscr{E}
is equivalent to the
\mathcal{X}
-norm on
\operatorname{Ker}\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}
:

c\|U\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\leq\mathscr{E}[U]\leq C\|U\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2},\qquad U\in\operatorname{Ker}\Pi_{\mathrm{thr}}.

(B.1)

Assumption B.2

(Bounded-slab conditions)
.

The Carter bounded-slab theorems use the following ten conditions. The first five are the ones cited directly in Theorem
1.3
; the remaining items identify the auxiliary spectral, endpoint, and scope conventions.

H1.

Imported background and zero scalar curvature.

The metric is the Carter-form constant-scalar-curvature family of
[
1
]
, with coefficients (
2.3
)-(
2.4
), and every stability theorem imposes
k=0
, so
\Delta_{r}
and
\Delta_{x}
are the quadratics (
2.11
). No stability theorem is imported with the metric family.

H2.

Regular bounded product slab.

The slab is

\Omega=[r_{-},r_{+}]\times[x_{-},x_{+}],\qquad\Sigma=S^{1}_{\phi}\times\Omega,

contained in a smooth coordinate patch with

\rho^{2}\geq\rho_{-}^{2}>0,\qquad\Delta_{r}\geq d_{r,-}>0,\qquad\Delta_{x}\geq d_{x,-}>0

on
\overline{\Omega}
. Horizons, true axes, and the set
\rho^{2}=0
are not part of this interior slab theorem unless a separate regular form realization is built.

H3.

Positive Killing energy.

The time coefficient

A:=-\rho^{2}g^{tt}

satisfies
A\geq a_{-}>0
. In the full
\phi
-dependent theorem the azimuthal coefficient
\Phi:=\rho^{2}g^{\phi\phi}
also satisfies
\Phi\geq\phi_{-}>0
. In the axisymmetric refinement
\partial_{\phi}u=0
, the
\Phi
-term is absent.

H4.

Closed reflecting form realization.

The full domain
V_{\rm full}\subset H^{1}(\Sigma)
is a closed, conjugation-invariant,
\phi
-periodic form domain containing the constant function
1
. The spatial form is

q[u,\eta]=\int_{\Sigma}\left(\Phi u_{\phi}\overline{\eta_{\phi}}+\Delta_{r}u_{r}\overline{\eta_{r}}+\Delta_{x}u_{x}\overline{\eta_{x}}\right).

(B.2)

Reflecting boundary conditions mean this form realization; homogeneous Dirichlet is a different coercive problem and is not the threshold theorem studied here.

H5.

Energy-space convention.

Hilbert spaces are complex unless a real subspace is explicitly specified, sesquilinear forms are linear in the first argument, and the energy space is
V_{\rm full}\times L^{2}(\Sigma)
. The mixed
B\partial_{t\phi}
term is used only through the skew-Hermitian identity proved in Section
10
.

H6.

Axisymmetric Friedrichs realization.

For Theorem
1.6
, the axisymmetric form domain
V_{\rm ax}\subset H^{1}(\Omega)
is the reflecting version of
H4
, and the spatial operator is the Friedrichs operator associated with

q_{0}[u]=\int_{\Omega}(\Delta_{r}|u_{r}|^{2}+\Delta_{x}|u_{x}|^{2})\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x

on
L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)
. Compact resolvent and the zero-mode classification are proved later.

H7.

Separated endpoint convention.

When the separated ODE discussion reaches a radial or angular endpoint, the admissible branch is the one for which the separated mode

u=e^{-\mathrm{i}\sigma t+\mathrm{i}m\phi}R(r)S(x)

extends smoothly in a regular endpoint chart; at a simple endpoint this is the regular Frobenius branch. Appendix
E
proves the corresponding Wronskian cancellation. The bounded-slab energy theorem itself is formulated through closed form domains and does not require a pointwise endpoint trace.

H8.

Standard tools.

The paper uses standard functional analysis without reproof: Riesz representation, closed-form representation, Banach-Alaoglu, Rellich compactness on bounded Lipschitz domains, the Lions-Magenes weak-continuity lemma, Sobolev trace/density facts, the Banach-valued Riemann-Lebesgue lemma, elementary ODE theory, and the compact self-adjoint spectral theorem.

H9.

Exterior boundary.

Horizons, ergoregions, noncompact ends, and radiation conditions are not bounded-slab hypotheses. They enter only through the exterior package
BH1
-
BH9
and the Kerr-Newman-specific regimes below.

H10.

Scope.

The closed equation is the neutral conformal scalar equation
h_{\mu\nu}=2ug_{\mu\nu}
,
\Box_{g}u=0
for
k=0
. No main theorem claims nonlinear stability, full tensorial gravitational stability, charged-scalar stability, or non-axisymmetric Kerr-Newman exterior stability.

Assumption B.3

(Axisymmetric black-hole exterior package)
.

Fix an integer
s_{0}\geq 0
. The axisymmetric exterior problem is said to satisfy the verified black-hole package if the following conditions hold for every integer
s\geq s_{0}
.

BH1.

Exterior geometry and nondegenerate horizons.

The spacetime is a stationary axisymmetric exterior region of a
k=0
member of the Carter-type family. The exterior hypersurface intersects each future event horizon in a regular way. Each horizon endpoint is a simple root of
\Delta_{r}
with positive surface gravity, and in a horizon-regular coordinate system the coefficients of
\Box_{g}
extend smoothly up to the future horizon. The theorem is restricted to the axisymmetric subspace
\partial_{\phi}u=0
.

BH2.

Outer/asymptotic boundary condition.

Every noncompact, conformal, or finite radial end is equipped with one fixed admissible boundary condition for both the evolution and the stationary resolvent. In an asymptotically flat or de Sitter end this means the outgoing/radiation condition. In a reflecting anti-de Sitter type, finite conformal, or finite wall end this means a closed boundary form, such as the Neumann wall used below. The condition is energy compatible: it creates no incoming forward flux and is invariant under time translation and axisymmetry.

BH3.

Exterior Hilbert scale, generator, and density.

There are Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}
for Cauchy data
U=(u,u_{t})
, a closed generator
\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}
with domain
\operatorname{Dom}(\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh})\subset\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}
, and a strongly continuous future evolution
e^{t\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}}
. Smooth axisymmetric data satisfying the boundary condition and regular at each future horizon are dense in the graph norm appropriate to
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}
. The equation
\Box_{g}u=F
is equivalent to the first-order system

\partial_{t}U=\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}U+\mathcal{F},

(B.3)

with the usual source vector
\mathcal{F}=(0,F)
in the corresponding dual local-energy space.

BH4.

Finite-time red-shift and local-energy estimate with compact error.

There are local-energy norms
LE^{s}
and dual norms
LE^{s,*}
, including nondegenerate red-shift control near each horizon, and there is a compactly supported cutoff
\chi_{\rm c}
in the interaction region, such that every smooth solution of
\Box_{g}u=F
on
[0,T]
satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|U(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}}^{2}+\|U\|_{LE^{s}([0,T])}^{2}

(B.4)

\displaystyle\leq C_{s}\Bigl(\|U(0)\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}}^{2}+\|F\|_{LE^{s,*}([0,T])}^{2}

\displaystyle+\|\chi_{\rm c}U\|_{\begin{subarray}{c}L^{2}([0,T];\\
H^{s}_{\rm loc}\times H^{s-1}_{\rm loc})\end{subarray}}^{2}\Bigr).

The constant
C_{s}
is independent of
T
. This is the finite-time red-shift/Morawetz input used in the proof. It is not the final dispersive estimate because the compact local term remains. That compact term is removed below by the projected limiting-absorption/smoothing input in
BH7
.

BH5.

Finite-frequency mode stability and resolvent estimate away from zero.

Let
P(\sigma)
be the outgoing stationary operator obtained by substituting
D_{t}=\sigma
in
\Box_{g}
. If
\operatorname{Im}\sigma>0
, then there is no nonzero outgoing solution of
P(\sigma)v=0
satisfying the horizon and asymptotic or wall boundary conditions. If
I\Subset\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
is compact and
\chi\prec\chi_{1}
are spatial cutoffs, then the limiting resolvent exists and obeys

\sup_{\sigma\in I,\ 0<\epsilon\leq 1}\|\chi P(\sigma+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)^{-1}\chi f\|_{H^{s+1}_{\rm loc}}\leq C_{I,s}\|f\|_{H^{s}_{\rm comp}}.

(B.5)

Equivalently, there are no real outgoing resonances on
I
, and the finite-frequency resolvent construction has no hidden compact obstruction at those frequencies.

BH6.

High-frequency propagation and trapping control.

For
|\sigma|\geq\sigma_{0}
the outgoing resolvent satisfies the high-frequency propagation estimate appropriate to the exterior geometry. In a nontrapping exterior this is a nontrapping semiclassical estimate. If the trapped set is present, it must be controlled by an explicitly stated trapping theorem. In the notation of the Hilbert scale this means that, after choosing
s_{0}
large enough to absorb any loss, one has

\|\chi P(\sigma+\mathrm{i}0)^{-1}\chi f\|_{H^{s+1}_{\rm loc}}\leq C_{s}\langle\sigma\rangle^{N_{s}}\|f\|_{H^{s}_{\rm comp}},\qquad|\sigma|\geq\sigma_{0},

(B.6)

with the polynomial power
N_{s}
compatible with the limiting-absorption and local-energy estimates below.

BH7.

Projected limiting absorption, compact smoothing, and dispersive representation.

The real-axis boundary values from
BH5
-
BH6
are compatible with the evolution, and the zero-frequency singular part from
BH8
has been removed. More precisely, for the homogeneous projected evolution

U_{\perp}(t):=e^{t\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}}(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}})U_{0},

(B.7)

the compact interaction-region smoothing estimate

\|\chi_{\rm c}U_{\perp}\|_{L^{2}([0,\infty);H^{s}_{\rm loc}\times H^{s-1}_{\rm loc})}\leq C_{s}\|(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}})U_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}}

(B.8)

holds, where
\chi_{\rm c}
is the compact cutoff in
BH4
. In addition, for every compact
K
away from any imposed outer boundary there is an operator-valued density
B_{K}(\sigma)
such that

\chi_{K}U_{\perp}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\mathrm{i}t\sigma}B_{K}(\sigma)(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}})U_{0}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma

(B.9)

in
H^{1}(K)\times L^{2}(K)
, and

\|B_{K}(\cdot)(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}})U_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R};H^{1}(K)\times L^{2}(K))}\leq C_{K,s}\|(I-\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}})U_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh}}.

(B.10)

Thus
BH7
supplies exactly the two noncompact consequences needed below: a smoothing bound that removes the compact error in
BH4
, and a local absolutely continuous spectral representation that gives local decay.

BH8.

Zero-frequency threshold control.

In a punctured neighborhood of
\sigma=0
, the cutoff outgoing resolvent on the chosen exterior energy realization has a finite-rank Laurent expansion, possibly with no singular coefficient after the radiation/decay condition has removed the constant threshold:

\displaystyle\chi P(\sigma)^{-1}\chi

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\nu_{0}}\sigma^{-j}A_{j}+R_{0}(\sigma),\qquad\nu_{0}\geq 0,

(B.11)

\displaystyle R_{0}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\text{ bounded between the local spaces used above},

with each
A_{j}
finite rank when
\nu_{0}>0
. The singular coefficients, or in the regular decaying case the explicit splitting of the zero threshold space, and the corresponding adjoint threshold functionals determine a bounded finite-rank data-space projection

\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}U=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{d}\ell_{\alpha}(U)V_{\alpha},\qquad V_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh},\quad\ell_{\alpha}\in(\mathcal{E}^{s}_{\rm bh})^{\prime},

(B.12)

satisfying

\displaystyle\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}},

(B.13)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ran}\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{0,{\rm bh}}:=\bigcup_{N\geq 1}\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}^{N},

\displaystyle e^{t\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}}\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{0,{\rm bh}}e^{t\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}}.

The restriction of
\mathcal{G}_{\rm bh}
to
\mathcal{T}_{0,{\rm bh}}
is nilpotent of finite length. In dissipative future-horizon realizations this nilpotent block can be semisimple, as it is in the Kerr-Newman wall verification below; in the decaying full asymptotically flat Kerr-Newman realization the threshold space is trivial.

BH9.

Compatibility of regularity, cutoffs, and domains.

The cutoffs used near horizons, in the compact interaction region, and near asymptotic or wall ends preserve the axisymmetric domain and the boundary condition modulo lower-order terms controlled by (
B.4
)-(
B.10
). Commutators with these cutoffs are bounded between the displayed spaces, and the derivative index
s\geq s_{0}
is chosen so that every loss in
BH6
is absorbed by the Hilbert scale.

Assumption B.4

(External subextremal Kerr, Reissner-Nordström, and Schwarzschild scalar packages)
.

The main Kerr and Reissner-Nordström theorems and the appendix Schwarzschild theorem use the following standard scalar-wave packages, in the same package sense as Assumption
B.3
.

EKR1.

Subextremal Kerr scalar package.

On every compact genuine subextremal Kerr parameter set with
0<|a|<M
, the neutral scalar wave equation on the domain of outer communications, restricted here to the axisymmetric sector, has a future-horizon regular and outgoing decaying radiation realization, a nondegenerate red-shift estimate, uniform energy boundedness, integrated local energy decay with the standard trapping loss, quantitative mode stability, high-frequency cutoff resolvent bounds, a limiting absorption principle, compact Kato smoothing, and an
L^{1}_{\sigma}
local spectral-density representation after the zero-frequency threshold is removed. This is the package form of the scalar Kerr theory of Dafermos-Rodnianski and Dafermos-Rodnianski-Shlapentokh-Rothman, together with mode stability in the sense of Whiting and Shlapentokh-Rothman; see
[
12
,
13
,
6
,
14
]
.

EKR2.

Subextremal Reissner-Nordström scalar package.

On every compact genuinely charged subextremal Reissner-Nordström parameter set with
0<|Q|<M
, the neutral scalar wave equation has the analogous nondegenerate red-shift, uniform boundedness, integrated local energy, limiting-absorption, smoothing, spectral-density, and no-mode package. Because the metric is spherically symmetric, the package is obtained by spherical-harmonic decomposition and summation and is not restricted to axisymmetry. The constants are uniform on compact subextremal parameter sets and may degenerate at extremality. This package is the exterior scalar Reissner-Nordström theory; the cited Franzen result is an interior boundedness input and reference point, while the exterior red-shift/Morawetz mechanism is the standard Dafermos-Rodnianski black-hole scalar theory specialized to Reissner-Nordström; see
[
10
,
11
,
15
]
.

EKR3.

Schwarzschild endpoint.

The same package is available at
a=Q=0
, with the photon sphere trapping loss at
r=3M
, nondegenerate red-shift at
r=2M
, and the standard asymptotically flat outgoing radiation condition. In the present manuscript this endpoint is recorded only in Appendix
A
, because the revised main theorem list reserves the main body for genuine Kerr and genuine Reissner-Nordström.

EKR4.

Low-frequency compatibility.

The scalar packages above are used together with the direct zero-frequency classifications proved in this manuscript. In the decaying radiation energy spaces, the nonzero constant solution is not an admissible data vector, and the direct ODE arguments in Section
19.2
and Appendix
A
exclude all regular decaying zero states and generalized zero companions. Thus the data-space threshold projections in these realizations are trivial.

Assumption B.5

(External full subextremal Kerr-Newman scalar scattering package)
.

Fix a compact subextremal parameter set

\mathscr{K}_{\delta,M_{0},M_{1}}=\{(M,a,Q):M_{0}\leq M\leq M_{1},\ a^{2}+Q^{2}\leq(1-\delta)M^{2}\},\qquad 0<\delta<1.

The full asymptotically flat Kerr-Newman application in Theorem
A.2
assumes the following theorem-level scalar-wave package for the neutral scalar equation on the domain of outer communications, uniformly for
(M,a,Q)\in\mathscr{K}_{\delta,M_{0},M_{1}}
. There is an integer
s_{\rm KN}
and, for each
s\geq s_{\rm KN}
, constants depending only on the compact parameter set, the cutoffs, and
s
, such that the following hold.

EKN1.

Horizon-regular and outgoing realization.

The future event horizon is treated in ingoing Kerr-Newman coordinates, the Cauchy hypersurface crosses the horizon regularly, and the asymptotically flat end is equipped with the outgoing finite-energy radiation condition. The scalar evolution is a strongly continuous future semigroup on the red-shift/radiation Hilbert scale
\mathcal{E}^{s}_{{\rm KN},{\rm af}}
, and smooth compactly supported axisymmetric data are dense in the decaying radiation energy.

EKN2.

Nondegenerate red-shift and uniform energy boundedness.

There is a future timelike red-shift multiplier
N
, uniformly timelike on the compact subextremal set, such that homogeneous solutions obey

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}E_{N}^{s}[u](t)\leq C_{s}E_{N}^{s}[u](0)

(B.14)

for all
T\geq 0
. The constants may degenerate only when the compact subextremality assumption is lost.

EKN3.

Integrated local energy decay with trapped-set loss.

For solutions of
\Box_{g_{M,a,Q}}u=F
, one has

\|u\|_{LE^{s}_{{\rm KN},{\rm af}}([0,T])}^{2}\leq C_{s}\Bigl(E_{N}^{s+q_{\rm trap}}[u](0)+\|F\|_{LE^{s+q_{\rm trap},*}([0,T])}^{2}\Bigr),

(B.15)

where
q_{\rm trap}
is the finite loss caused by the Kerr-Newman trapped set. The norm is nondegenerate at the horizon, has the standard Morawetz degeneration at trapping, and has the standard asymptotically flat weights at infinity.

EKN4.

Quantitative nonzero real-frequency mode stability.

Let
P_{\rm KN}(\sigma)
be the outgoing stationary scalar operator. For every compact interval
I\Subset\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, the outgoing homogeneous problem

P_{\rm KN}(\sigma)v=0,\qquad\sigma\in I,

has no nonzero solution satisfying future-horizon regularity and the outgoing radiation condition at infinity. Moreover, the corresponding quantitative mode-stability constants are strong enough to give Fredholm invertibility and uniform cutoff resolvent bounds on
I
.

EKN5.

High-frequency semiclassical resolvent through trapping.

For every compactly supported cutoff
\chi
and all
|\sigma|\geq\sigma_{0}
, the outgoing boundary value satisfies

\|\chi P_{\rm KN}(\sigma\pm i0)^{-1}\chi f\|_{H^{s+1}_{\rm loc}}\leq C_{s}\langle\sigma\rangle^{N_{s}}\|f\|_{H^{s+q_{\rm trap}}_{\rm comp}},

(B.16)

with a finite polynomial loss
N_{s}
and finite derivative loss compatible with (
B.15
). This is the global trapped-set replacement for the nontrapping wall commutator.

EKN6.

Limiting absorption on the real axis.

For
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, the limits

\chi P_{\rm KN}(\sigma\pm i0)^{-1}\chi:=\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\chi P_{\rm KN}(\sigma\pm i\epsilon)^{-1}\chi

(B.17)

exist between the weighted local Sobolev spaces used in (
B.16
), depend continuously on
\sigma
away from zero, and have no real-axis pole or embedded resonance.

EKN7.

Low-frequency compatibility with the decaying radiation space.

Near
\sigma=0
, the outgoing cutoff resolvent has a finite-rank expansion on the weighted spaces. After the decaying radiation condition at null infinity is imposed, the spatial constant is not a data-space vector. The only possible singular coefficients are therefore those associated with actual regular decaying zero-frequency states or Jordan companions; Lemma
19.18
proves in the present paper that these do not occur in the neutral axisymmetric sector. Consequently the projected full-exterior resolvent used in Theorem
A.2
is regular at zero.

EKN8.

Kato smoothing and compact-error removal.

For every compact interaction cutoff
\chi_{c}
, the homogeneous projected evolution satisfies

\|\chi_{c}e^{t\mathcal{G}_{{\rm KN},{\rm af}}}U_{0}\|_{L^{2}([0,\infty);H^{s}_{\rm loc}\times H^{s-1}_{\rm loc})}\leq C_{s}\|U_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s+q_{\rm trap}}_{{\rm KN},{\rm af}}}.

(B.18)

This is the estimate used to remove the compact interaction-region error in the red-shift/Morawetz inequality.

EKN9.

Local spectral-density representation and decay.

For every compact
K
in the domain of outer communications and every integer
N\geq 0
, after choosing
s\geq s(N)
there is an operator-valued density
B_{K}(\sigma)
such that

\chi_{K}U(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-it\sigma}B_{K}(\sigma)U_{0}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma

(B.19)

in
H^{1}(K)\times L^{2}(K)
, and

\sum_{j=0}^{N}\|\partial_{\sigma}^{j}B_{K}(\cdot)U_{0}\|_{L^{1}_{\sigma}(H^{1}(K)\times L^{2}(K))}\leq C_{K,N,s}\|U_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}^{s}_{{\rm KN},{\rm af}}}.

(B.20)

The case
N=0
is exactly the input needed for qualitative compact local decay; larger
N
gives optional polynomial local decay by integration by parts.

This external package is the only non-elementary global scattering input used for the full asymptotically flat Kerr-Newman theorem. It is the package form of the neutral scalar Kerr-Newman stability and mode-stability theory cited in
[
17
,
18
]
; the present paper does not claim to reprove the trapped-set microlocal analysis contained in that scalar theory.

Assumption B.6

(External extremal horizon-instability input)
.

For the extremal Kerr-Newman branch, the only external extremal input used beyond the horizon charge proved in Proposition
19.20
is the standard Aretakis higher-order horizon-instability mechanism for smooth axisymmetric scalar waves on degenerate horizons: for generic admissible data with the appropriate nonzero Aretakis charge, some transversal derivatives on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
fail to satisfy subextremal nondegenerate decay and higher transversal derivatives exhibit the corresponding Aretakis instability. This input is invoked only for the generic higher-order conclusion and not for the first conserved charge, the first nondecay obstruction, or the failure of the nondegenerate red-shift package, which are proved in Section
19
; see, for example,
[
19
,
20
]
.

Appendix C
Metric algebra, scalar-curvature variation, and Carter curvature

This appendix collects the essential algebra behind the geometric part of the paper. It combines the inverse-metric computation, the scalar-curvature variation formula, and the direct curvature computation for the Carter family.

Expanding the stationary block of (
2.5
) gives

\displaystyle g_{tt}

\displaystyle=\frac{a^{2}\Delta_{x}-\Delta_{r}}{\rho^{2}},

\displaystyle g_{t\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{a(1-x^{2})\Delta_{r}-a(r^{2}+a^{2})\Delta_{x}}{\rho^{2}},

\displaystyle g_{\phi\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}\Delta_{x}-a^{2}(1-x^{2})^{2}\Delta_{r}}{\rho^{2}}.

(C.1)

The determinant of this
(t,\phi)
-block is

g_{tt}g_{\phi\phi}-g_{t\phi}^{2}=-\Delta_{r}\Delta_{x}.

(C.2)

Inverting the block gives precisely (
2.14
)-(
2.16
); multiplying by the diagonal
r
- and
x
-entries yields

\det g=-\rho^{4},\qquad\sqrt{-g}=\rho^{2}.

(C.3)

Thus

\Box_{g}u=\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\partial_{\mu}(\rho^{2}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}u),

(C.4)

which is the structural reason why the wave equation separates.

Scalar-curvature variation

Let
g_{\varepsilon}=g+\varepsilon h+O(\varepsilon^{2})
. The inverse metric varies by

\delta g^{\mu\nu}=-h^{\mu\nu},

(C.5)

and the connection by

\displaystyle\delta\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}g^{\lambda\sigma}(\nabla_{\mu}h_{\sigma\nu}+\nabla_{\nu}h_{\sigma\mu}-\nabla_{\sigma}h_{\mu\nu}).

(C.6)

Contracting the linearized Ricci tensor gives

\displaystyle\delta R_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\lambda}\delta\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}-\nabla_{\nu}\delta\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\lambda}.

(C.7)

After the standard cancellations and the contribution
\delta g^{\mu\nu}R_{\mu\nu}
, one obtains

\displaystyle\delta R

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\mu}\nabla^{\nu}h_{\mu\nu}-\Box_{g}(\operatorname{tr}_{g}h)-R_{\mu\nu}h^{\mu\nu}.

(C.8)

For
h_{\mu\nu}=2ug_{\mu\nu}
in four dimensions,

\operatorname{tr}_{g}h=8u,\qquad\nabla^{\mu}\nabla^{\nu}h_{\mu\nu}=2\Box_{g}u,\qquad R_{\mu\nu}h^{\mu\nu}=2ku.

(C.9)

Hence

L_{g}[2ug]=-6\Box_{g}u-2ku,

(C.10)

and the conformal scalar-curvature equation is
(\Box_{g}+k/3)u=0
. When
k=0
, it is exactly the massless wave equation.

Direct curvature computation for the Carter family: numerator cancellations

The curvature computation is used only through identities that can be checked as polynomial numerator cancellations. With the inverse metric of Proposition
2.2
, all Christoffel symbols have denominators built from powers of
\rho^{2}
,
\Delta_{r}
, and
\Delta_{x}
. After forming the Ricci tensor, the apparent
\Delta_{r}
and
\Delta_{x}
denominators cancel in the scalar contraction, leaving

\mathcal{N}_{R}:=\rho^{2}R(g)+\Delta_{r}^{\prime\prime}(r)+\Delta_{x}^{\prime\prime}(x)=0.

(C.11)

For the traceless Ricci tensor, the corresponding check is that the following reduced numerators vanish identically after substituting (
2.3
)-(
2.4
) and
\delta=C_{3}-a^{2}C_{5}
:

\displaystyle\rho^{6}\left(S^{t}{}_{t}-\delta\frac{a^{2}x^{2}-r^{2}-2a^{2}}{\rho^{6}}\right)

\displaystyle=0,\qquad\rho^{6}\left(S^{t}{}_{\phi}-2a\delta\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})(1-x^{2})}{\rho^{6}}\right)=0,

(C.12)

\displaystyle\rho^{6}\left(S^{\phi}{}_{t}+2a\delta\frac{1}{\rho^{6}}\right)

\displaystyle=0,\qquad\rho^{6}\left(S^{\phi}{}_{\phi}+S^{t}{}_{t}\right)=0,

(C.13)

\displaystyle\rho^{4}\left(S^{r}{}_{r}+\delta\frac{1}{\rho^{4}}\right)

\displaystyle=0,\qquad\rho^{4}\left(S^{x}{}_{x}-\delta\frac{1}{\rho^{4}}\right)=0.

(C.14)

All other mixed components reduce to zero numerators. Equations (
C.11
)-(
C.14
) are the algebraic certificate behind Propositions
3.1
and
3.2
: a reader checking the computation need only verify these numerator identities in the polynomial ring generated by
r,x
and the parameters.

Reproducible polynomial verification protocol

For clarity, the numerator cancellations above can be verified by the following purely algebraic protocol. Work in the polynomial ring

\mathbb{Q}[a,C_{0},C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{4},C_{5},k,r,x]

(C.15)

localized at the nonzero factors
\rho^{2}
,
\Delta_{r}
, and
\Delta_{x}
. Then:

(1)

Insert the metric components from (
2.5
) and the inverse components from Proposition
2.2
.

(2)

Compute
\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}g^{\lambda\alpha}(\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha\nu}+\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha\mu}-\partial_{\alpha}g_{\mu\nu})
. Since the coefficients are independent of
t
and
\phi
, all
t
- and
\phi
-derivatives are set to zero before simplification.

(3)

Form
R_{\mu\nu}
from the coordinate formula and raise one index to obtain
R^{\mu}{}_{\nu}
.

(4)

Substitute
\delta=C_{3}-a^{2}C_{5}
and the quartics (
2.3
)-(
2.4
).

(5)

For each claimed identity, multiply by the denominator displayed in (
C.11
)-(
C.14
), expand, and collect the numerator as a polynomial in
r
and
x
. The certificate is valid exactly when every coefficient of this polynomial is zero.

No analytic approximation or asymptotic expansion is involved. The calculation is finite because the metric coefficients are rational functions of
r
and
x
, and the required denominators have been displayed explicitly. Thus the curvature input used in the stability theorem reduces to a finite polynomial identity check.

For a completely mechanical verification, the following is the exact algebraic certificate required from any symbolic implementation. Let
\mathfrak{R}
be the localized polynomial ring above. A verification file is accepted if and only if it constructs the six mixed Ricci-defect numerators

N^{t}{}_{t},\quad N^{t}{}_{\phi},\quad N^{\phi}{}_{t},\quad N^{\phi}{}_{\phi},\quad N^{r}{}_{r},\quad N^{x}{}_{x}

(C.16)

obtained by multiplying the six component differences in (
C.12
)-(
C.14
) by their displayed powers of
\rho
, expands them as

N^{\mu}{}_{\nu}=\sum_{j,\ell}c^{\mu}{}_{\nu;j\ell}(a,C_{0},\ldots,C_{5},k)r^{j}x^{\ell},

(C.17)

and checks

c^{\mu}{}_{\nu;j\ell}=0\quad\hbox{for every}\quad(\mu,\nu),j,\ell.

(C.18)

The scalar-curvature certificate is the analogous coefficient check for

N_{R}=\rho^{2}R(g)+\Delta_{r}^{\prime\prime}+\Delta_{x}^{\prime\prime}.

(C.19)

This is the strongest algebraic statement used by the analysis: no estimate, sign argument, or threshold computation depends on a curvature identity beyond these finite numerator cancellations.

Detailed computation for Proposition
3.1
.

Use the inverse coefficients from Proposition
2.2
in the coordinate formula

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\alpha}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}-\partial_{\nu}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\alpha}+\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\beta}_{\alpha\beta}-\Gamma^{\beta}_{\mu\alpha}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\nu\beta}.

(C.20)

All coefficients depend only on
r
and
x
. Substitution and simplification over the common denominator
\rho^{6}
cancel all third derivatives and all mixed derivative terms. The remaining scalar contraction is

R(g)=-\frac{\Delta_{r}^{\prime\prime}(r)+\Delta_{x}^{\prime\prime}(x)}{\rho^{2}}.

(C.21)

For the quartics (
2.3
)-(
2.4
),

\Delta_{r}^{\prime\prime}+\Delta_{x}^{\prime\prime}=-k(r^{2}+a^{2}x^{2})=-k\rho^{2},

(C.22)

so
R(g)=k
. This proves Proposition
3.1
.
∎

Detailed computation for Proposition
3.2
.

Set

S^{\mu}{}_{\nu}=R^{\mu}{}_{\nu}-\frac{k}{4}\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}.

(C.23)

The same coordinate computation gives the mixed components

\displaystyle S^{t}{}_{t}

\displaystyle=\delta\,\frac{a^{2}x^{2}-r^{2}-2a^{2}}{\rho^{6}},\qquad S^{t}{}_{\phi}=2a\delta\,\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})(1-x^{2})}{\rho^{6}},

\displaystyle S^{\phi}{}_{t}

\displaystyle=-2a\delta\,\frac{1}{\rho^{6}},\qquad S^{\phi}{}_{\phi}=-S^{t}{}_{t},

\displaystyle S^{r}{}_{r}

\displaystyle=-\delta\,\frac{1}{\rho^{4}},\qquad S^{x}{}_{x}=\delta\,\frac{1}{\rho^{4}}.

(C.24)

with all other mixed components zero. Every component is proportional to
\delta=C_{3}-a^{2}C_{5}
. Lowering an index with the smooth metric gives

S_{\mu\nu}=\delta\,\mathcal{S}_{\mu\nu},

(C.25)

where
\mathcal{S}
is smooth and trace-free on every regular patch. Thus
S_{\mu\nu}=0
if and only if
\delta=0
. This proves Proposition
3.2
.
∎

Appendix D
Bounded-slab well-posedness and compact spectral input

This appendix supplies the functional analysis used in the bounded-slab theorems. The first part proves the full
\phi
-dependent evolution by Galerkin approximation. The second part gives the compact-resolvent spectral theorem used in the axisymmetric refinement.

Detailed proof of Theorem
10.9
.

Let
V_{\mathrm{full}}\subset H^{1}(\Sigma)
be the closed reflecting form domain, with
1\in V_{\mathrm{full}}
. Equip it with

\displaystyle(u,v)_{V_{\mathrm{full}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\bigl(Auv+\Phi u_{\phi}v_{\phi}+\Delta_{r}u_{r}v_{r}+\Delta_{x}u_{x}v_{x}\bigr)\mathrm{d}\phi\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x.

(D.1)

By strict stationarity this inner product is equivalent to the
H^{1}
-inner product. Choose a countable Hilbert basis
\{w_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
of
V_{\mathrm{full}}
, with
w_{1}
proportional to the constant function. No smooth core is required; all integrations by parts are understood in the closed-form sense. For
V_{N}=\mathrm{span}\{w_{1},\dots,w_{N}\}
, seek

u_{N}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}d_{j}^{(N)}(t)w_{j}

(D.2)

satisfying, for every
\varphi\in V_{N}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}\left(Au_{N,tt}\varphi-2Bu_{N,t\phi}\varphi+\Phi u_{N,\phi}\varphi_{\phi}+\Delta_{r}u_{N,r}\varphi_{r}+\Delta_{x}u_{N,x}\varphi_{x}\right)

\displaystyle=0.

(D.3)

In matrix form,

M_{N}\ddot{d}_{N}+C_{N}\dot{d}_{N}+K_{N}d_{N}=0,

(D.4)

where
M_{N}
is Hermitian positive definite,
K_{N}
is Hermitian nonnegative, and

(C_{N})_{ij}=\mathfrak{c}[w_{j},w_{i}]

(D.5)

is skew-Hermitian by (
10.23
). Therefore the finite-dimensional problem has a global solution and the discrete energy

E_{N}(t)=\frac{1}{2}(\dot{d}_{N}^{*}M_{N}\dot{d}_{N}+d_{N}^{*}K_{N}d_{N})

(D.6)

is conserved.

Testing (
D.3
) with the constant basis vector gives

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\int_{\Sigma}Au_{N}(t)=0.

(D.7)

Thus the mean part is affine and the mean-zero part is controlled by the weighted Poincaré inequality. On every compact time interval
[-T,T]
,

\displaystyle\sup_{|t|\leq T}\bigl(\|u_{N}(t)\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma)}+\|u_{N,t}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C_{T}\bigl(\|u_{0,N}\|_{H^{1}}+\|u_{1,N}\|_{L^{2}}\bigr).

(D.8)

The sequence
u_{N}
is equi-Lipschitz in
L^{2}(\Sigma)
. The equation also bounds
u_{N,tt}
in
L^{\infty}([-T,T];V_{\mathrm{full}}^{\prime})
. Rellich compactness and the Arzela-Ascoli theorem give, after passing to a subsequence,

\displaystyle u_{N}\to u\quad\hbox{in }C([-T,T];L^{2}(\Sigma))

\displaystyle u_{N,t}\to u_{t}\quad\hbox{in }C([-T,T];V_{\mathrm{full}}^{\prime}).

(D.9)

Passing to the limit in the weak formulation gives a weak solution of (
10.3
). Lower semicontinuity gives

E_{\mathrm{full}}[u](t)\leq E_{\mathrm{full}}[u](0).

(D.10)

Applying the same construction backward from an arbitrary time
t_{0}
gives the reverse inequality, hence exact conservation of
E_{\mathrm{full}}
.

If two weak solutions have the same data, their difference has zero initial energy and zero initial average. Conservation and the affine average law force the difference to have zero time derivative, zero spatial derivatives, and zero constant mode; hence it vanishes. The norm

\|(u,v)\|_{*}^{2}=2E_{\mathrm{full}}[u,v]+|\Pi_{0}u|^{2}+|\Pi_{0}v|^{2}

(D.11)

is equivalent to
H^{1}\times L^{2}
, so conservation of energy, the affine average law, and uniqueness give strong continuity and the group property. This proves Theorem
10.9
.
∎

Compact resolvent and the axisymmetric spectral theorem

Lemma D.1

(Equivalence of weighted norms)
.

On a bounded slab where
A,\Delta_{r},\Delta_{x}
are bounded above and below by positive constants, the norm

\|u\|_{A,1}^{2}=\int_{\Omega}(A|u|^{2}+\Delta_{r}|u_{r}|^{2}+\Delta_{x}|u_{x}|^{2})\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}x

(D.12)

is equivalent to the standard
H^{1}(\Omega)
-norm.

Proof.

The two integrands bound each other pointwise by positive constants, and integration gives the claim.
∎

Lemma D.2

(Compact positive resolvent)
.

Let
V_{\mathrm{ax}}\subset H^{1}(\Omega)
be the reflecting form domain and set

a_{1}[u,v]=\langle u,v\rangle_{A}+q_{0}[u,v].

(D.13)

For each
f\in L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)
, there is a unique
Tf\in V_{\mathrm{ax}}
satisfying

a_{1}[Tf,v]=\langle f,v\rangle_{A}\qquad\text{for all }v\in V_{\mathrm{ax}}.

(D.14)

The operator
T:L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)\to L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)
is compact, self-adjoint, and positive.

Proof.

The form
a_{1}
is an inner product equivalent to
H^{1}
by Lemma
D.1
. Riesz representation gives existence and uniqueness of
Tf
. The map
T:L_{A}^{2}\to V_{\mathrm{ax}}
is bounded, and the embedding
V_{\mathrm{ax}}\hookrightarrow L_{A}^{2}
is compact by Rellich, so
T
is compact on
L_{A}^{2}
. Symmetry follows from

\langle Tf,g\rangle_{A}=a_{1}[Tf,Tg]=\langle f,Tg\rangle_{A},

(D.15)

and positivity from
\langle Tf,f\rangle_{A}=a_{1}[Tf,Tf]\geq 0
.
∎

Lemma D.3

(Spectral resolution of a compact positive operator)
.

If
T
is compact, self-adjoint, and positive on a Hilbert space
H
, then
H
has an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors of
T
, and the nonzero eigenvalues form a finite or countable sequence decreasing to zero.

Proof.

If
T=0
, the statement is immediate. Otherwise the maximum of the Rayleigh quotient is attained by compactness: take a maximizing sequence
u_{n}
, use compactness of
Tu_{n}
, and the inequality
\|Tu_{n}\|^{2}\leq\|T\|\langle Tu_{n},u_{n}\rangle
to show that a subsequence converges to an eigenvector for the top eigenvalue. Self-adjointness makes its orthogonal complement invariant. Iterating gives an orthonormal eigenfamily. Compactness rules out positive eigenvalues accumulating away from zero, and the orthogonal complement of the obtained eigenvectors must lie in
\operatorname{Ker}T
. Adding an orthonormal basis of
\operatorname{Ker}T
completes the basis.
∎

Theorem D.4

(Direct spectral decomposition of
L_{0}
)
.

There exists an
L_{A}^{2}(\Omega)
-orthonormal basis
\{\psi_{j}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}\subset V_{\mathrm{ax}}
and a sequence

0=\lambda_{0}\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\cdots\nearrow+\infty

(D.16)

such that

q_{0}[\psi_{j},v]=\lambda_{j}\langle\psi_{j},v\rangle_{A}\qquad\text{for all }v\in V_{\mathrm{ax}}.

(D.17)

If
\Omega
is connected and constants are admitted by the boundary conditions, then
\lambda_{0}=0
is simple and
\psi_{0}
is constant.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
D.2
and Lemma
D.3
to
T
. If
T\psi=\mu\psi
with
\mu>0
, then

q_{0}[\psi,v]=(\mu^{-1}-1)\langle\psi,v\rangle_{A}.

(D.18)

Set
\lambda=\mu^{-1}-1
. Since
\mu\to 0
,
\lambda\to+\infty
. The kernel statement follows from Proposition
12.4
.
∎

Expanded proof of Theorem
12.6
.

Expand the data in the eigenbasis:

u_{0}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}a_{j}\psi_{j},\qquad u_{1}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}b_{j}\psi_{j}.

(D.19)

The conditions
u_{0}\in H^{1}
and
u_{1}\in L^{2}
are equivalent to

\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(1+\lambda_{j})|a_{j}|^{2}<\infty,\qquad\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}|b_{j}|^{2}<\infty.

(D.20)

Define

\displaystyle u(t)

\displaystyle=a_{0}\psi_{0}+b_{0}t\psi_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(a_{j}\cos(\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}t)+b_{j}\frac{\sin(\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}t)}{\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}\right)\psi_{j}.

(D.21)

The series converges in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{1}(\Omega))
, and its derivative converges in
C(\mathbb{R};L^{2}(\Omega))
, by the two summability conditions. Each coefficient solves
\ddot{c}_{j}+\lambda_{j}c_{j}=0
, with the
j=0
coefficient affine. Hence the series solves (
12.2
). Conservation of energy follows mode by mode, and uniqueness follows by applying the conserved energy to the difference of two solutions.
∎

Appendix E
Endpoint and separated ODE calculus

This appendix keeps only the separated ODE facts that are used by the main text: the algebra of separation, Wronskian identities, simple endpoint behavior, and the bounded-interval Fredholm picture.

Insert

u=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\Omega t+\mathrm{i}m\phi}R(r)S(x)

(E.1)

into (
5.14
). The stationary part becomes

\displaystyle-\Omega^{2}\rho^{2}g^{tt}+2\Omega m\rho^{2}g^{t\phi}-m^{2}\rho^{2}g^{\phi\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{((r^{2}+a^{2})\Omega-am)^{2}}{\Delta_{r}}-\frac{(a(1-x^{2})\Omega-m)^{2}}{\Delta_{x}}.

(E.2)

The mass term separates as

\frac{k}{3}\rho^{2}=\frac{k}{3}r^{2}+\frac{k}{3}a^{2}x^{2}.

(E.3)

Thus the equation splits into (
7.5
) and (
7.6
) with one separation constant.

If
R_{1},R_{2}
solve the same radial equation, subtracting the two equations after cross multiplication gives

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\left(\Delta_{r}(R_{1}^{\prime}R_{2}-R_{1}R_{2}^{\prime})\right)=0.

(E.4)

The angular Wronskian identity is the same with
r
replaced by
x
. These identities are the ODE version of vanishing flux. The signs in the radial and angular equations are kept in this form so that endpoint regularity and reflecting boundary conditions can be checked by the same boundary form. On a bounded interval the Wronskian is the Green identity boundary term; at a simple horizon root it distinguishes the horizon-regular branch from the singular outgoing branch.

Regular-singular endpoints

Lemma E.1

(Radial simple-zero endpoint)
.

Suppose
r_{h}
is a simple zero of
\Delta_{r}
:

\Delta_{r}(r)=\kappa_{h}(r-r_{h})+O((r-r_{h})^{2}),\qquad\kappa_{h}\neq 0.

(E.5)

Then (
7.5
) has a regular singular point at
r_{h}
, with indicial roots

s_{\pm}=\pm\mathrm{i}\sigma_{h},\qquad\sigma_{h}=\frac{(r_{h}^{2}+a^{2})\Omega-am}{\kappa_{h}}.

(E.6)

Proof.

Put
y=r-r_{h}
. The leading part of the radial equation is

\kappa_{h}(yR^{\prime})^{\prime}+\frac{((r_{h}^{2}+a^{2})\Omega-am)^{2}}{\kappa_{h}y}R=0.

(E.7)

With
R=y^{s}
, the indicial equation is
s^{2}+\sigma_{h}^{2}=0
. Frobenius theory then gives local branches
y^{s_{\pm}}(1+O(y))
.
∎

Lemma E.2

(Angular simple endpoint)
.

Suppose
x_{\star}
is an endpoint at which

\Delta_{x}(x)=\kappa_{\star}(x-x_{\star})+O((x-x_{\star})^{2}),\qquad\kappa_{\star}\neq 0.

(E.8)

Set

\nu_{\star}=a(1-x_{\star}^{2})\Omega-m.

(E.9)

Then (
7.6
) has indicial roots

s_{\pm}=\pm\frac{\nu_{\star}}{\kappa_{\star}}.

(E.10)

If two functions lie on the regular branch with
\Re s_{+}>0
, then their angular Wronskian satisfies

\Delta_{x}(\overline{S}_{1}S_{2}^{\prime}-\overline{S}_{1}^{\prime}S_{2})\to 0\qquad\text{as }x\to x_{\star}.

(E.11)

Proof.

With
y=x-x_{\star}
, the leading angular equation is

\kappa_{\star}(yS^{\prime})^{\prime}-\frac{\nu_{\star}^{2}}{\kappa_{\star}y}S=0.

(E.12)

The indicial equation is
s^{2}-\nu_{\star}^{2}/\kappa_{\star}^{2}=0
. If
S_{j}=y^{s_{+}}\widetilde{S}_{j}
with bounded
\widetilde{S}_{j}
, then

\overline{S}_{1}S_{2}^{\prime}-\overline{S}_{1}^{\prime}S_{2}=O(y^{2\Re s_{+}-1}).

(E.13)

Multiplication by
\Delta_{x}=\kappa_{\star}y+O(y^{2})
gives the claimed vanishing.
∎

Proof of Proposition
8.2
.

The admissible endpoint branch is the branch selected by smoothness in the endpoint chart. Lemma
E.2
shows that the corresponding Wronskian boundary contribution vanishes. Hence the angular Green identity has no boundary term on the admissible domain, and the angular Sturm-Liouville realization is self-adjoint.
∎

Fredholm and Green operators on bounded intervals

On a bounded interval, the angular and radial separated operators are closed operators obtained from regular or regular-singular Sturm-Liouville forms with separated boundary conditions. When the endpoint Wronskians vanish, the realization has compact resolvent. Consequently the bounded-domain spectra are discrete, and for the axisymmetric operator

L_{0}\psi_{j}=\lambda_{j}\psi_{j},\qquad 0=\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\cdots,

(E.14)

the resolvent on the orthogonal complement of constants is

G_{\perp}f=\sum_{j\geq 1}\frac{\langle f,\psi_{j}\rangle_{A}}{\lambda_{j}}\psi_{j}.

(E.15)

For the wave resolvent,

\displaystyle(L_{0}-\sigma^{2})^{-1}f

\displaystyle=-\sigma^{-2}\Pi_{0}f+\sum_{j\geq 1}\frac{\langle f,\psi_{j}\rangle_{A}}{\lambda_{j}-\sigma^{2}}\psi_{j}.

(E.16)

Thus the zero-frequency singularity is exactly the rank-one pole associated with constants; in time, this pole is the affine contribution
c_{0}+c_{1}t
.
